### [book p.248 / PDF 269] — header "BASICS OF ECONOMY"

**Left column — box titled "ECONOMICS"**

• Economics is the study of how people, businesses, and governments use limited resources to produce, distribute, and consume goods and services. Economics is broadly divided into: *Macroeconomics and Microeconomics.*

| Aspects | Macroeconomics | Microeconomics |
|---|---|---|
| Definition | It studies the economy as a whole | It studies individual consumers, firms, and markets |
| Approach | Top down | Bottom up |
| Focus Area | It is focused on the action of government and countries that are taken to influence broader economics like aggregate demand, aggregate supply, prices, etc. | It is focused with the action of individual or businesses including concepts like demand, supply etc. |
| Scope | Large-scale, entire economy | Small-scale, specific sector |
| Father | J. M. Keynes (from England) wrote the "General Theory of Employment, Interest and money" | Adam Smith (Founding Father of Modern Economy) wrote the book "The wealth of Nations" |
| Examples | GDP, inflation rates, fiscal policies, national income, unemployment rates | Pricing of a product, consumer behavior, competition in a market |

*The economic system can be classified based on Ownership, Motive and Government Role:*
*1. Capitalist Economy   2. Socialist Economy   3. Mixed Economy*

| | Capitalist | Socialist | Mixed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ownership | Private ownership | Public or State ownership | Public + Private ownership |
| Economic motive | Profit | Social welfare | Both |
| Government role | No role | Complete involvement | Limited role |
| Income distribution | Unequal (High Inequality) | Equal (Theoretically) | Less unequal |
| Economic freedom | Complete | Lack of freedom | Limited freedom |
| Example | USA | USSR, North Korea | India |

**Right column**

**Socialist Economy (Centrally Planned Economy)**
Government controls and makes decision regarding production, exchange, and consumption of goods and services. Eg: USSR.

**Spillover Effect**
Macroeconomics considers "spillover effect", where an event in one part of the economy (or world) impacts others. E.g. The great US depression affecting others

**Key Historical Figures & Texts**

*Adam Smith*
- *Known as:* Father of Micro Economics / Father of Modern Economics
- *Key Work:* • The Wealth of Nations (1776)
- *Core Ideas:*
  - • *Invisible Hand:* Markets regulate themselves through the forces of demand and supply, leading to efficient outcomes.
  - • *Market Economy:* Individuals pursuing their self-interest ("Animal spirit"- by J.M. Keynes) unintentionally promote overall economic welfare.
  - • *Laissez-faire Policy:* Advocated minimal government interference in economic activities.

*Alfred Marshall*
- Also Known as: Father of Microeconomics
- *Key Work:* • Principles of Economics (1890)
- *Core Contributions:*
  - • Developed the modern theory of demand and supply.
  - • Introduced concepts like elasticity of demand, consumer surplus, and partial equilibrium analysis.

*John Maynard Keynes*
- *Known as:* Father of Macroeconomics
- *Key Work:* • The General Theory of Employment, Interest and Money (1936)
- *Core Ideas:*
  - •Unlike the strict Laissez- Faire approach, Keynes argued that government intervention is necessary to manage "Animal spirits" and revive the economy during downturns.
  - •Supported fiscal policy and public spending to boost demand and employment.

*Scarcity Definition of Economics (Given by Lionel Robbins)*
Economics is science that studies human behavior as relationship between limited resources and unlimited wants.

### [book p.249 / PDF 270] — header "BASICS OF ECONOMY"

**Left column**

**Key wordings**
- •"Most of the decisions are taken as a result of animal spirits". - *John Maynard keynes*
- •"Managing animal spirits requires active role of the government" - *Robert J. Shiller (Nobel Prize-winning economist)*
- •"We need to revive the animal spirit in the country's economy" - *Manmohan Singh*

*Classification of goods* (orange banner)
Goods are classified based on two criteria: Excludability and Rivalry.

**Definitions**
- • *Excludability:* Is it possible to prevent someone who hasn't paid from using the good?
  - • *Excludable:* You must pay to use it (e.g. buying a ticket).
  - • *Non-Excludable:* You cannot prevent anyone from using it (e.g. air, free YouTube videos).
- • *Rivalry:* Does one person's consumption reduce the availability for others?
  - • *Rivalrous:* If I consume it, you cannot (e.g. eating an apple, buying a limited edition car).
  - • *Non-Rivalrous:* My consumption does not affect yours (e.g. watching a sunset, breathing air).

**FOUR TYPES OF GOODS — Classification Based on Excludability and Rivalry** (2×2 graphic; columns RIVALROUS | NON-RIVALROUS; rows EXCLUDABLE (black side-bar) / NON EXCLUDABLE (arrow))

| | RIVALROUS | NON-RIVALROUS |
|---|---|---|
| EXCLUDABLE | **PRIVATE GOODS** — Excludable & Rivalrous. paid for, and consumption reduces availability for others. Examples: Food, clothing, cars, Parking spaces. | **CLUB GOODS** — Excludable & Non rivalrous. paid for, but can be shared simultaneously without reducing supply. Examples: Cinemas, Private Parks, Netflix subscription. |
| NON EXCLUDABLE | **COMMON POOL RESOURCES** — Non-Excludable & Rivalrous. Free to access , but finite supply (prone to depletion). Examples: Fish stocks in the ocean, Timber in a forests, coal. | **PUBLIC GOODS** — Non-Excludable & Non Rivalrous. Free to all ; one's use doesn't hurt another's. Examples: National Defense, Street Lights, Free-to-Air TV. |

- Yellow box — **Opportunity Cost :** The value of the next best alternative given up. Natural resources like sunlight often have zero opportunity cost for the consumer.
- Black box — **Free Rider Problem :** A challenge with Public Goods where people benefit without paying (e.g., using a street light without paying taxes).

**Right column — box titled "SECTORS OF ECONOMY"**

**There are three sectors of economy on the basis of nature of activities**
1. *Primary Sector*  2. *Secondary Sector*  3. *Tertiary Sector*

**Primary Sector (Red Collar Jobs)**
- •This sector is directly dependent on the environment.
- •In India Primary Sector is mostly seen in *agricultural activities.*
- *Eg. Agriculture, Fishing, Hunting, Forestry, Mining.*
- •During British colonial rule in India, about 85% of the population lived in villages, with nearly 70–75% engaged in agriculture either directly or indirectly for their livelihood.
- [boxed] *Agriculture Sector is considered "The backbone of Indian Economy"*

**Secondary Sector (Blue Collar Jobs)**
- •It is also called as *Manufacturing Sector.*
- •They add value to natural resources by transforming raw materials into valuable products. *E.g. Manufacturing, processing, building/construction, electricity etc.*
- [side box] *Blue-collar jobs involve physical work and use practical skills. These jobs are often in fields like construction, manufacturing, and maintenance.*
- •Secondary Sector is considered *backbone of a country, as it helps in industrialisation of a country.* Eg. China.
- •Secondary sector job is not yet developed in India properly because we have directly leaped to tertiary sector.

**Tertiary Sector (White Collar Jobs)**
- •It is also known as *Service Sector.*
- •It is a part of economy, where business produce services. *E.g. Teaching, Transport, Advertising, Retail, Hospitality and Healthcare.*
- •It is mostly *intangible* (something which cannot be touched) in nature.

**The Tertiary Sector is further subdivided into two specialized sector based on LEVEL OF KNOWLEDGE and EXPERTISE REQUIRED:**

*Quaternary Sector*
- •The Quaternary Sector focuses on knowledge-based activities and intellectual services. (continues on p.250)

### [book p.250 / PDF 271] — header "BASICS OF ECONOMY"

**Left column (continues)**
- •This sector is also known as the *"Knowledge Sector".* E.g. Senior, Business Executive, Teaching, Manager, Banking, Chartered Accountant, Lawyer, Scientists.

*Quinary Sector* (sub-heading as printed)
- •The Quinary Sector involves high-level, *knowledge-based activities that require expertise and advanced skills → All the decision making activities are involved here.*
- •The activities in this sector are also known as *Gold Collar Jobs.*
  *E.g. Media, Financial Services, Education, Research Scientists, Information Technology, Policy makers, Activity of Politicians.*

| % in GDP | 14.39% | 31.46% | 54.15% (Highest) |

*"LFPR: Labour Force Participation Rate.*
•*Percentage of women engaged in Primary Sector: 62.8%*

**Sectors: On the basis of employment**

| Organized sector | Unorganized sectors |
|---|---|
| The organized sectors cover those enterprises where the term of employment is regular. | The unorganized sectors is characterized by small and scattered units which are largely outside the control of government. |
| They are registered by government. They follow rules and regulations and various laws such as the factories act, minimum wages act, payment of gratuity, shops and establishments act. | Unorganized sector does not follow any rules and regulation pass by the government. |
| Workers in the organized sector are paid according to prescribed scale. They also get payment in time on regular basis. | Most of the jobs are low paid and often not regular. |
| They get annual increment and allowances such as provident fund, payment during holidays, paid leave, gratuity medical benefits, safe working conditions. | There is no provision of over time, paid leave, holidays, leave due to sickness etc. |
| They can form trade unions. | They cannot form trade unions. |

**Right column**

[boxed] *Initiative for unorganised sector:*
*The E-Shram Portal, 2021 is a flagship initiative designed to create a comprehensive National Database of Unorganised Workers (NDUW) seeded with Aadhaar.*

**Real Vs Financial Sector**
- •*Real Sector :* It involves the actual production of goods and services using raw materials, such as land and labours.
- •*Financial Sector:* Deals with Financial assets like mutual funds, stocks and banking services.

**Sectors: On the basis of ownership**
(Infographic "PUBLIC SECTOR V/S PRIVATE SECTOR — Who Runs the Economy?")

| | PUBLIC SECTOR ("FOR SOCIETY") | PRIVATE SECTOR ("FOR PROFIT") |
|---|---|---|
| Definition | Government owned Organizations and services. | Businesses Owned by Individuals or companies. |
| Examples | (logos: BHEL, SAIL, ONGC — as pictured) | (logos: Reliance, one more, Adani/Tata-type logo — as pictured; unreadable at this resolution) |
| Key Work | • Funded by taxes. • Focus on Public Welfare. • Non Profit Oriented | • Profit driven • Competitive • Market oriented • Profit over Welfare |
| Benefits | Equal Access Stability. | Innovation & Efficiency |
| Challenges | Public Goods | Job Insecurity |

**Normative Vs Positive Economics**

| Normative Economics | Positive Economics |
|---|---|
| It is based on opinion and value of the interpreter. | It is based on actual data, facts, and numbers. |
| Verification is not possible. | Results can be verified |
| No way to test feasibility and applicability in various field. | Safe and beyond doubt and can be proved scientifically. |
| It deals with idealistic situation. | It deals with actual and realistic situations. |
| E.g. The level of unemployment must be 5% maximum. | E.g. The current unemployment rate is 20% |

**Parallel Economy**
- •Parallel Economy refers to all the forms of activities through which black income is generated and those which are usually not accounted.
- •It renders punctuation by Government punctuation. (as printed — apparent typo, likely "sanction/punishment"; ⚠ meaning unclear)

## PART A — PARMAR SSC FATMAN (2nd Ed. 2026): Economics Ch.2 "MICROECONOMICS" (book pp.251–256, PDF pp.272–277)

Running header on every page: "MICROECONOMICS". Chapter opens straight into a box headed "MACROECONOMICS" (as printed — the chapter's own map lists Macroeconomics, Demand, Supply, Costs, Types of Market, Macroeconomic Concepts as its sub-parts). Ends with ■■■ at bottom of left column p.256. (Right column of p.256 is blank apart from faint show-through of the next chapter's page — not book text.)

### [book p.251 / PDF 272]

**Left column — box titled "MACROECONOMICS"**

The fundamental problem of economics arises from the conflict between limited resources and unlimited human wants.
- •*Scarcity:* Resources (money, land, labor, time) are finite.
- •*Choice:* Because resources are limited, an economy cannot produce everything. It must choose how to allocate resources effectively.

The *production possibility of the economy* refers to all the possible combinations of goods and services that an economy can produce using the available resources and current technology.

**Production Possibility Curve/Or Production Possibility Frontier (PPF):**
- •It is a graph that shows the different combinations of two goods or services that an economy can produce *when all resources are used efficiently.*

E.g. If a person has ₹ 6000, and they have two buying options: 6 pairs of shoes for ₹ 6000 and 6 pairs of jeans for ₹ 6000. Spending the entire amount on one product means buying 6 pairs of shoes with no money left for jeans, or buying 6 pairs of jeans with no money left for shoes.

[Graph box — PPF: y-axis "Product A: Shoes", x-axis "Product B: Jeans"; concave-to-origin curve with points A, B, C, X, Y marked; "Production Possibility Frontier (PPF) Shifts outward" arrow]
*Graph 1 characteristics:*
- • It shows the trade-off between two goods (e.g., Shoes and Jeans).
- • *Slope:* Downward sloping (Negative slope) because to produce more of one good, you must produce less of the other due to limited resources.

*Marginal Rate of Transformation (MRT):*
- • The slope of the PPC is determined by the MRT.
- • It represents the amount of one good that must be sacrificed to produce one extra unit of another good.

- • If they want more shoes, they must give up some jeans and vice versa. This also illustrates *inverse relationship* means that as we spend more *money on one product we have less money available for the other product due to limitation on budget.*

*At any point*
- •On the curve: *Full utilisation of resources*
- •Down/under the curve: *Under utilisation of resources*
- •Up/Over the curve: *Not achievable (due to limited resources).*

**Opportunity Cost**
- •Opportunity cost means the value of *what you give up when you choose one thing over another.*

E.g. If a person has ₹ 6000 and can buy either a pair of jeans or a pair of shoes, choosing the jeans means they miss out on the shoes. The shoes are the opportunity cost.

**Right column**

**Free Goods:** Goods like air and sunlight have no opportunity cost because they are abundant in nature and not scarce. No sacrifice is needed to obtain them.

**Utility**
Utility is the satisfaction or benefit derived from consuming a good or service.

**Types of Utility Measurement**
1. **Cardinal Utility:** Satisfaction is measured in numerical values (Quantitative). *Less realistic.*
2. **Ordinal Utility:** Satisfaction is ranked (Qualitative/ Psychological). *More realistic.*

**Indifference Curve**
- •A consumer's indifference curve is a graph that shows *different combinations of two goods that provide the consumer with the same level of satisfaction or happiness.*

[Graph 2: A consumer's indifference curve — y-axis CLOTHING (2,4,6,8,10,12), x-axis FOOD (1–6); points A (1,12), B (2,6), C, D on curve "IC", label "Convex at Origin"]
E.g. At point A, we have 1 unit of food and 12 units of clothing and point B, we have 2 units of food and 6 units of clothing. Both points make one equally happy, so they lie on the same indifference curve.

**Graph Properties**
- •*Shape:* *Convex to the origin (Rectangular Hyperbola).*
- •*Slope:* Determined by the *Marginal Rate of Substitution (MRS)* - the rate at which a consumer is willing to give up one good for another to maintain the same satisfaction.
  [formula box] MRS = Δ Sacrifice / Δ Gain
- •*No Intersection:* *Two ICs never intersect.*

**Marginal Utility**
The *additional satisfaction or benefit* that a consumer derives from buying an additional unit of a commodity/ service.
- •*Marginal Product (MP)* is the additional output produced by employing one more unit of a factor of production (usually labour).
- •*Marginal Cost (MC)* is the change in total cost from producing one additional unit.
- •*Marginal Revenue (MR)* is the additional income from selling one more unit.
[small graphs (a) "Marginal Product" vs Labour (MP curve, hump-shaped, declining) and (b) "Marginal Cost" vs Quantity (MC curve, U-shaped); labels "Labor MP", "MC", "Quantity"]

### [book p.252 / PDF 273]

**Left column**

**Average fixed cost curve and Variable fixed cost curve** (heading as printed)
*The average fixed cost (AFC)* is the total fixed costs (E.g. Rent ) that remain constant while the number of goods and services produced by a company changes.
[Graph: "Average Fixed Cost Curve" — y-axis Average Fixed Cost (₹) 2–10; x-axis Output (in units) 1–5; downward-sloping curve labelled "AFC"]

*Average variable cost (VC) curve* shows the relationship between a firm's variable costs (E.g. Raw materials) and its output level in the short run.
[Graph: "Average Variable Cost" — y-axis Average Variable Cost (₹) 1–6; x-axis Output (in units) 1–5; U-shaped curve labelled "AVC"]

Average variable cost (AVC) + average fixed cost (AFC) = average total cost (ATC)

**Law of Diminishing Marginal Utility**
- •The Law of Diminishing Marginal Utility states that as a person consumes more units of a good, *the additional satisfaction (utility) derived from each extra unit gradually decreases, assuming all other factors remain constant.*

*E.g. Jab tum pahla golgappa khate ho toh jo anand ki prapti hoti hai woh dusre golgappe main nahi hoti kyunki dhire dhire additional satisfaction (utility) kam hota jata hai.*
[Graph: Law of Diminishing Marginal Utility — y-axis Marginal Utility of Product (25…−10), x-axis Units of Product 1–6; points A, B, C, D, E; "Zero MU (Point of Satiety)"; "Negative MU"; curve labelled MU]

**Right column**

**ISOQUANT GRAPH**
A curve showing combinations of inputs (eg. Labor and Capital) that produce the same quantity of output.
*Slope:* Marginal Rate of Technical Substitution (MRTS).
[Graph: Capital per year (10–50) vs Labor per year (10–50); three isoquants labelled q=275, q=375, q=450]

**Box heading: DEMAND**
- •The quantity of a good or service that consumers are willing and able to buy at a particular price during a specific period of time *(willingness + ability).*
- •**Law of Demand:** It is a fundamental principle which states that there is an inverse relationship between price and quantity demanded.

[Graph: "EAGERNESS TO BUY SOMETHING" — Price ($) 10, 20 vs Quantity Demanded 100, 200; downward curve D0 from A to B; label "NEGATIVE SLOPING CURVE"; "RELATION BETWEEN PRICE AND DEMAND"; side box "OTHER FACTORS REMAIN CONSTANT (CETERIS PARIBUS) E.G. INCOME."]

- •There is an *inverse relationship between price and demand.* When the price of a product increases, people tend to buy less of it and when the price decreases, people usually buy more of it. *However, this is not always true, this is true only under given conditions:*
  1. When income is constant.
  2. When price of other goods is constant.
  3. When there is no change in preferences.
  [small arrows graphic: P↑ D↓ / P↓ D↑]

**Movement and Shift in Demand Curve**
- •Movement in demand curve happens when there is a *change in price of the product.*
- •Shift in demand curve occurs *when factors other than price change,* such as income, consumer preferences, or the price of related goods changes.

### [book p.253 / PDF 274]

**Left column**

- •Shift on the curve can occurs in *rightward or leftward position.*
[two small graphs] "Rightward Shift: Increase in demand." / "Leftward Shift: Decrease in demand."

**Exceptions to Law of Demand**
- •**Giffen Goods:** These are a type of *inferior goods/non-luxurious goods* where *demand may increase when prices rise,* due to the *absence of better alternatives or limited purchasing power.*
  *E.g. Increase in price of wheat.*
- •**Veblen Goods:** Veblen goods are *luxury goods* that have a *direct relationship between price and demand.* This means that the demand for Veblen goods *increases as their price increases,* which is the opposite of the law of demand.
  *E.g. iPhone, Mercedes.*

**Ex Ante (Expected) Aggregate Demand**
Intended expenditure on goods and services during a given period.
AD (Aggregate Demand) = C (Intended consumption) + I (Intended investment).
- •*Equilibrium Condition:* Equilibrium is reached when aggregate demand (AD) equals aggregate supply (AS) i.e., AD=AS.
- •If AD >AS; producers increase output to meet demand.
- •If AD<AS; inventory buildup occurs, so producers reduce output.

*NOTE:* In macroeconomics treating a single commodity as representative of all goods allows simplification of aggregate analysis because it allows economists to model total production, price levels as a single variable.

**Box heading: SUPPLY**
- •Supply is the amount of a product or service that is *available for purchase or use.*

**Supply Curve**
- •A supply curve is a graphical representation of the *relationship between the price of a good or service* and the quantity that producers are willing and able to supply in a given period of time.
- •Producer want to maximise profit.

**Right column**

[Graph 4: Supply curve — Price vs Quantity, upward-sloping "Supply"]
*Positive sloping curve* is seen. When there is a direct relation between price and quantity supplied. Producers produce more when they get better price for their product.

**When things exists in Equilibrium the Point where the demand curve intersect the supply curve**
- •This determine the *"equilibrium price"* or market clearing price.
- •Quantity Demanded = Quantity Supplied.

[Graph 5: Supply-Demand curve at Equilibrium — Price vs Quantity; "Deficit" (left of equilibrium, below price), "Surplus" (right, above); "Equilibrium Price", "Equilibrium Quantity"; curves labelled Supply Curve, Demand Curve]
- •Equilibrium shifts, if any of the factors shift.
- •If demand decreases, the curve shifts to the left while if demand increases, the curve shifts to the right.

**Price Elasticity**
- •Price elasticity of demand measures how much the quantity demanded of a good or service changes in response to a change in its price.
  [formula box] e_d = % change in demand / % change in price

**Perfectly Elastic Demand**
- •In perfectly elastic demand, *a small rise in price results in a fall in demand to zero, while a small fall in price causes an increase in demand to infinity.* In such a case, the demand is perfectly elastic or (e = ∞)
*E.g. Luxury products such as gold and cars.*
[Graph 6: Perfectly Elastic Demand curve — horizontal line D at price P; label "e = ∞", "Horizontal Curve"; axes Price, Output]

**Relatively Elastic Demand**
- •Relatively elastic demand is when a *small change in price leads to a large change in the quantity demanded of a product or service.* This means that consumers are sensitive to price changes.
(continues p.254)

### [book p.254 / PDF 275]

**Left column**

[Graph 7: Relatively Elastic Demand curve — Price (in Rs), Quantity Demanded (in units); "Slight change in price result in high change in demand"; "e_d > 1"]

**Perfectly Inelastic Demand**
- •Perfectly inelastic demand is *when the demand for a product remains the same regardless of price changes.* This means that the quantity demanded is fixed and does not change.
*Eg. Necessary goods (water, wheat and rice).*
[Graph 8: Perfectly Inelastic Demand curve — vertical curve; "No changes in quantity of product demanded when the price changes"; "e_d = 0"]

**Relatively Inelastic Demand**
- •Relatively inelastic demand is *when the demand for a product changes in response to price, but the change is not proportional to the price change.* This means that the price of a product changes more than the demand for that product.
[Graph 9: Relatively Inelastic Demand curve — "Changes in price causes relatively less change in quantity demand"; "(e_d < 1)"; "e_d < 1"]

**Unitary Elastic Demand**
The demand curve for unitary elastic demand is a *rectangular hyperbola.* It occurs when a change (rise or fall) in price results in an equally proportional change in demand.
- •In this case total revenue is maximum
[Graph 10: Unitary Elastic Demand — "rectangular hyperbola"; "e_d = 1"]

**Right column**

**Income Elasticity**
- •Income elasticity of demand measures the relationship between a *change in the quantity demanded for a particular good and a change in real income.*
- •For most goods, when income goes up, demand goes up. The demand curve shifts to the right, showing a positive relationship.
  [formula box] Income Elasticity = % of change in quantity demanded / % of change in income

**1. Luxury Goods**
- •Income Elasticity of Demand > 1 (Positive)
- •Demand increases more than proportionately when income increases.
- •People buy these goods mainly when their income rises.
*Examples: Luxury cars, designer clothes, expensive vacations.*

**2. Inferior Goods**
- •Income Elasticity of Demand < 0 (negative)
- •Demand decreases when income increases because consumers switch to better-quality goods.
*Examples: Low-quality food grains, public transport instead of cars.*

[Graph 11: Income Elasticity Curve — three panels: "Income Elastic" (steep upward curve), "Income Inelastic" (flattening curve), and a third with "Positive Income Elastic" / "Negative Income Inelastic"]

**Cross-Price Elasticity**
- •Cross-price elasticity is an economic measure of how a *change in the price of one good affects the demand for another good.*
*E.g. If the price of Pepsi rises by 10% and Coke's demand increases by 5%.*
[Graph 12: Cross-Price Elasticity Curve — Price of Good Y (Py2=$3, Py1=$1) vs Quantity of Good X (Qx1=1, Qx2=4); note: "Negative cross price elasticity of Demand => Complementary Goods • If Price of Y increases, Qty of Y decreases, and Qty of X decreases (Since X and Y are consumed together)"]

### [book p.255 / PDF 276]

**Left column**

**Substitute Goods vs Complimentary Goods** (spelling as printed)

| Substitute Goods | Complimentary Goods |
|---|---|
| They refer to those goods which can be used in place of one another to satisfy a particular want. | They refer to those goods which are used together to satisfy a particular want. |
| They have a competitive demand. | They have a joint demand. |
| Price of one substitute good, has positive relationship with quantity demanded of another substitute good. | Price of one complementary good has a negative relationship with the quantity demanded of another complimentary good. |
| E.g. Tea and Coffee, Coke and Pepsi. | E.g. Tea and Sugar, Car and Petrol. |

**Law of Diminishing Returns**
After a certain level, additional inputs will lead to smaller gains in output.
It is applicable in short run production [a time period where at least one factor of production (typically capital) is fixed, while others, such as labour and raw materials are variable].
[Graph: Total Output vs Total Input; curve labelled zones "Most Productive" → "Point of Diminishing Returns" (X marker) → "Diminishing Returns" → "Point of Maximum Yield" (X marker) → "Negative Returns" (curve falls)]

**Abnormal Profits**
Profits earned by a business firm over and above the opportunity cost of the factor inputs.

**Optimum profit**
Max achievable profit a business can attain, occurring when marginal revenue (MR) equals marginal cost (MC).

**Regulated profit**
Maximum earnings, prices, or rate of return set by government authorities for specific industries to prevent monopoly abuses and ensure fair consumer pricing.

**Bandwagon Effect (Demonstration Effect)**
Increase in demand of a commodity because others are also consuming the commodity.
*Eg: Buying 2nd edition of Fatman, buying a new phone in market which everyone is buying.*

**Right column**

**Snob Effect**
Decrease in demand of a commodity because others are also consuming the commodity; It is the opposite of Bandwagon effect.
*Eg: Buying a limited-edition watch*

**Box heading: COSTS**

**Fixed Cost**
Remains constant regardless of production volume. *(e.g. Rent, License cost, Registration cost etc.)*

**Variable Cost**
It fluctuates directly with output *(e.g. raw materials, shipping.)*

**Explicit Cost**
It is a direct payment made to others in the course of running a business such as *wage, rent and materials.*

**Sunk Cost**
Cost incurred in the past and that cannot be recovered in the future. *(eg: Advertising expense, Legal and accounting fee.)*

**Break Even Point of a firm**
It is the point where total revenue equals total cost (expenditure). It is the point of *"no profit, no loss".*

**Production function of a firm**
It is the relationship between inputs used and output produced.

**Box heading: TYPES OF MARKET**

**Monopoly**
- •One seller controls the entire market.
- •No close substitutes for the product.
- •High barriers to entry.
- •*E.g. Indian railways*

**Oligopoly**
- •A few large firms dominate the market.
- •Products may be similar or differentiated.
- •Many buyers for one product.
- •No easy entry.
- •*E.g. Telecom sector, laptop market*

### [book p.256 / PDF 277]

**Left column**

**Monopolistic Competition**
- •Many sellers and many buyers for a product.
- •Products are similar but not identical (differentiated)
- •Some control over price.
- •Easy entry and exit.
- • Highly Elastic (but not perfectly elastic)
- •*E.g. Toothpaste*

**Perfect Competition**
- •Many buyers and sellers.
- •Products are identical (homogeneous).
- •No barriers to entry.
- •Price is determined by supply and demand.
- •Price elasticity: Perfectly Elastic (Ed = ∞)
*E.g. Agriculture products*

**Box heading: MACROECONOMIC CONCEPT**

**Marginal Propensity to Consume**
- •Marginal Propensity to Consume (MPC) refers to the *portion of additional income that a person spends on consumption rather than saving.* It measures how much consumption increases when income rises by one unit.
- •MPC is *dependent on the income level* as it may vary with the income levels *(MPC is lower at higher income levels).*
  [formula box] MPC = Change in Consumption (ΔC) / Change in Income (ΔY)
- •*MPC value: 0 < MPC < 1*
- •*E.g. If income rises by Rs. 10,000 and spending increases by Rs. 1,000, then:*
  [box] MPC = 1000 / 10000 = 0.1

**Autonomous Expenditure**
- •It is the spending that occurs *regardless of the level of income* in an economy. It is *independent of income.*
  [box] C = A + cY  — A: Income-independent consumption; cY: Income-dependent consumption (c: Marginal propensity to consume; Y: Level of income); C: Total consumption
  [box] Savings = 1 − c
  [box] Multiplier = 1 / (1−c)
  [box] MPC + MPS = 1
*MPC - Marginal Propensity to consume*
*MPS - Marginal Propensity to save*

■■■ (red end marker — end of Ch.2)

- ⚠ Heading "Average fixed cost curve and Variable fixed cost curve" — as printed; the second curve described is *Average Variable Cost* (the text and graph both say AVC).
- ⚠ The AFC/AVC graphs (p.252) carry only illustrative points; not book text.

Luxury products such as gold and cars" (p.253) — as printed; textbooks usually give perfect competition/homogeneous product as the practical case (Parmar itself lists "Perfect Competition — Price elasticity: Perfectly Elastic (Ed = ∞), E.g. Agriculture products" on p.256). Flagged as an internal inconsistency in examples, not corrected. Also, "luxury goods" are normally *relatively elastic*, not perfectly elastic.
- ⚠ "Giffen Goods … E.g. Increase in price of wheat" (p.253) — as printed; the standard textbook Giffen examples are staple foods such as inferior grains/potatoes; "wheat" is Parmar's example.
- ⚠ "Veblen goods … E.g. iPhone, Mercedes" — as printed.
- ⚠ "Monopoly — E.g. Indian railways" — as printed (Parmar's example).
- ⚠ "Oligopoly … Many buyers for one product" — as printed.
- ⚠ Unitary elastic demand: "In this case total revenue is maximum" — as printed (the standard statement is that total revenue is unchanged/constant along a unitary-elastic demand curve and maximised at the unit-elastic point on a linear demand curve); not corrected.
- ⚠ Income elasticity: "Inferior Goods — Income Elasticity of Demand < 0" and "Luxury goods > 1" as printed — matches the standard classification.
- ⚠ Law of Diminishing Returns graph labels: transcribed as read at 120 dpi (point labels partly small).
- ⚠ Ex Ante (Expected) Aggregate Demand: "AD = C + I" (no G or NX) — as printed.
- ⚠ Multiplier formula given as "1/(1−c)" and "Savings = 1 − c" with c = MPC — as printed.

## PART B — LUCENT'S GK (2026), Ch.06 "Indian Economy", §2 "Economy and Economics" (Lucent book pp.252–254)

Page mapping: Lucent Economy PDF page = book page + 12 (PDF 264 = book 252 … PDF 266 = book 254).

### [Lucent book p.252 / PDF 264]

**2. Economy and Economics**

Economy : It is the state of a country or region in terms of the production and consumption of goods and services and the supply of money.

Economics : It is the branch of knowledge concerned with the production, consumption and transfer of wealth.

**Types of Economy**
- ➤ Depending upon the dominant view of the time in a particular country, different forms of production patterns evolved and different economic systems finally came up, providing alternative ways of organising an economy.
- ➤ The three models of economic systems which we see coming up are basically the different stages in the evolutionary process for our experiments which define a better way of organising our economy.

**1. Capitalistic Economy**
- ➤ The capitalist form of economy has its origin in the famous work of Adam Smith—*Wealth of Nations* (1776). Adam Smith (1723-1790) the Scottish philosopher-economist professor at University of Glasgow whose writings formed the basis of classical economics had stressed certain fine ideas which were to take fancy among some of the western countries and finally capitalism took birth.
- ➤ Smith raised his voice against the heavy-handed government regulation of commerce and industry of the time which did not allow the economy to tap its full economic worth and reach the level of well-being. Stressing 'division of labour', an environment of 'laissez faire' (non-interference by the government), he proposed that the 'invisible hand' of 'market forces' (price mechanism) will bring a state of equilibrium in the economy and a general well-being to the countrymen. For such an economy to function for public well-being, he has acknowledged the need of *competition* in the market.
- ➤ Once the USA attained Independence the ideas of Adam Smith were made part of its public policy—just one year after *Wealth of Nations* was published. From here the idea spread to other parts of Euro-America—by 1800 the economic system called 'capitalism' was established which was later known by different names—Private Enterprise System. Free Enterprise System, Market Economy.
- ➤ The decisions of what to produce, how much to produce and at what price to sell are taken by the market, by the private enterprises in this system, with the state having no economic role.
- ➤ [pink box] As Keynes has suggested that the capitalist economy should move few steps towards socialistic economy, Prof. Lange was suggesting just the same in the case of the state economies.

**2. State Economy**
- ➤ Rooted in the ideas of historical change proposed by the German philosopher Karl Marx (1818-1883) more specifically, this kind of economic system first came up in the erstwhile USSR after the Bolshevik Revolution (1917) and got its ideal shape in the People's Republic of China (1949).
- ➤ This form of economic system also spread to other countries in eastern Europe. Here we see two versions of the state economy—erstwhile USSR known as the *socialist economy* and in pre-1985 China as the *communist economy.*
- ➤ While socialistic economy emphasised the collective ownership of the means of production (property and assets) and it also ascribed a large role to the state in running the economy, communist economy advocated state ownership of all properties including labour and absolute power to state in running the economy.
- ➤ Though for Marx, Socialism was a transitional stage to communism, it never did happen in reality.
- ➤ The socialist and communist economies used to criticise capitalistic economics of being based on exploitation. In response, the capitalist economies called them the practioners of 'state capitalism', where the states were the sole exploitators.

**3. Mixed Economy**
- ➤ The belief in the self-correcting quality of the market and the 'invisible hand' of Adam Smith got a major setback in early 20th century during the 'Great Depression' (1929).
- ➤ The impact of the depression spread from the USA to other economies of Western Europe escalating large scale unemployment, downfall in demand and economic activities and lockouts in industrial enterprises. The prevailing Smithonian macro ideas failed to check the crisis. A new approach was needed which came in the famous work.
- ➤ *The General Theory of Employment, Interest and Money* (1936) by the English economist at Cambridge University, John Maynard Keynes (1883-1946).
- ➤ Keynes questioned the very principle of 'laissez-faire' and the nature of the 'invisible hand'. He even opined that the invisible hand brings equilibrium to the economy but by 'strangulating the poor'. He suggested that prices and wages are not flexible enough to provide empolyment to all.
- ➤ Questioning the limitations of the market mechanism, Keynes suggested *strong government intervention* in the economy. To get the economy out of the depression, he suggested an increase in government expenditures, discretionary fiscal policy (fiscal deficit, lower interest rates, cheap money supply, etc.) to boost the demand of goods and services as this was the reason behind the depression.
- ➤ As Keynesian policies were followed, the concerned economies were successfully pulled out of the Great Depression.
- ➤ As a follow up to the Keynesian advices, many trendsetting economic policies were initiated throughout the capitalist economies. One very important initiative which came out was the government's active role in the economy. [continues p.253]

### [Lucent book p.253 / PDF 265]

**Left column**
- ➤ The essential goods and services which were till date being purchased by the people as 'private goods' were soon made available by the state 'free-of-cost' giving people more spare money to create demand for the goods and services which were part of the market.
- ➤ The *mixed economy* arrived in this way and the classical capitalist economy was challenged by it.
- ➤ On the margins of the developments given above, it is interesting to note the developments in the state economies of the time. It was *Prof. Oscar Lange (1904-65),* the Polish philosopher, who in 1950s suggested the same thing for the socialist economy as Keynes had for the capitalist. Prof. Lange praised the state economy for many of its good things, but also suggested inclusion of some of the good things of the capitalistic economy.
- ➤ He advised the state economies to adopt *'market socialism'* (the term was coined by him). His suggestions were outrightly rejected by the state economies as such compromises in the socialistic economic order were blasphemous at that time (this was ultimately a suggestion towards democracy from dictatorship).
- ➤ As Keynes has suggested that the capitalist economy should move few steps towards socialistic economy. Prof. Lange was suggesting just the same in the case of the state economies. Democracies are flexible thus they were able to go for an experiment which paid them in coming times.

**Some Important Books/Theories of Economics** [pink table]
| Book / Theory | Author |
|---|---|
| The Wealth of Nations | Adam Smith |
| Money Illusion | Irwin Fisher |
| Capital and Growth | Sri J.R. Hicks |
| General Theory of Employment, Interest and Money | J. M. Keynes |
| A Discources on Political Economy | Jean Jacques Rousseau |
| Planned Economy for India | M. Vishveshwaraiya |
| The Value and Capital | Sri J.R. Hicks |
| The Canon (theory) of Consumer's Surplus | Marshall |
| Big Push Theory | A. R. Rodon |
| Datt & Sundharam's Indian Economy | Gaurav Datt and Ashwani Mahajan |

**Economic Growth and Development**
- ➤ A term coming from the life sciences, 'growth' in economics means economic growth. An increase in economic variables over a period of time is—economic growth. The most important aspect of growth is its *quantifiability,* i.e. one can measure it in absolute terms*.
  (*As the IMF and the WB considered this yardstick of development as quoted in Gerald M. Meier and James E. Rauch, *Leading issues in Economic Development,* Oxford University Press, New Delhi, 2006.)
- ➤ All the units of measurement may be applied to show it, depending upon the economic variable, where the growth is being studied. We have a few examples :
1. An economy might have been able to see growth in food production during a decade which could be measured in tonnes.
2. The growth of road network in an economy might be measured for a decade or any period in miles or kilometres.

**Right column**
3. Similarly, the value of the total production of an economy might be measured in currency terms which means the economy is growing.
4. Per capita income for an economy might be measured in monetary terms over a period.
We may say that *economic growth is a quantitative progress.*

To calculate the *growth rate* of an economic variable the difference between the concerned period is converted into percentage form.
- ➤ Growth rate is an *annual concept* which may be used. Otherwise with the clear reference to the period for which it is used.
- ➤ Though growth is a value neutral term, i.e., it might be positive or negative for an economy for a period, we generally use it in the positive sense. If economists say an economy is growing it means the economy is having a positive growth otherwise they use the term *'negative growth'.*

**Economic Growth**
- ➤ The National Statistics Office (NSO), Ministry of Statistics and Programme Implementation (MoSPI) released the Quarterly Estimates of Gross Domestic Product (GDP) for the July-September Quarter (Q2) of the Financial Year (FY) 2024-25. Real GDP has been estimated to grow by 5.4 % in Q2 of FY 2024-25 over the growth rate of 8.1% in Q2 of FY 2023-24.
- ➤ Despite sluggish growth observed in manufacturing and mining and quarrying sectors, real Gross Value Added (GVA) in H1 (April-September) has recorded a growth rate of 6.2 %. Real GDP or GDP at Constant Prices in Q2 of 2024-25 is estimated at ₹ 44.10 lakh crore, against Rs. 41.86 lakh crore in Q2 of 2023-24, showing a growth rate of 5.4 %.
- ➤ Nominal GDP or GDP at Current Prices in Q2 of 2024-25 is estimated at ₹ 76.60 lakh crore, against ₹ 70.90 lakh crore in Q2 of 2023-24, showing a growth rate of 8.0 %.   *[Source : BHARAT 2025]*

**Prices**
- ➤ According to BHARAT 2025, The retail inflation rate based on the Consumer Price Index (Combined) (Base 2012=100) for 2023-24 decreased to 5.4 % from 6.7 % in 2022-23. In the financial year (2024-25), the average retail inflation rate was 4.8 % between April and October.

**GDP Growth**
- ➤ India's GDP at constant (2011-12) prices grew by 6.7% and 5.4 % in Q1 and Q2 FY25, respectively. This implied a real GDP growth of 6.0 % in the first half of the current fiscal.   *[Source : Economic Survey 2024-25]*

**GDP by expenditure categories**
- ➤ India's GDP at constant (2011-12) prices grew by 6.7 % and 5.4 % in Q1 and Q2 FY25, respectively. This implied a real GDP growth of 6.0 % in the first half of the current fiscal.
- ➤ From a demand perspective, Private Final Consumption Expenditure (PFCE) firmed up in H1 FY25, growing by 6.7 % YoY. While National Accounts data is not disaggregated by geography, indicators such as 2-wheeler and 3-wheeler sales and tractor sales [continues p.254]

### [Lucent book p.254 / PDF 266]

**Left column**
signal that rural demand contributed to private consumption growth.
- ➤ This is also reflected in the January 2025 round of National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development (NABARD's) Rural Economic Conditions and Sentiments Survey, where 78.5 % of rural households reported an increase in their consumption expenditure during the last year.

**Manufacturing Sector Growth**
- ➤ According to ES 2024-25 the industrial sector grew by 6 % in H1 FY25. Q1 saw a strong growth of 8.3 %, but growth moderated in Q2.
- ➤ Disaggregated data reveals that while many manufacturing sub-sectors experienced growth, others faced challenges, likely due to global and seasonal factors. Oil companies suffered due to inventory losses and lower refining margins, while steel companies faced price pressures and lower global prices. The cement sector faced weak demand in Q2 due to heavy rains and lower selling prices.
- ➤ Despite various challenges, India continues to register the fastest growth in manufacturing PMI. The latest Manufacturing PMI for December 2024 remained well within the expansionary zone. The expansion rate for December 2024 exceeded its long-term average, driven by new business gains, robust demand, and advertising efforts.
- ➤ According to the RBI's Industrial Outlook Survey, manufacturing firms reported improved demand conditions in Q3 FY25 and expect further improvements in Q4 FY25 and Q1 FY26.

**Services Sector Growth**
- ➤ The services sector continues to perform well in FY25. A notable growth in Q1 and Q2 resulted in 7.1 % growth in H1 FY25. Across sub-categories, all the sub-sectors have performed well. The robust performance of the services sector is also reflected in high-frequency indicators (HFIs). PMI services have been in an expansionary zone during H1 FY25, supported by growth in new orders, rise in output, improvement in sales and enhanced employment generation. The hospitality sector performed well, with hotel occupancy rates in H1 FY25 similar to the previous year.
- ➤ Air cargo activity grew in double digits, while port traffic remained stable. Information Technology (IT) companies also performed better than the previous quarter (RBI Bulletin, December 24, 2024).

**Economic Development**
- ➤ Economic growth was considered as a cause and effect for the betterment of lives of the people. This was the reason why economists till the 1950s failed to distinguish growth and development though they knew the difference between these terms.
- ➤ It was during the 1960s and in the later decades that economists came across many countries where the growth was comparatively higher, but the quality of life was comparatively low.
- ➤ For economists, development indicates the quality of life in the economy which might be seen in accordance with the availability of so many variables such as :

**Right column**
1. The level of nutrition
2. The expansion and the reach of healthcare facilities—hospitals, medicines, safe drinking water, vaccination, sanitation, etc.
3. The level of education among the people
4. Other variables on which the quality of life depends
- ➤ Here, one basic thing must be kept in mind that if the masses are to be guaranteed with a basic minimum level of quality-enhancing inputs (above-given variables such as food, health, education, etc.) in their life, a minimum level of income has to be guaranteed for them. Income is generated from productive activities. It means that before assuring development we need to assure growth.
- ➤ Higher economic development requires higher economic growth. But it does not mean that a higher economic growth automatically brings in higher economic development—a confusion the early economists failed to clear.

# ECONOMY RAW EXTRACTION — National Income Accounting and GDP Highlights

Sources: Parmar SSC Fatman (2nd Ed. 2026), Economics; Lucent's General Knowledge (2026), Ch.06 Indian Economy. Verbatim transcription with typos preserved; ⚠ marks doubts. No test cross-referencing and no ★-marking at this stage.

Order: Part A (Parmar) then Part B (Lucent) then Part C (cross-book comparison).

---

## PART A — PARMAR SSC FATMAN (2nd Ed. 2026): Economics Ch.3 "NATIONAL INCOME" (book pp.257–260, PDF pp.278–281)

Running header "NATIONAL INCOME". Chapter ends with ■■■ at right column, mid-page 260 (rest of the page blank).

### [book p.257 / PDF 278]

**Left column — box titled "NATIONAL INCOME"**
- •National income is the total value of all goods and services produced by a country's residents in a given year. It is a *"Macroeconomic"* concept.
- •For the first time, the concept of *National Income* was given by *Dadabhai Naoroji.*
- •*PC Mahalanobis* headed the *National Income Committee (1949).*
- •*V.K.R.V. Rao* used a scientific approach to calculate National Income. *He also gave the two-sector economy classification.*

**Circular Flow of Income**
- •The Circular Flow of Income is a model/concept often used to describe the *continuous movement of goods, services, resources, and money in an economy.*
[Diagram "Circular flow of income": Households ⇄ Firms; upper arrows "Goods & Services" (Product Method) and "Consumer Expenditure" (Expenditure Method); lower arrows "Wages, Rent, Dividends" and "Factor for Production" (Income Method)]
- •Households own land, labor, and capital. They provide these to firms in return for income (wages, rent, profits).
- •Firms use these resources to produce goods and services and sell them to households.
- •Households spend money to buy goods and services from firms (expenditure).
- •Firms pay money to households for the resources they use (income).
- •This cycle keeps the economy moving by maintaining a continuous flow.

**Real Flow**
Real flow refers to the movement of goods, services, and factors of production (land, labour, capital, entrepreneurship) between households and firms in the economy.

**Money Flow**
Money flow refers to the movement of money (payments) between households and firms in exchange for goods, services, and factors of production.

| Factors of Production | Factors of Payment |
|---|---|
| Land | Rent |
| Labour | Wages |
| Capital | Interest |
| Entrepreneurs | Profit |

**Right column**

**Law of Variable Proportions**
When we increase quantity of one factor of production (e.g. labour) while keeping others fixed.
The total output, first rise at increasing rate, then rise at decreasing rate, then declines.
[Graph "Law of Variable Proportion": y-axis Product; x-axis Labour; curves TP, AP, MP; Stage I, Stage II, Stage III marked; "Point of Inflexion" at A; points B, C, D, E, O labelled]

**Two-Sector Model :**
- •This is the simplest model.
- •Households and Firms

**Three-Sector Model :**
- •This model introduces the Government sector
- •Households, Firms, Government

**Four-Sector Model: (Open Economy)**
- •Households, Firms, Government and Foreign trade

**Injection (Money Entering the Economy)**
- •Injections are additional *funds that enter the economy,* increasing the total spending and income. They help expand economic activity.
*Example*
- •Investment: When businesses spend money on capital goods like machinery or buildings to expand production.
- •Government Spending: Public sector expenditure on goods and services like infrastructure, education, or defense.
- •Exports: Sales of domestic goods and services to foreign buyers, bringing money into the domestic economy.

**Leakage (Money Leaving the Economy)**
- •Leakages are *funds that exit the circular flow,* reducing total spending and slowing economic growth.
*Example*
- •Savings: When households save instead of spending their income.
- •Taxes: When people pay taxes to the government.
- •Imports: When people spend money on foreign-produced goods and services.

### [book p.258 / PDF 279]

**Left column**

**Three Methods to Calculate National Income**

**1. Value Added Method/Production Method/Output Method**
- •It is a way to calculate the *value of a product or service at each stage of production.* It is also known as production method.
[box] Value added method = Output-Input
*Example — Value added in every stage.*

| Cotton | Yarn | Cloth | Shirt |
|---|---|---|---|
| Rs 100 | Rs 200 | Rs 400 | Rs 800 |
| Rs 100 | Rs 100 | Rs 200 | Rs 400 |

(top row = value of output at each stage; bottom row = value added at each stage, as printed)

- •*Gross Value Added (GVA):* The value that producers add to goods and services they buy and sell.
[box] GVA = GVA₁ + GVA₂ + GVA₃ GVA + ....... GVA_N (as printed)
[box] National Statistics Office (CSO) adopted the GVA in 2015

**2. Income Method**
This method includes *all the income generated by the factor of production* which are inputs in the process of producing final goods or services, in the entire economy.
*Components of Income Method*
(a) *Compensation to Employees:* It refers to the *total wages or salaries paid to workers for their product services.*
(b) *Operating Surplus:* It refers to the *profit earned by businesses* (profit, rent and interest) *after deducting employees compensation* (wages).
(c) *Mixed Income:* It is the income earned by *self-employed individuals and unincorporated businesses.* It arises when *elements of factor income (labour and capital) cannot be distinctly separated.* E.g. A doctor running their own clinic.

**3. Expenditure Method**
It is way to calculate a country's GDP by *adding up all spending on goods and services in the economy.* It's the most common method for calculating GDP.
[box] Expenditure Method = C + G + I + (X–M)
C = Consumption; G = Government Expenditure; I = Investment; X= Export; M = Import

[box] **Orange Economy** — It refers to the part of the economy that is based on creativity, culture, and intellectual property. It includes activities where ideas, talent, and cultural expression are converted into economic value and jobs. *Examples: Film industry, Music industry, Fashion design.*

**Right column**

| Final Goods | Intermediate Goods |
|---|---|
| These goods will now not undergo any economic process. | These are semi-finished products that will undergo various economic process and transformation to be converted to final products. |
| Goods used by the consumer for personal consumption or Investment. | Goods used as inputs for producing other goods and services and will get customised in the process. |
| Direct consumption or capital investment. | Further processing or resale. |
| E.g. When you buy tea from a shop for personal consumption, It is a final good. | E.g. When a tea shop or hotel buys tea to prepare and sell tea to customers, it becomes an intermediate good. |

Every economy faces three fundamental problems due to scarcity of resources:
*What to Produce?   How to Produce?   For Whom to Produce?*

**What to Produce?**
This problem refers to deciding which goods and services should be produced and in what quantity.
An economy must decide how its limited resources should be allocated between different types of goods.

**Types of Final Goods**
*Consumer Goods*
Finished goods that are used directly by individuals for personal consumption and are not used to produce other goods.
*Example: Clothes, food, smartphones.*
*Capital Goods*
Goods that are used by businesses to produce other goods and services. They help in production and do not get consumed immediately.
*Example: Machines used to produce T-shirts, factory equipment, tools.*

**Inventory**
- •It refers to the *items a business keeps in stock for sale or production purposes.* It can include raw materials, work-in-progress items and finished goods, depending on the type of business.
- •If the sale of goods increases the inventory decumulates and if the sale of goods decreases the inventory accumulates. It can be either planned or unplanned.

### [book p.259 / PDF 280]

**Left column**

**Aids or Auxiliaries to Trade**
Auxiliaries to trade refers to the factors that are essential for bringing the goods from the place of their production to the place of their consumption. In other words, these are essential services and functions that assist the business. *E.g. Transportation, communication, banking, insurance, warehousing, and advertising.*

**Stock vs Flow**

| Stock | Flow |
|---|---|
| Static in nature | Dynamic in nature |
| It is a quantity measured at a point of time | It is a quantity measured over a period of time |
| E.g. Wealth, loan, inventory, capital, Money Supply | E.g. Income, expenditure, change in inventory, depreciation |

**Box heading: AGGREGATES OF NATIONAL INCOME**

**Gross Domestic Product (GDP)**
- •GDP measures *total value of all final goods and services* produced within the territory of a country *within a financial year (Financial Year = 1st April-31 March).*
- *In GDP second hand goods are never counted.*
- •While calculating GDP, value of *only final goods is considered* and not intermediate goods to avoid *Double Counting.*
[side box] GDP was developed by Russian-American Economist "Simon Kuznets" in 1934.

**Does GDP Reflect the Welfare of Society?**
Although GDP is an important indicator of economic growth and production, it does not fully reflect the welfare or well-being of society.
*Limitations of GDP as a Measure of Welfare*
1. *Ignores Income Distribution* — GDP shows the total income of a country, but it does not indicate how that income is distributed among people. A country may have high GDP while many people \ poor. (as printed, stray backslash)
2. *Excludes Non-Market Activities* — Activities such as household work, voluntary services, and caregiving contribute to welfare but are not included in GDP because they are not sold in the market.
3. *Ignores Environmental Damage* — Production that causes pollution or environmental degradation increases GDP but may reduce the overall quality of life.
4. *Excludes Quality of Life Factors* — GDP does not consider health, education, leisure, safety, or happiness, which are important for social welfare.
5. *Underground Economy Not Included* — Economic activities in the informal or black market are generally not counted in GDP.

**Right column**

**Gross National Product (GNP)**
- •*Total value of all final goods and services produced by the nationals of the country.*
[box] GNP = GDP-Factor Income to Abroad + FI from Abroad
OR
GNP = GDP+Net Factor Income from Abroad (NFIA)

**Net Domestic Product (NDP)**
- •It is the measure of a *country's economic output* after adjusting for the *depreciation (or consumption) of fixed capital.*
- •Monetary Value of an asset *decreases over time due to various factors* (E.g. wear & tear) and is called as *Depreciation.* It is seen in Capital Goods.
[box] NDP = GDP - Depreciation

**Net National Product (NNP)**
- •It is the *monetary value* of *finished goods and services* produced by *a country's citizens in a given period.*
[box] NNP = GNP - Depreciation

| Market Price (MP) | Factor Cost (FC) |
|---|---|
| The market price is the final value of the product being sold, which includes indirect taxes. | Factor cost is the cost of factors of production, or total value of inputs, where indirect tax is not included |

[box] FC = MP - Indirect tax + Subsidy
FC = MP - (Indirect tax - Subsidy)
FC= MP - Net Indirect Tax
[box] Basic Price = FC + Net production tax
Net production tax = (Production tax - Subsidy)

*REMEMBER :* Product taxes are levied per unit of goods/ services sold (e.g., GST, excise duty, sales tax), whereas production taxes are paid regardless of output volume, such as land taxes, stamp duties, or license fees. (continues p.260)

### [book p.260 / PDF 281]

**Left column**

Production taxes are independent of production quantity, while product taxes depend on sales volume.

**To Summarise**
1. Gross से Net में जाना हो तो Depreciation substract करो।
2. Domestic से National में जाना हो तो NFIA add करो।
3. MP से FC में जाना हो तो Net Indirect Tax को substract करो।

**Calculation of income**
Nation's Territory = GDP
Nation's People = GNP
Total Income = Total Expenditure = Total value of goods and services

**Real GDP vs Nominal GDP**

| Real GDP | Nominal GDP |
|---|---|
| In this, a base year is taken as reference. | It is more than Real GDP due to Inflation. |
| It is calculated on constant prices. | It is calculated on current prices. |
| It is inflation adjusted. | Inflation is not adjusted here. |

[box] GDP Deflator = (Nominal GDP / Real GDP) × 100

- •GDP Deflator is also called as *"Implicit Price Deflation"* and *"Price Index".*
- •*GDP of India is calculated by: National Statistical Office (NSO) under Ministry of Statistics and Programme Implementation (MOSPI).*
- •The Base Year is considered as: *2022-2023.*

**Per Capita Income**
- •It is the average amount of money earned by each person in a specific area over a period of time.
[box] Per Capital Income = National Income / Population (spelling "Capital" as printed)

**Purchasing Power Parity**
- •Purchasing power is the amount of goods and services that can be bought with a specific amount of money. It can also be referred to as *"a common basket of goods".*
- •As of *2026, India's GDP* ranked *5th in the world* and *India's economy* ranks at *3rd position in the world* in terms of *Purchasing Power Parity* after *USA* and *China* (their economy is inflated)

**Right column**

**Personal Income**
- •Total income of an individual earned from all the sources before taxes.
[box] *Ranking of GDP (Nominal)*: 1. USA  2. China  3. Germany  4. Japan  5. India
[box] PI = National Income (NI) + Income received but not earned-Income earned but not received
OR
PI = NI + Govt. transfers + Net interest paid by Govt. Corporate tax-payment for social security
PI = NI + Transfer Payment - Undistributed corporate profit *"Transfer payment example: Government subsidies/ Scholarship/Pension"*

**Personal Disposable Income**
- •It is the amount of money a *person has after paying taxes* and other mandatory withholding.
[box] Disposable Income = Personal Income - Tax

**Potential GDP**
- •It is the total output an economy would produce if its resources are fully utilised.
[box] Green GDP = GDP-Environmental Damage
Potential GDP-Real GDP = Recessionary Gap

**Gross Domestic Capital Formation:**
The total investment made within a country in a given period of time to create or Increase physical assets.
E.g. Factory building, New machinery.

■■■ (red end marker — end of Ch.3)

India" — verify against current IMF/World Bank rankings; other sources in circulation put India at a different nominal rank; (iii) "National Statistics Office (CSO) adopted the GVA in 2015" (p.258; the body named "CSO" — CSO was merged into NSO in 2019; box text as printed).

Corporate tax-payment for social security" — garbled as printed (the standard form is PI = NI − undistributed profits − corporate tax − social-security contributions + transfer payments); transcribed verbatim, not corrected.

**Key Highlights**
- ➤ Real GDP has been estimated to grow by 6.5% in FY 2024-25. Nominal GDP is expected to witness a growth rate of 9.9% in FY 2024-25. Both the growth rates are revised upward from their respective First Advance Estimates.

- ➤ As per the Final Estimates, Real GDP has observed a growth rate of 7.6% in the financial year 2022-23, mainly contributed by double-digit growth rates in 'Trade, Hotels, Transport, Communication & Services related to Broadcasting' sector (12.3%), 'Financial, Real Estate & Professional Services' sector (10.8%) and 'Electricity, Gas, Water Supply & Other Utility Services' sector (10.8%).

- ➤ The growth rate of Real GDP for Q2 of financial year 2024-25 has been revised upward to 5.6%.
- ➤ 'Construction' sector is estimated to observe a growth rate of 8.6%, followed by 'Financial, Real Estate & Professional Services' sector (7.2%) and 'Trade, Hotels, Transport, Communication & Services related to Broadcasting' sector (6.4%) during 2024-25.
- ➤ Private Final Consumption Expenditure (PFCE) is expected to register a good growth of 7.6% during 2024-25 as compared to 5.6% growth observed during 2023-24.

**Annual Estimates and Growth Rate**

The growth rate in Real GDP during 2024-25 is estimated at 6.5% as compared to 9.2% in 2023-24. Nominal GDP or GDP at Current Prices in Q3 of FY 2024-25 is estimated at ₹ 84.74 lakh crore, against ₹ 77.10 lakh crore in Q3 of FY 2023-24, showing a growth rate of 9.9%.

**Industrial growth pushes up Q4 for GDP to 7.4%**
- ➤ India's Real Gross Domestic Product (GDP) gained strength as it picked up pace sequentially to a four-quarter high of 7.4 % in January-March, 2025 on the back of firm industrial growth in manufacturing, construction and mining sectors even as consumption demand slowed down. The full-year growth rate, however, is estimated to have slowed to a four-year low of 6.5 % for financial year 2024- 25, the same level as the second advance estimates released February-end, data released by the National Statistics Office (NSO) on May 30, 2025 showed.
- ➤ GDP had grown 8.4 % in Q4 in 2023-24 and 9.2 % in FY24. The growth rate for the previous quarter was also revised up, with Q3 growth now estimated at 6.4% against 6.2 % earlier,
- ➤ Industrial growth picked up pace to 6.8 % in Q4 FY25 from 5.1 % in Q3 even though it was slower than the 10.2 % growth seen in Q4 FY24. Manufacturing growth rose to a three-quarter high of 4.8 % in January-March, while the construction sector clocked a double-digit growth [continues p.251]

### [Lucent book p.251 / PDF 263]

**Left column**

rate of 10.8 %. In the year-ago period, manufacturing had grown 11.3 %, while construction growth was 8.7 %.
- ➤ Growth rate in Gross Value Added (GVA) terms came in lower than GDP at 6.8 % in January-March. GVA growth was 6.5 % in the previous quarter and 7.3 % in Q4 FY24. GDP is GVA plus net product taxes (taxes-subsidies). The wide divergence between GVA and GDP rates are being seen on account of a sharp jump in net taxes, which grew by 12.7 % in Q4 as against 5 % growth in the previous quarter and 18.7 % growth in the year-ago period. For the full financial year FY25, GVA growth was recorded at 6.4 % as against 8.6 % in FY24.
- ➤ The upward growth momentum in Q4 was also seen for mining that grew 2.5 % as against 13 % in Q3 and 0.8 % in Q4 FY24. Agriculture is estimated to have grown 5.4 % in Q4, slower than 6.6 % but much higher than 0.9 % in Q4 FY24. Services sector's growth remained steady over 7 % at 7.3 % in Q4. It had grown 7.4 % in the previous quarter and 7.8 % in Q4 FY24.
- ➤ Growth in private final consumption expenditure slowed to a five-quarter low of 6.0 % in Q4 as against 8.1 % in the previous quarter, indicating a tempering of demand at the higher income levels.

**Critical Analysis and Explanation**

**What latest economic data show ?**
- ➤ THE MINISTRY of Statistics and Programme Implementation (MoSPI) released two interrelated data sets on India's national income and the size of its economy on May 30, 2025.
- ➤ The first provides an estimate of India's economic growth in the fourth quarter (Q4, January to March) of the last financial year (2024-25 or FY25). The second provides provisional estimates of economic growth for FY25,

**How is national income measured ?**
- ➤ Economic growth is measured using two metrics.
- ★ Gross Domestic Product (GDP) is calculated by adding up all the expenditures made in the economy, including expenditures by Indians in their individual capacity, expenditures by governments, expenditures by private businesses, etc. This provides a picture of the demand side of the economy.
- ★ Gross Value Added (GVA) looks at the supply side It effectively measures the contribution of each sector of the economy by calculating and summing the value added (or income) at each stage of production.
- ➤ Both GDP and GVA are linked: they measure the same economic performance but through different routes. Their relationship can be spelled out using the following equation:
  GDP = (GVA) + (taxes earned by government) - (subsidies provided by government)

**Why are the estimates "provisional" ?**
- ➤ What makes the estimates released on Friday "provisional" is that they will be revised over the next few years. For any financial year, GDP estimates go through several revisions.
- ➤ In January, the government releases the First Advance Estimates (FAEs) for that financial year. At

**Right column**

the end of February, after incorporating the data from Q3 (third quarter, October to December), MoSPI comes up with the Second Advance Estimates (SAES). By May-end come the Provisional Estimates (PEs) after incorporating data from Q4.
- ➤ The PEs are then revised over the next two years: the First Revised Estimates come a year later, and the Final Estimates two years later. For FY25, these will come in 2026 and 2027 respectively. Each revision benefits from more data, making GDP estimates more accurate.

**Four key takeaways from the data released on May 30, 2025:**

**1. Nominal GDP & its growth**
- ➤ India's nominal GDP grew to Rs 330.7 trillion (lakh crore) by the end of March 2025, a growth of 9.8% over the GDP in FY24. When converted into US dollar terms (dividing by the dollar-rupee exchange rate of 85.559) for international comparisons, by March - end the size of India's economy was $3.87 trillion.
- ➤ It is noteworthy that the growth of the nominal GDP is less than 10%: at 9.8%, FY25's growth was the third - slowest since the current government took charge in 2014, and the sixth slowest growth rate in nominal GDP since India liberalized its economy in 1991. Table1 also shows how the nominal GDP growth rate has been decelerating. The Compounded Annual Growth Rate (CAGR) since 2014-15 stands at 10.3% while the CAGR since the start of the second term of the NDA government in 2019 stands at 9.8%.

**2. Real GDP & its growth**
- ➤ While the size of the economy uses nominal GDP data, international comparisons of growth rate are done based on the growth rate of real GDP. This is because inflation differs from country to country, and only real GDP provides a genuine understanding about how many actual new goods and services were produced in a particular year.
- ➤ India's real GDP grew by 6.5% in FY25 to reach a level of Rs. 188 trillion. The deceleration in the pace of real GDP growth compared to FY24, when the growth rate was 9.2% - is even more stark than in the case of nominal GDP growth. The gap between the real and nominal GDP shows the effect of inflation in prices of goods and services.
- ➤ The CAGR of real GDP now stands at just above 5% since 2019. India's economy has lost its growth momentum over the past decade, with the CAGR being just above 6% since 2014.

**3. GVA & Sectoral health of economy**
- ➤ The real GVA across the three main sectors of the Indian economy:
- ➤ Agriculture and allied activities (such as forestry, etc.);
- ➤ Industry (including sub-sectors such as manufacturing, construction etc.); and
- ➤ Services (including fields like financial services, trade and hotels etc.)
- ➤ For FY25, the real GVA grew by 6.4%, losing a step over the 8.6% growth in FY24. But notably, none of the sectors have grown at a CAGR anywhere close to 6% since 2019-20.

### [Lucent book p.252 / PDF 264 — top-left only]

**4. Manufacturing growth and Agriculture**
- ➤ Since 2019-20, manufacturing GVA has registered a slower growth rate (CAGR of 4.04%) than even agriculture and allied activities (4.72%). This explains, to some extent, the high urban - in particular, youth - unemployment in India. It also provides an understanding of why labour has been moving back to Indian villages, and joining agriculture and allied activities.
  *[Source : IE, May 31, 2025]*

### [Lucent book p.258 / PDF 270 — right column + table heading]

**5. National Income**
- ➤ National income is the measurement of flow of services and goods in economic system.
- ➤ Comparison between National income with National wealth :The national wealth is the measurement of present assets available on a given time, while the National income is the measurement of the production power of economic system in a given time period.
  NNP at Factor Cost = NNP at market prices – Indirect Taxes + Subsidies = GNP at market prices – Indirect Taxes + Subsidies = National Income.
- ➤ The base of one year is taken for calculating National income, as all the seasons come in a year.
- ➤ The data of estimation of India's National income are issued by Central Statistical Organisation (CSO).

### [Lucent book p.260 / PDF 272 — left column and top of right column]

**FORMULAE**
1. GVA at basic prices (Production Approach) = Output at basic price – Intermediate Consumption.
2. GVA at basic prices (Income Approach) = CE + OS/MI + CFC + Production taxes less Production subsidies^(i)
3. GDP = ΣGVA at basic prices + Product taxes less Product subsidies^(ii)
4. NDP/NNI = GDP/GNI - CFC
5. GNI = GDP + Net primary income from ROW (Receipts less payments)
6. Primary Incomes = CE + Property and Entrepreneurial Income
7. NNDI = NNI + other current transfers^(iii) from ROW, net (Receipts less payments)
8. GNDI = NNDI + CFC = GNI + other current transfers^(iii) from ROW, net (Receipts less payments)
9. Gross Capital Formation^(iv) (Financing Side) = Gross Savings + Net Capital Inflow from ROW
10. GCF (Expenditure Side) = GFCF + CIS + Valuables
11. Gross Disposable Income of Govt. = GFCE + Gross Saving of Central Government
12. Gross Disposable Income (GDI) of Households = GNDI – GDI of Govt. – Gross Savings of All Corporations

**REMARKS on the Formulae**
(i) Production taxes or subsidies are paid or received with relation to production and are independent of the volume of actual production. Some examples are : Production Taxes—Land Revenues, Stamps & Registration fees and Tax on profession; Production Subsidies—Subsidies to Railways, Subsidies to village and small industries.
(ii) Product taxes or subsidies are paid or received on per unit of product. Some examples are : Product Taxes—Goods & Services Tax, Excise duties, Sales tax, Service Tax and Import, Export duties; Product Subsidies—Food, Petroleum and fertilizer subsidies
(iii) Other Current Transfers refers to current transfers other than the primary incomes.
(iv) Gross Capital Formation (GCF) at the current as well as the constant prices is estimated by two approaches :— (i) through flow of funds, derived as Gross Saving plus net capital flow from Rest of the World (RoW); and (ii) by the commodity flow approach, derived by the type of assets.

**Gross Domestic Product (GDP) Price Deflator** (top of right column)
- ➤ The Gross Domestic Product (GDP) price deflator is a formula that measures the amount that the real value of an economy's total output is reduced by inflation. The GDP deflator formula takes into account the value of all final goods including exports. It does not factor in the prices of imports. The following formula calculates the GDP price deflator.
  GDP Price Deflator = (Nominal GDP ÷ Real GDP) × 100

(Recorded while transcribing; verify against Part A/B text before relying.)
