# ECONOMY RAW EXTRACTION — Budget, Fiscal System, Taxation and GST

Sources: Parmar SSC Fatman (2nd Ed. 2026), Economics; Lucent's General Knowledge (2026), Ch.06 Indian Economy. Verbatim transcription with typos preserved; ⚠ marks doubts. No test cross-referencing and no ★-marking at this stage.

Order: Part A (Parmar) then Part B (Lucent) then Part C (cross-book comparison).

---

## PART A — PARMAR SSC FATMAN (2nd Ed. 2026): Economics Ch.4 "BUDGET AND TAXATION" (book pp.261–265, PDF pp.282–286)

Running header "BUDGET AND TAXATION". Chapter ends with ■■■ at bottom of left column, p.265 (right column blank apart from faint show-through of the next chapter's page — not book text).

### [book p.261 / PDF 282]

**Left column — box titled "BUDGET"**

**Budget**
- •It is an approximation of revenue and expenses over a specified period of time.
- •*Article 112* of the Constitution of India requires the government to present an *Annual Financial Statement (an estimated statement of Receipt and Expenditure)* to the Parliament.
- •The *President of India* is responsible for presenting the Annual Financial Statement to both houses of Parliament.
- •Prepared by: *Department of Economic Affairs (Ministry of Finance)*
- •Presented by: *Finance Minister*
- •The *first Budget* was presented by *R K Shanmukham Chetty* and the *second budget* was presented by *John Mathai.*

**Most Number of Budget**
1. Morarji Desai → 10 budgets (Record holder) 6 times in a row
2. P. Chidambaram → 9 budgets
3. Nirmala Sitharaman → 9 budgets (including 9 consecutive budgets)
4. Pranab Mukherjee → 8 budgets
5. Yashwant Sinha → 8 budgets

[box] Gender Sensitive Budget: 2005-06
Indira Gandhi is the 1st female Finance Minister of India (temporary)
[box] Child Budgeting was introduced by Govt in: 2008-09

**Railway and General Budget**
- •In *1921,* the *Acworth Committee* recommended *separating the Railway Budget from the General Budget.* In *2016,* based on the recommendations of the *Bibek Debroy Committee,* the Indian government decided to *merge the Railway Budget with the General Budget.* This merger was implemented from the *2017-18 financial year.*

**Functions of Budget**
*1. Allocation Functions*
- •It refers to the process of *dividing resources between public goods and private goods,* essentially deciding which social services and infrastructure projects to fund.

**Right column**

*2. Redistribution Functions*
- •It aims to *reduce income inequality* by using *progressive taxation* (higher tax rates for higher earners) and providing social welfare programs like unemployment benefits.
*3. Stabilisation Functions*
- •It is aimed at maintaining economic stability by adjusting government spending and taxation to counter recession (by increasing spending) or inflation (by decreasing spending).

[box] **Asset** — An asset is a *resource with economic value* that an individual, a company, or a country owns or controls with the expectation that it will provide a future benefit. E.g. Gold, Property.
[box] **Liability** — A liability represents the obligation or responsibility to fulfill a debt or duty to another party. E.g. Payment of loan.

**Box heading: REVENUE AND CAPITAL**
- •Budget is divided into two heads: *Revenue* and *Capital.*

**Revenue**
- •Revenue refers to the funds that a government collects to *finance its activities and public services.* This primarily comes from taxes, as well as other sources like fees, fines, and duties.
[Tree diagram: Budget → Revenue {Receipt, Expenditure} and Capital {Receipt, Expenditure}]
- •It is of two types: *Revenue Receipts and Revenue Expenditure*

**Revenue Receipts**
- •These are earnings that *do not create liabilities or reduce assets.*
- •Revenue Receipts *do not lead to claim on government* and are of *non-redeemable in nature.*

**Sub-types**
1. *Tax Revenue:* The income that is collected by governments through taxation. Eg. Wealth tax.
2. *Non-Tax Revenue:* It is the recurring income that is earned from sources other than taxes by the government. E.g. Interest on loans, PSU earnings, railway tickets.

### [book p.262 / PDF 283]

**Left column**

**Revenue Expenditure**
- •It is the amount of money spent that *does not lead to increase in assets nor decrease in liabilities.*

**Sub-Types**
1. *Plan Revenue Expenditure:* It is a government expenditure that is part of the *plan budget and does not create assets or liabilities.* E.g. *Government grants/financial assistance.*
2. *Non-Plan Revenue Expenditure:* It is the *money a government spends on its day-to-day operations, excluding capital expenditures.* Eg. *Salary/pension, interest payment (largest component of non-plan expenditure), maintenance of infrastructure.*

**Capital**
- •It is the physical or financial resources that is used to produce value in an economy.
- •It is of two types: *Capital Receipts and Capital Expenditures.*

**Sub-Types**
1. *Capital Receipt:* They are those receipts which *either create liabilities or reduce the asset value* of the government. It creates new liabilities and reduces an existing asset. E.g. Loan, disinvestment.
2. *Capital Expenditure:* It refers to the funds that organizations or governments allocate to acquire, upgrade, or maintain long-term assets such as property, infrastructure, or equipment. *It creates new assets for government and reduces liabilities.* E.g. *Loan repayment, developmental expenditure, infrastructure creation (roads, schools, hospitals).*

**Fiscal Consolidation**
- •Central Government will target reaching a debt-to-GDP ratio of 50±1 % by 2030.
- •The debt-to-GDP ratio is estimated to be 55.6 % of GDP in BE 2026-27, compared to 56.1 % of GDP in RE 2025-26.
- •In RE 2025-26, the fiscal deficit has been estimated at par with BE of 2025-26 at 4.4 % of GDP. In line with the new fiscal prudence path of debt consolidation, the fiscal deficit in BE 2026-27 is estimated to be 4.3 % of GDP.
- *Kelkar committee (2002-03):* recommended the simplification of direct taxes in post-liberalisation India
- *Kelkar Committee (2015):* recommended improving the PPP model to enhance efficiency and transparency in infrastructure projects
- *The Raja Chelliah Committee (1991)* was set up to reform India's tax system. It recommended reducing personal (continues right column)

**Right column**

income tax and corporate tax rates, widening the tax base by removing unnecessary exemptions, simplifying the tax structure, expanding the MODVAT scheme to avoid cascading of taxes, and gradually reducing high customs duties to make the tax system more efficient and growth-oriented.

**Taxation System in India**
*Direct Tax*
- •These taxes are *directly* payable to the government.
- •*It cannot be passed onto someone else.*
- •Direct taxes are *progressive* in nature.
- •Here, the *marginal tax rate is greater than the average tax rate.*
E.g. Income tax, wealth tax, gift tax, capital gain tax, corporate tax.
[photo captioned "Someone is Blushing!!" — meme]

*Indirect Tax*
- •These are *indirectly* payable to the government.
- •*It can be passed onto someone else.*
- •Indirect taxes are *regressive/proportional* in nature.
E.g. GST, Excise Duty (petrol, diesel, aviation turbine fuel), Custom Duty.

*Paper Tax*
- •Tax which carry their *significance only on paper* and have *no significance in terms of revenue yield* are called paper taxes.
E.g. Wealth tax, gift tax, estate duty (not levied now)

*Laffer's Curve*
- •The Laffer curve is a graph that shows the relationship between the *tax rates and government revenue.*
- •The curve suggests that *increasing taxes beyond a certain point can actually decrease the revenue.*
- •This is because higher taxes can discourage investment and consumption, and workers may work less if they believe their extra efforts result in lower income.
[Graph "Laffer's Curve": y-axis Tax Revenue, x-axis Tax Rate (%); "Bell-shaped curve"]
[photo of the Finance Minister with small meme caption (Hinglish, partly illegible at this resolution — something like "Laffer Gaya Toh Lane Hain … Lagaenge") — not book content]

### [book p.263 / PDF 284]

**Left column**

**Goods and Services Tax (GST)**
- •It was introduced through *101st Constitutional Amendment Act.*
- •The tax came into effect by: *1st July 2017.*
- •According to *Art 279A,* GST council is a *constitutional body* which consists of *33 members (2 from Centre +31 from States/UTs).* The Chairman of the GST Council is the *Finance Minister.*

*Cascading Tax (Tax on Tax):* Cascading tax refers to a situation where tax is levied on a product at every stage of production or distribution without giving credit for the tax already paid, resulting in tax being charged on previously paid tax, which increases the final price of the product.

**Other Taxes**
- •*Surcharge:* An *additional tax on income tax* for taxpayers above a certain income threshold.
- •*Cess:* A *tax on tax liability* that is levied for a specific purpose → *Surcharge* and *Cess* are *levied by Central Government but not shared with the states.*
- •*Pigouvian Tax:* A *tax on activities that cause negative externalities,* or external costs that are not included in the market price. It is levied to lower the negative externalities.

**"Rupee Comes From" (doughnut chart) — As Per Budget 2025-2026 (caption printed under the second chart)**
| Source | Share |
|---|---|
| Borrowing and Other Liabilities | 24% |
| Income Tax | 22% |
| GST & other taxes | 18% |
| Corporation tax | 17% |
| Non-Tax Receipts | 9% |
| Union Excise Duties | 5% |
| Customs | 4% |
| Non-Debt Capital Receipts | 1% |
(sum = 100%)

**"Rupee Goes To" (doughnut chart)**
| Head | Share |
|---|---|

| Interest payments | 20% |
| Central sector scheme, (excluding capital outlay on defence and subsidy) | 16% |
| Defence | 8% |
| Finance commission and other transfer | 8% |
| Central sponsored scheme | 8% |
| Other expenditure | 8% |
| Subsidies | 6% |
| Pensions | 4% |
(sum = 100%)

**Right column**

**WMA,1997 (Ways and Means Advances) Agreement**
As per the agreement, the Central Government would meet temporary mismatches between receipts and expenditure through Ways and Means Advances (WMA) provided by the RBI.
*Purpose:* To manage temporary shortages in government cash flow, not a permanent source of finance.

**Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management Act, 2003 (FRBM)**
- •The Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management (FRBM) Act was enacted to ensure fiscal discipline and responsible financial management by the Government of India.
- •The FRBM Act was passed in *2003* to reduce fiscal deficit and improve transparency in government finances. It aimed to limit fiscal deficit to around *3% of GDP* and promote prudent management of public debt.

*FRBM Review Committee*
- • It was formed on *May 2016* and headed by *NK Singh.* He gave few recommendation as below
- •Debt-GDP Target: Total public debt should be 60% of GDP (40% for Centre + 20% for States).
- •Fiscal Deficit Target: Reduce fiscal deficit to 2.5% of GDP in the medium term by 2023
- •Escape Clause: Government can deviate from fiscal deficit target by 0.5% of GDP in exceptional situations like war, national security issues, natural disasters, or economic slowdown by 2023
- •Fiscal Council: Recommended setting up an independent Fiscal Council to monitor fiscal discipline
1. *Medium Term Fiscal Policy Statement:* This statement outlines a *3-year rolling target for key fiscal indicators,* such as revenue and fiscal deficits etc.
2. *Fiscal Policy Strategy Statement:* This outlines the government's *financial goals* and the methods it plans to use to achieve them.
3. *Macroeconomic Framework Statement:* This provides an *overview of the country's economic situation,* including details like the GDP growth rate.

**Table 1: Deficit and debt targets (% of GDP)**
| Year | Fiscal Deficit | Revenue Deficit | Debt |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2017–18 | 3.0% | 2.1% | 47.3% |
| 2018–19 | 3.0% | 1.8% | 45.5% |
| 2019–20 | 3.0% | 1.6% | 43.7% |
| 2020–21 | 2.8% | 1.3% | 42.0% |
| 2021–22 | 2.6% | 1.1% | 40.3% |
| 2022–23 | 2.5% | 0.8% | 38.7% |

### [book p.264 / PDF 285]

**Left column**

**Crowding In:**
Crowding in occurs when government spending (especially on infrastructure and public investment) encourages and stimulates private sector investment, leading to higher overall economic activity.

**Crowding Out:**
Crowding out occurs when increased government borrowing or expenditure raises interest rates and reduces private sector investment, as the government competes with the private sector for available funds.

**A Budget can be classified into three types:**
1. *Balanced Budget:* It is a condition where the total revenues are equivalent to the total expenditure *(Receipt = Expenditure).*
2. *Surplus Budget:* It is a condition when total income or receipts exceeds its total expenditures *(Receipt > Expenditure).*
3. *Deficit Budget:* It is a condition when expenses exceed the revenue *(Expenditure > Receipt).*

**Deficit Financing (By J.M Keynes)**
Deficit financing refers to a situation where government expenditure exceeds its revenue and the gap is financed by creating new money or by borrowing funds.

**Methods of Deficit Financing:**
*Debt Financing:*
It refers to raising funds through borrowing, where the government or a firm takes loans from banks, financial institutions, or issues bonds and the amount must be repaid with interest.
*Equity Financing:*
It refers to raising funds by issuing shares, where investors become owners (shareholders) of the company and share in profits through dividends, without any obligation to repay the capital.

**Deficit**
- •A deficit is when the expenses exceed revenue.

**Types of Deficit**
1. *Budgetary Deficit:* It occurs when government's total expenditures exceed its *total revenues from taxes and other sources.* This indicates that the government is spending more than it is earning, leading to borrowing.
   [box] Budgetary Deficit = Total expenditure - Total Receipt
2. *Revenue Deficit:* It occurs when a *government's revenue expenditure exceeds its revenue receipts.*
   [box] Revenue Deficit = Revenue Expenditure - Revenue Receipt

**Right column**

3. *Effective Revenue Deficit:* It is the difference between *revenue deficit and grants used to create capital assets.*
   [box] Effective Revenue Deficit = Revenue Deficit - Grant given for the developmental purpose
4. *Fiscal Deficit:* It represents the total borrowing requirement of government. It occurs when *total government expenditure exceeds the sum of total receipts (excluding borrowings).*
   [box] Fiscal Deficit = Total Exp - [Total receipt-Debt creating Capital Receipt) (Loans/Borrowing)
   OR
   [box] Total expenditure - (RR+Non debt Capital Receipt)
5. *Primary Deficit:* It is defined as the difference between the *fiscal deficit and the previous year interest payments* of the government.
   [box] Primary Deficit = Fiscal Deficit - Interest Payment
[box] Externalities: An externality is an indirect cost or benefit to an uninvolved third party that arises as an effect of another party's activity. It can be Positive or Negative.

**16th Finance Commission**
The Finance Commission is a constitutional body under *Article 280* of the Constitution of India, constituted by the President every *5 years* to recommend the distribution of tax revenues between the *Centre and the States.*
The *16th Finance Commission* was constituted on *31 December 2023* under *Article 280(1)* of the Constitution. The Chairman is *Arvind Panagariya* and the commission consists of *1 Chairman and 4 Members.*

*Its functions include recommending:*
- •*Vertical Devolution* – It refers to the share of the total divisible pool of central taxes given to all states collectively. The Government has accepted the recommendation of the Finance Commission to retain the vertical share of devolution at *41%.*
- •*Horizontal Devolution* – It determines how that *41%* share is distributed among individual states. It is determined by *Income Distance (42.5%), Population (17.5%), Area (10%), Forest and Ecology (10%), Demographic Performance (10%)* & a *10%* weight for contribution to *GDP* (added through 16th Finance Commission)
- •Grants- in-aid to states from the Consolidated Fund of India under *Article 275.*
- •Measures to improve finances of Panchayats and Municipalities.

### [book p.265 / PDF 286]

**Left column**

**Fiscal Matter (Budget context):**

**Table 2: Criteria for distribution of central taxes among states**
| Criteria | 15th FC (2021-26) | 16th FC (2026-31) |
|---|---|---|
| Income Distance | 45% | 42.5% |
| Population (2011) | 15% | 17.5% |
| Demographic Performance | 12.5% | 10% |
| Area | 15% | 10% |
| Forest | 10% | 10% |
| Tax and Fiscal Efforts | 2.5% | - |
| Contribution to GDP | - | 10% |
| Total | 100% | 100% |
Sources: Reports of the 15th and 16th Finance Commissions; PRS.

A provision of ₹1.34 lakh crore has been made to the states for *FY 2026-27* as Finance Commission grants, including Rural & Urban Local Body grants and Disaster Management grants.

[box] **Operation Twist** — Operation Twist is a monetary policy tool used by the Reserve Bank of India in which it simultaneously buys long-term government securities and sells short-term securities to reduce long-term interest rates and manage the yield curve. When RBI buys long-term bonds, their prices increase and yields fall, making long-term loans cheaper.

## PART B — LUCENT'S GK (2026), Ch.06 "Indian Economy", §10 "Indian Fiscal System" (Lucent book pp.270–274)

Page mapping: PDF = book + 12 for book pp.250–272 (PDF 282 = book 270; PDF 283 = 271; PDF 284 = 272). **An unnumbered ad page (UPSC-books PDF advertisement, no book text) sits at PDF 285**, so from book p.273 onward PDF = book + 13 (PDF 286 = book 273; PDF 287 = book 274; PDF 288 = 275; PDF 289 = 276). Section starts in the left column of p.270 (after the HCES summary, end of §9) and ends in the right column of p.274 just above "11. Money, Banking and Insurance in India".

**Cross-book note:** Parmar Ch.4 (Budget, deficits, taxation, FRBM, Finance Commission tables, Budget 2025-26 "rupee comes from/goes to") is in `partA_ch04_budget_taxation.md`; Ch.7's fiscal-policy box is in `partA_ch07_monetary_policy.md`. Lucent's Budget 2025-26 summary, tax slabs and Finance Commission text are time-sensitive.

### [Lucent book p.270 / PDF 282 — left column (from "10.") and right column]

**Left column**

**10. Indian Fiscal System**
- ➤ *Fiscal System :* It refers to the management of revenue and capital expenditure finances by the state. Hence, fiscal system includes budgetary activities of the government that is revenue raising, borrowing and spending activities.
- ➤ *Fiscal Policy :* Fiscal Policy refers to the use of taxation, public expenditure and the management of public debt in order to achieve certain specified objectives.
- ➤ Indian Fiscal System includes or refers to the management of revenue sources and expenditure of the Central and State governments, Public debt, Deficit financing, Budget, Tax structure etc.
- ➤ *Sources of Revenue for Centre :* The revenue of the Central Government consists of the following elements: 1. Tax revenue and 2. Non-tax revenue. Tax revenue comes broadly from three sources—(a) taxes on income and expenditure (b) taxes on property and capital transactions and (c) taxes on commodities and services. Non-tax revenue, consists of—(a) currency, coinage and mint-(b) interest receipts and dividends and other non-tax revenue.
- ➤ *Sources of Revenue for State :* The main sources are (a) state tax revenue, (b) share in central taxes, (c) income from social, commercial and economic service and profits of state-run enterprises. State tax revenue includes among others, land revenue, stamp, registration and estate duty etc.
- ➤ *Expenditure of the Centre :* The central government makes expenditure broadly under two heads : 1. Plan expenditure and 2. Non-plan expenditure.
- ➤ Under Plan expenditure comes outlay for agriculture, rural development, irrigation and flood control, energy, industry and minerals, transport, communications, Science and Technology, environment and economic services etc.
- ➤ The major non-plan expenditures are interest payments, defence, subsidies and general services.
- ➤ *Expenditure of State :* Like the Union Government, the State Governments too have two broad heads of expenditure : (a) Non-Development Expenditure and (b) Development Expenditure.
- ➤ Public debt of the government of India is of two kinds–Internal and External.
- ➤ *Internal debt :* It comprises loans raised from the open market, compensation bonds, prize bonds etc., treasury bills issued to the RBI, commercial banks etc.

**Right column**
- ➤ *External debt :* It consists of loans taken from World Bank, IMF, ADB and individual countries like USA, Japan etc.
- ➤ *Deficit Financing* is a fiscal tool in the hands of the government to bridge the gap between revenue receipt and revenue expenditure.

**Deficits**
- ➤ In a budget statement, there is a mention of four types of deficits : 1. revenue, 2. budget, 3. fiscal and 4. primary.
1. *Revenue Deficit* refers to the excess of revenue expenditure over revenue receipts. [In fact, it reflects one crucial fact : what is the government borrowing for ? As an individual if you are borrowing to play the house rent, then you are in a situation of revenue deficit, i.e. while you are borrowing and spending, you are not creating any durable asset. This implies that there will be a repayment obligation (sometime in the future) and at the same time there is no asset creation via investment.]
   Revenue Deficit = Total Revenue Expenditure – Total Revenue Receipts = Non-plan Expenditure + Plan Expenditure – (net tax revenue + non-tax revenue)
- ➤ A revenue deficit occurs when realized net income is less than the projected net income. This happens when the actual amount of revenue and the actual amount of expenditures do not correspond with budgeted revenue and expenditures.
2. *Budget Deficit* refers to the excess of total expenditure over total receipts. Here, total receipts include current revenue and net internal and external capital receipts, of the government.
   Budget Deficit = Total Expenditure – Total Receipts = (non-plan expenditure + plan expenditure) – (Revenue Receipts + Capital Receipts)
3. *Fiscal Deficit* refers to the difference between total expenditure *(revenue, capital and loans net of repayment)* on one hand, and on the other hand, revenue receipts plus all those capital receipts which are not in the form of borrowings but which in the end accrue to the government.
   Fiscal Deficit = Revenue Receipts *(net tax revenue + non-tax revenue)* + Capital Receipts *(only recoveries of loans and other receipts)* – Total Expenditure *(plan and non-plan)*
4. *Primary Deficit* refers to fiscal deficit minus interest payments. In other words, it points to how much the government is borrowing to pay for expenses other than interest payments. Also, it underscores another key fact : how much the government is adding to future burden *(in terms of repayment)* on the basis of past and present policy.
   Primary Deficit = Revenue Deficit – Interest Payments [⚠ as printed]
   Monetised Deficit = Increment in Net RBI Credit to the Central Government.

**Budget**
- ➤ The Budget of the Government of India, for any year, gives a complete picture of the estimated receipts and expenditures of the Government for that year on the basis of the budget figures of the two previous years. [continues p.271]

### [Lucent book p.271 / PDF 283]

**Left column**
- ➤ Every budget, for instance, gives three sets of figures : (a) actual figures for preceding year, (b) budget and revised figures for the current year and (c) budget estimates for the following year.
- ➤ The core of the budget is called the *Annual financial statement.* This is the main budget document. *Under article 112 of the constitution,* a statement of estimated receipts and expenditure of the Govt. of India has to be laid before the parliament in respect of every financial year or fiscal year (FY) running from April 1 to March 31 while *under article 202* of the constitution a statement of estimated receipts and expenditures of the state Governments has to be laid before the house of the state legislature concerned.
- ➤ The Annual Financial Statement is the main Budget document which shows the receipts and payments of Government under the three parts in which Government accounts are kept: (a) Consolidated Fund, (b) Contingency Fund and (c) Public Account.
- ➤ All revenues received by Government, loans raised by it, and also its receipts from recoveries of loans granted by it, form the consolidated Fund. All expenditure of Government is incurred from the Consolidated Fund and no amount can be withdrawn from the Fund without authorisation from Parliament.
- ➤ Occasions may arise when Government may have to meet urgent unforeseen expenditure pending authorisation from Parliament. The *Contingency Fund* is an imprest placed at the disposal of the President to incur such expenditure.
- ➤ Parliamentary approval for such expenditure and for withdrawal of an equivalent amount from the Consolidated Fund is subsequently obtained and the amount spent from Contingency Fund is subsequently recouped to the Fund. According to 'India 2023', the corpus of the Fund authorised by the Parliament, from April, 2021 is ₹ 30,000 crore.
- ➤ Besides the normal receipts and expenditure of Government which relate to the Consolidated Fund, certain other transactions enter Government accounts, in respect of which, Government acts more as a banker, for example, transactions relating to provident funds, small savings collections and other deposits, etc. The moneys thus received are kept in the Public Account and the connected disbursements are also made therefrom. Parliamentary authorisation for such payments from the Public Account is, therefore, not required.
- ➤ Under the Constitution, Budget has to distinguish expenditure on revenue account from other expenditure. Government Budget, therefore, comprises (a) Revenue Budget and (b) Capital Budget.
- ➤ The *Annual Budget* of the Central Government provides estimates of receipts and expenditures of the Government. The Budget consists of two parts viz; 1. Revenue Budget 2. Capital Budget.
- ★ *Revenue Budget :* All 'current receipts' such as taxation, surplus of Public enterprises, and 'expenditures' of the Government.
- ★ *Capital Budget :* All 'Capital receipts' and 'expenditure' such as domestic and foreign loans, loan repayments, foreign aid etc.

**Right column**

**Summary of Union Budget 2025-26** [pink heading; partly obscured in scan]
- ➤ No income tax on average monthly income of upto Rs. 1 lakh; to boost middle class household savings & consumption.
- ➤ Salaried class to pay nil income tax upto 12.75 lakh per annum in new tax regime.
- ➤ Union Budget recognises 4 engines of development Agriculture, MSME, Investment and Exports.
- ➤ Loans upto RS. 5 lakhs through KCC under modified interest subvention scheme.
- ➤ FY-25 estimated to end with fiscal deficit of 4.8%, target to bring it down to 4.4% in FY-26.
- ➤ Significant enhancement of credit with guarantee cover to MSMEs from 5 cr to 10 cr.
- ➤ A national manufacturing mission covering small, medium and large industries for furthering "Make In India".
- ➤ 50,000 Atal Tinkering Labs in government schools in next 5 years.
- ➤ Centre of excellence in Artificial Intelligence for education, with a total outlay of 500 crore.
- ➤ PM Svanidhi with enhanced loans from banks, and UPI linked credit cards with 30,000 limit.
- ➤ GIG workers to get identity cards, registration on E. Shram Portal & Healthcare under PM Jan Arogya Yojana.
- ➤ 1 lakh crore urban challenge fund for 'Cities As Growth Hubs'.
- ➤ Nuclear energy mission for r&d of small modular reactors with an outlay of 20,000 crore.
- ➤ Modified UDAN scheme to enhance regional connectivity to 120 new destinations.
- ➤ Gyan Bharatam Mission for survey and conservation of manuscripts to cover more than one crore manuscripts.
- ➤ FDI limit enhanced for Insurance from 74 to 100 %.
- ➤ Jan Vishwas Bill 2.0 to be introduced for decriminalising more than 100 provisions in various laws.
- ➤ Updated Income Tax Returns time limit increased from two to four years.
- ➤ Delay in TCS payment decriminalized.
- ➤ TDS on rent increased from 2.4 lakh to 6 lakh.
- ➤ BCD exempted on 36 lifesaving drugs and medicines for treating cancer, rare and chronic diseases.
- ➤ BCD on IFPD increased to 20% and on open cells reduced to 5%.
- ➤ BCD on parts of open cells exempted to promote domestic manufacturing.
- ➤ To boost battery production, additional capital goods for EV and mobile battery manufacturing exempted.
- ➤ BCD exempted for 10 years on raw materials & components used for ship building.
- ➤ BCD reduced from 30% to 5% on frozen fish paste and 15% to 5% on fish Hydrolysate.
- ➤ The Finance Minister presented the Union Budget 2025-26 with the theme "Sabka Vikas" stimulating balanced growth of all regions.
- ➤ Union Budget highlights that Agriculture, MSME, Investment, and Exports are four engines in the journey to Viksit Bharat using reforms as fuel, guided by the spirit of inclusivity. [continues p.272]

### [Lucent book p.272 / PDF 284]

**Left column**

**Financial Sector Reforms and Development**
- ➤ The Union Finance Minister proposed to raise the Foreign Direct Investment (FDI) limit for the insurance from 74 to 100%, to be available for those companies that invest the entire premium in India.
- [heading partly clipped: "…onsolidation" — presumably "Fiscal Consolidation"] ➤ Reiterating the commitment to stay the course for fiscal consolidation, the Union Finance Minister stated that the Government endeavours to keep the fiscal deficit each year such that the Central Government debt remains on a declining path as a %age of the GDP and the detailed roadmap for the next 6 years has been detailed in the FRBM statement. The Revised Estimate 2024-25 of fiscal deficit is 4.8 % of GDP, while the Budget Estimates 2025-26 is estimated to be 4.4 % of GDP.

**Revised Estimates 2024-25**
- ➤ The Finance Minister said that the Revised Estimate of the total receipts other than borrowings is ₹ 31.47 lakh crore, of which the net tax receipts are ₹ 25.57 lakh crore. She added that the Revised Estimate of the total expenditure is ₹ 47.16 lakh crore, of which the capital expenditure is about ₹ 10.18 lakh crore.

**Budget Estimates 2025-26**
- ➤ For FY 2025-26, the total receipts other than borrowings and the total expenditure are estimated at ₹ 34.96 lakh crore and ₹ 50.65 lakh crore respectively. The net tax receipts are estimated at ₹ 28.37 lakh crore.

**Revised Tax Rates**
- ➤ Reposing faith on middle class in nation building, the Union Budget 2025-26 proposes new direct tax slabs and rates under the new income tax regime so that no income tax is needed to be paid for total income upto ₹ 12 Lakh per annum, i.e. average income of Rs 1 Lakh per month, other than special rate income such as Capital Gain. Salaried individuals earning upto ₹ 12.75 Lakh per annum will pay NIL tax, due to standard deduction of ₹ 75,000. Towards the new tax structure and other direct tax proposals, Government is set to lose revenue of about ₹ 1 lakh crore.
- ➤ The Budget proposes revised tax rate structure under the new tax regime as follows :

| Total Income per annum | Rate of Tax |
|---|---|
| ₹ 0-4 Lakh | NIL |
| ₹ 4-8 Lakh | 5% |
| ₹ 8-12 Lakh | 10% |
| ₹ 12-16 Lakh | 15% |
| ₹ 16-20 Lakh | 20% |
| ₹ 20-24 Lakh | 25% |
| Above ₹ 24 Lakh | 30% |

- ➤ To rationalize TDS/TCS, Budget doubles limit for tax deduction on interest earned by senior citizens from the present ₹ 50,000 to ₹ 1 Lakh. Further, TDS threshold on rent has been increased to ₹ 6 Lakh from ₹ 2.4 Lakh per annum. After the decriminalization of delay in payment of TDS, delay in TCS payments has now been decriminalized.
- ➤ As relief on import of Drugs/Medicines, 36 lifesaving drugs and medicines for treating cancer, rare diseases and chronic diseases have been fully exempted from Basic Customs Duty (BCD).

**Right column**
- ➤ The Budget 2025-26 fully exempts cobalt powder and waste, scrap of lithium-ion battery, Lead, Zinc and 12 more critical minerals. To promote domestic textile production, two more types of shuttle-less looms added to fully exempted textile machinery. [⚠ right edge slightly clipped]

**Reforms in Budget 2017-18** [pink box]
- ➤ The Budget for 2017-18 contained three major reforms. *First,* the presentation of the Budget was advanced to 1st February to enable the Parliament to avoid a Vote on Account and pass a single Appropriation Bill for 2017-18, before the close of the current financial year. This enabled the ministries and departments to operationalise all schemes and projects, including the new schemes, right from the commencement of the next financial year.
- ➤ *Second,* the merger of the Railways Budget with the General Budget was a historic step. The colonial practice prevalent since 1924 was discontinued. This decision brought the Railways to the centre stage of Government's fiscal policy and facilitated multi modal transport planning between railways, highways and inland waterways.
- ➤ *Third,* the plan and non-plan classification of expenditure has been done away with. This will give a holistic view of allocations for sectors and ministries and would facilitate optimal allocation of resources.   *[Source : INDIA 2018]*

**Finance**
- ➤ The Ministry of Finance is responsible for administration of finances of the government. It is concerned with all economic and financial matters affecting the country as a whole, including mobilisation of resources for development and other purposes. It regulates expenditure of the government including transfer of resources to the states.

**Department of Economic Affairs**
- ➤ The Department of Economic Affairs is the nodal agency of the government to formulate and monitor country's economic policies and programmes having a bearing on domestic and international aspects of economic management. A principal responsibility of this Department is the preparation and presentation of the Union Budget (including Railway Budget) to the Parliament and the Budget for the state governments under President's Rule and union territory administrations.

**Finance Commission**
- ➤ According to BHARAT 2025, Finance Commission Division (FCD), Department of Expenditure undertakes processing of and follow-up action on the various recommendations of the Central Finance Commission including release of grants recommended by the successive Central Finance Commissions.
- ➤ Based on the recommendations of the 15th Finance Commission for the award period 2021-22 to 2025-26, funds amounting to ₹ 1,49,570.41 crore were released out of ₹ 1,79,140.00 crore allocated by the Commission for 2023-24. In addition, owing to severe natural calamities, additional financial assistance amounting to ₹ 1,048.50 crore was also released from National Disaster Response Fund.   *[Source : BHARAT 2025]*
- ➤ Finance Commission is a constitutional body constituted by the President under Art. 280 of the constitution. Since Independence, 15 Finance Commissions have submitted their reports. [continues p.273]

- ➤ XV FC grants are provided in two parts, namely, (i) Basic (United) Grant and (ii) Tied Grant. The basic grants are untied and can be used by Rural Local Bodies (RLBs) for location-specific felt needs, under the twenty nine subjects enshrined in the eleventh Schedule of the Constitution, except for salaries and other establishment costs. The tied grants are to be used for the basic services of (a) sanitation and maintenance of ODF status, and this should include management and treatment of household waste, and human excreta and faecal sludge management in particular and (b) supply of drinking water, rainwater harvesting and water recycling.

These grants are to be used to create diagnostic infrastructure to the primary health care facilities of sub-centres and primary health centres, establishing block level public health units, grants for buildlosing [⚠ "buildings"] of sub-centres, primary health centres and community health centres and conversion of rural primary health centres and sub-centres into Health and Wellness Centres.   *[Source : INDIA 2024]*

**Gender and Child Budgeting**
- ➤ From 2005-06, a separate Statement No. 13 on Gender Budget was included. It provided allocations for women specific programmes. It indicated provision for educational outlays, provisions for child protection, etc.

As the Indian concept of welfare transforms into empowerment; India is transitioning from women's development to women-led development, highlights the Economic Survey 2023-24. The budget allocated for children in the Union Budget for 2024-25 is ₹ 1,09,921 crore, which is 2.28 % of the total Budget. This is a 5.51 % increase from the previous year's budget of ₹ 1,04,180 crore.   *[Source : BHARAT 2025]*

**Demands for Grants**
- ➤ The estimates of expenditure from the Consolidated Fund included in the Annual Financial Statement and required to be voted by the Lok Sabha are submitted in the form of Demands for Grants in pursuance of Article 113 of the Constitution.
- ➤ The Demands for Grants are presented to the Lok Sabha along with the Annual Financial Statement. [continues p.274]

### [Lucent book p.274 / PDF 287]

**Left column**

**Finance Bill**
- ➤ A Finance Bill is a Money Bill as defined in Article 110 of the Constitution. It is accompanied by a Memorandum explaining the provisions included in it. At the time of presentation of the Annual Financial Statement before Parliament, a Finance Bill is also presented in fulfilment of the requirement of Article 110 (1) (a) of the Constitution, detailing the imposition, abolition, remission, alteration or regulation of taxes proposed in the Budget.

**Appropriation Bills**
- ➤ After the Demands for Grants are voted by the Lok Sabha, Parliament's approval to the withdrawal from the Consolidated Fund of the amounts so voted and of the amount required to meet the expenditure charged on the Consolidated Fund is sought through the Appropriation Bill.
- ➤ Under Article 114(3) of the Constitution, no amount can be withdrawn from the Consolidated Fund without the enactment of such a law by Parliament.

**Sources of Revenue**
- ➤ In accordance with the Constitution (Eightieth Amendment) Act, 2000, which has been given retrospective effect from April 1, 1996, all taxes in the Union List, except the duties and taxes referred to in Articles 268 and 269, respective surcharge on taxes and duties referred to in Article 271 and any cess levied for specific purpose under any law made by parliament, shall be levied and collected by the Government of India and shall be distributed between the Union and the states in such manner as may be prescribed by the President on the recommendations of the Finance Commission.
- ➤ The main sources of Union Tax revenue are Customs duties, Union Excise duties, Service tax, Corporate and Income Taxes, Non-Tax revenues largely comprise interest receipts, dividends/profits, fines and miscellaneous receipts collected in the exercise of sovereign functions, regulatory charges and license fees and user charges for publicly provided goods and services.

**Public Debt**
The Public Debt of India is classified into three categories of Union Government liabilities into internal debt, external debt and other liabilities.
- ➤ *Internal debt* for Government of India largely consists of fixed tenure and fixed rate government papers (dated securities and treasury bills) which are issued through auctions.
- ➤ These include market loans (dated securities), treasury bills (91, 182 and 364 days) and 14-day treasury bills (issued to state governments only), cash management bills, special securities issued to the Reserve Bank of India (RBI), compensation and other bonds, non-negotiable and non-interest bearing rupee securities issued to international financial institutions and securities, issued under market stabilization scheme with a view to reduce dependency on physical gold and reduce imports.
- ➤ *External debt* represents loans received from foreign governments and multilateral institutions. The Union

**Right column**
Government does not borrow directly for international capital markets. Its foreign currency borrowing takes place from multilateral agencies and bilateral sources, and is a part of official development assistance (ODA).
- ➤ *Other liabilities* category, not a part of public debt, includes other interest bearing obligations of the government, such as post office saving deposits, deposits under small savings schemes, loans raised through post office cash certificates, provident funds and certain other deposits.
- ➤ The Reserve Bank manages the public debt of the Central and the State Governments and also acts as a banker to them under the provisions of the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934 (Section 20 and 21).
- ➤ The Reserve Bank also undertakes similar functions for the State Governments by agreement with the Government of the respective State (under section 21 A).

*(Then: "11. Money, Banking and Insurance in India" — see §11.)*

### §10 doubts ⚠
- ⚠ **Time-sensitive (Budget 2025-26):** tax slabs (nil up to ₹12 lakh; ₹12.75 lakh for salaried; slab table 0–4/4–8/8–12/12–16/16–20/20–24/24+ at nil/5/10/15/20/25/30%), fiscal deficit 4.8% (FY25 RE) and 4.4% (FY26 BE), RE 2024-25 (receipts ₹31.47 lakh crore; expenditure ₹47.16 lakh crore; capex ₹10.18 lakh crore), BE 2025-26 (₹34.96 / ₹50.65 lakh crore; net tax receipts ₹28.37 lakh crore) — as printed; superseded by later budgets (verify: Budget 2026-27 was presented before today's date).
- ⚠ **Primary deficit formula as printed:** "Primary Deficit = Revenue Deficit – Interest Payments" (p.270) whereas the definition in the same paragraph says "fiscal deficit minus interest payments" — internal inconsistency in the book; kept as printed. Compare Parmar Ch.4 deficits.
- ⚠ **Fiscal-deficit formula (as printed):** "Revenue Receipts + Capital Receipts (only recoveries of loans and other receipts) – Total Expenditure" — the sign/ordering follows the printed line.
- ⚠ **"Reforms in Budget 2017-18"** — Budget advanced to 1 February; Railway Budget merged (colonial practice "since 1924"); plan/non-plan distinction removed — as printed.
- ⚠ **Finance Commission facts:** "15 Finance Commissions have submitted their reports" (p.272) while the 16th is said to be constituted Dec 2023 with Arvind Pangariya as chair (p.273) — as printed; "15th FC for the period 2020-25 was constituted in November 2017 with N.K. Singh" while the same page says "award period 2021-22 to 2025-26" — as printed. Compare Parmar Ch.4 (16th FC tables).
- ⚠ **Duplicate rows in the "Some Financial Institutions" table:** rows 1 and 12 (ICICI, Jan 1955), rows 4 and 16 (NABARD / "National Agriculture and Rural Development Bank", 12 July 1982) — as printed. "Industrial Reconstruction Bank of India — 20 March 1985"; "SIDBI 1990"; "EXIM Bank 1 Jan 1982"; "RRB 2 October 1975"; "LIC Sept 1956"; "NHB July 1988"; "GIC Nov 1, 1972"; "SBI July 1, 1955" — as printed.
- ⚠ **Typos preserved:** "tires" (tiers), "play the house rent" (pay), "Parlimentandthe", "buildlosing" (my reading of "buildings"), "Arvind Pangariya", "consolidation" heading clipped.
- ⚠ **Eightieth Amendment Act, 2000** with retrospective effect from April 1, 1996 — as printed (Articles 268, 269, 271).
- ⚠ **Public debt text** — "compensation and other bonds … securities issued under market stabilization scheme with a view to reduce dependency on physical gold and reduce imports" is printed as one sentence (likely a merge of Gold Monetisation text) — as printed.
- ⚠ **Contingency Fund ₹30,000 crore "from April 2021" (India 2023)** — as printed.

---

## PART B — LUCENT'S GK (2026), Ch.06 "Indian Economy", §12 "Tax System" (incl. GST) (Lucent book pp.280–282)

Page mapping: PDF = book + 13 (PDF 293 = book 280; PDF 294 = 281; PDF 295 = 282). Section starts in the right column of p.280 (below Demonetisation, end of §11) and ends in the left column of p.282 just above "13. Industry".

**Cross-book note:** Parmar Ch.4 (direct/indirect taxes, Laffer curve, GST, FRBM) is in `partA_ch04_budget_taxation.md` — see Part C.

### [Lucent book p.280 / PDF 293 — right column, lower]

**12. Tax System**
- ➤ A compulsory contribution given by a citizen or organisation to the Government is called Tax, which is used for meeting expenses on welfare work.
- ➤ Tax imposing and Tax collecting is at three levels in India– Central level, State level and Local level.
- ➤ The distribution of tax between Centre and State has been clearly mentioned in the provisions of Indian Constitution. For rationalising it from time to time, Finance Commission has been constituted.
- ➤ The tax system has been divided into two parts :
  *Tax by Central Government :* Custom Duty, Income Tax and Corporate Tax etc.
  *Tax by State Government :* The state government has right to collect all the taxes in this category and spend them.
- ➤ There are two types of taxes : 1. Direct Taxes 2. Indirect Taxes.
- ★ *Direct Taxes :* The taxes levied by the central government on incomes and wealth are important direct taxes. The important taxes levied on incomes are—corporation tax and income tax. Taxes levied on wealth are wealth tax, gift tax etc. [continues p.281]

### [Lucent book p.281 / PDF 294]

**Left column**
- ★ *Indirect Taxes :* This type of tax is not paid by someone to the authorities and it is actually passed on to the other in the form of increased cost. They are levied on goods and services produced or purchased. Excise Tax, Sales Tax, Vat, Entertainment Tax are indirect taxes. The main forms of indirect taxes are customs and excise duties and sales tax. The central government is empowered to levy customs and excise duties *(except on alcoholic liquors and narcotics)* whereas sales tax is the exclusive jurisdiction of the state governments.
- ➤ However, the union excise duties form the most significant part of central taxes. The major tax revenue sources for states are their shares in union excise duties and income tax, commercial taxes, land revenue, stamp duty, registration fees, state excise duties on alcohol and narcotics etc. Sales tax forms the most important component of commercial taxes.
- ➤ *Progressive Tax :* A tax that takes away a higher proportion of one's income as the income rises is known as progressive tax. Indian Income Tax is a progressive and direct tax.
- ➤ R. Chelliah Committee was constituted in August, 1991 for suggesting reforms in Tax Structure.
- ➤ Chelliah Committee recommended Income Tax for agricultural income of more than ₹ 25,000 p.a. Chelliah Committee also recommended for lowering down the tax rates and reducing the tax slabs.
- ➤ K.L. Rekhi Committee was constituted in 1992 for suggesting uniform regulations for indirect taxation *(Custom Duty and Excise Duty).*

**Types of Taxes** [pink table]
| Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| Direct Tax | Income Tax, Property Tax, Gift Tax etc. |
| Indirect Tax | Sales Tax, Excise Duty, Custom Duty etc. |
| Taxes imposed by the Central Government | Income Tax, Corporate Tax, Property Tax, Succession Tax, Wealth Tax, Gift Tax, Custom Duty, Tax on agricultural wealth etc. |
| Taxes imposed by the State Government | Land revenue tax, Agricultural income tax, Agricultural Land Revenue, State Excise Duty, Entertainment Tax, Stamp duty, Road Tax, Motor Vehicle Tax etc. |

**Central Board of Direct Taxes**
- ➤ The Central Board of Direct Taxes (CBDT), created by the Central Board of Revenue Act 1963, is the apex body entrusted with the responsibility of administering direct tax laws in India.
- ➤ CBDT is the cadre controlling authority for the Officers of the Indian Revenue Service and controlling authority for the officials of the Income Tax Department (ITD).

**Revenue Collection**
- ➤ During the financial year 2023-24, the net collection of Direct Taxes was at ₹ 19,60,166 crore (Provisional). During the financial year 2024-25 (up to November, 2024) the net collection of direct taxes was ₹ 12,46,229.00 crore, which is about 56.47 % of the Budget Estimates (BE) of ₹ 22,07,000 crore for the financial year 2024-25.   *[Source : BHARAT 2025]*

**Important Taxes Imposed in India**
- ➤ *Tax on Income and Wealth :* The central government imposes different types of tax on income and wealth, viz., income tax, corporate tax, wealth tax and gift tax.

**Right column**
Out of them income tax and corporate tax are more important from the revenue point of view.
- ➤ *Personal Income Tax :* Personal income tax is generally imposed on an individual, combined Hindu families and total income of people of any other communities.
- ➤ In addition to tax, separate surcharges are also imposed some times.
- ➤ Agricultural income in India is free from income tax.
- ➤ *Corporate Tax :* Corporate Tax is imposed on Registered Companies and Corporations.
- ➤ The rate of corporate tax on all companies is equal. However, various types of rebates and exemptions have been provided.
- ➤ *Custom Duties :* As per the Constitutional provisions, the central government imposes import duty and export duty both. Import and Export duties are not only sources of income but with the help of it the central government regulates the foreign trade.
- ➤ *Import Duties :* Generally import duties are ad-velorem in India. It means import duties are imposed on the taxable item on percentage basis.
- ➤ *Export Duties :* Export Duties are more important, compared to Import Duties in terms of revenue and regulation of foreign trade.
- ➤ *Excise Duties :* Excise duties are commodity tax as it is imposed on production of an item and it has no relevance with its sale. This is the largest source of revenue for the Central Government.
- ➤ Except liquor, opium and other drugs, production of all the other items is taxable under Central Excise Duties.
- ➤ On July 15, 2010 Indian rupee got its symbol, just like other leading currencies of the world viz. Dollar, Euro, Pound Sterling and Yen. [pink box; ₹ symbol image]
- ➤ The new symbol is an amalgamation of Devanagari 'Ra' and the Roman 'R' without the stem.
- ➤ On March 5, 2009 the Government announced a contest to create a symbol for the Rupee.
- ➤ The new symbol designed by D. Udaya Kumar, a post-graduate of IIT Bombay, was finally selected by the Union Cabinet on July 15, 2010.
- ➤ One Coin and One Rupee note belong to 'Legal Tender Money' category.
- ➤ M1 is known as Narrow Money.
- ➤ M3 is known as Broad Money.

**Goods and Services Tax**

**Legislative Development**
- ➤ The proposal to introduce a national level Goods and Services Tax (GST) was first mooted in 2006-07. The Goods and Services Tax Council was constituted in 2016.
- ➤ The GST Council is deliberating on important GST issues, Central GST Bill, 2017; The Integrated GST Bill, 2017; The GST (Compensation to States) Bill, 2017.
- ➤ [pink box] GST (Goods and Services Tax) Constitutional Amendment Bill received assent of the President on September 8, 2016 as the 101st Amendment Act. [continues p.282]

### [Lucent book p.282 / PDF 295 — left column, upper (through the GST material, before "13. Industry")]

- ➤ As per Article 279A (1) of the amended constitution, the GST Council was constituted on september 12, 2016.
- ➤ A three-tier levy of Central GST, State GST and an Integrated GST proposed. The IGST mechanism has been designed to ensure seamless flow of input tax credit from one state to another. The IGST was launched at the stroke of midnight on July 1, 2017.
- ➤ GST was implemented in the country in July, 2017. Subsuming of various central indirect taxes and levies such as central excise duty, additional excise duties, excise duty levied under the medicinal and toilet preparations (excise duties) Act, 1955, Service Tax, Additional Customs Duty commonly known as Countervailing Duty, Special Additional Duty of Customs and Central Surcharges and Cesses so far as they relate to the supply of goods and services.
- ➤ Levy of Integrated Goods and Services Tax on inter-state transactions of goods and services. Coverage of all goods and services, except alcoholic liquor for human consumption, for the levy of goods and services tax. In case of petroleum and petroleum products, it has been provided that these goods shall not be subject to the levy till a date notified on the recommendation of the GST Council.

**Taxes being submerged into GST** [pink box]
*Central level :* a. Central Excise Duty, b. Additional Excise Duty, c. Service Tax, d. Additional Customs Duty commonly known as Countervailing Duty, and e. Special Additional Duty of Customs.
*State level :* (a) State Value Added Tax/Sales Tax, (b) Entertainment Tax (other than the tax levied by the local bodies), Central Sales Tax (levied by the Centre and collected by the States), (c) Octroi and Entry tax, (d) Purchase Tax (e) Luxury tax and (f) Taxes on lottery, betting and gambling.

- ➤ The states are being compensated for loss of revenue arising on account of implementation of the new tax regime.
- ➤ GST has replaced the complex indirect tax structure with a simple, transparent and technology driven tax regime and thus integrated India into a single common market.   *[INDIA 2024]*

**GST Collection** [box]
- ➤ The Gross Goods and Services Tax (GST) collections hit a record high in April 2024 at ₹ 2.10 lakh crore. This represents a significant 12.4 % year- on-year growth, driven by a strong increase in domestic transactions and imports. After accounting for refunds, the net GST revenue for April 2024 stands at ₹ 1.92 lakh crore, reflecting a 15.5 % growth compared to the same period last year.   *[Source : BHARAT 2025]*

*(Then: "13. Industry" begins at the bottom of the left column of p.282 — see §13.)*

### §12 doubts ⚠

- ⚠ **"Excise Tax, Sales Tax, Vat, Entertainment Tax are indirect taxes"** — as printed (VAT, sales tax, service tax and entertainment tax have been subsumed by GST since 2017; the book still lists them). Compare Parmar Ch.4 (GST) — see Part C.
- ⚠ **Rupee symbol:** "contest announced 5 March 2009; designed by D. Udaya Kumar, IIT Bombay postgraduate; selected by Cabinet 15 July 2010" — as printed. "One Coin and One Rupee note belong to Legal Tender" — as printed. "M1 narrow money; M3 broad money" — as printed; compare Parmar Ch.8 (M0–M4).
- ⚠ **Revenue Collection:** FY2023-24 net direct taxes ₹19,60,166 crore (provisional); FY2024-25 up to Nov 2024 ₹12,46,229 crore = 56.47% of BE ₹22,07,000 crore — as printed; superseded.
- ⚠ **GST milestones:** "first mooted in 2006-07"; "GST Council constituted in 2016 / Article 279A(1) — 12 Sept 2016"; "101st Amendment Act — assent 8 Sept 2016"; "launched at midnight 1 July 2017"; taxes subsumed; "GST collection April 2024 ₹2.10 lakh crore (gross), ₹1.92 lakh crore (net)" — as printed. GST rate slabs are NOT given in Lucent.
- ⚠ **Chelliah Committee** dates ("August 1991"; recommends taxing agricultural income above ₹25,000) and **K.L. Rekhi Committee (1992)** — as printed; Parmar Ch.11 lists "Raja Chelliah Committee — central taxation" with no year; "Kelkar Committee (2002-03)" is not in Lucent's tax section.

# [4/9] inflation_unemployment_schemes_raw_extraction.md

# ECONOMY RAW EXTRACTION — Inflation, Unemployment and Welfare/Employment Schemes

Sources: Parmar SSC Fatman (2nd Ed. 2026), Economics; Lucent's General Knowledge (2026), Ch.06 Indian Economy. Verbatim transcription with typos preserved; ⚠ marks doubts. No test cross-referencing and no ★-marking at this stage.

Order: Part A (Parmar) then Part B (Lucent) then Part C (cross-book comparison).

---

## PART A — PARMAR SSC FATMAN (2nd Ed. 2026): Economics Ch.5 "INFLATION AND UNEMPLOYMENT" (book pp.266–269, PDF pp.287–290)

Running header "INFLATION AND UNEMPLOYMENT". Chapter ends with ■■■ at right column, upper part of p.269 (rest of page blank).

### [book p.266 / PDF 287]

**Left column — box titled "INFLATION"**
- •*Inflation is increase in the general level of prices of Goods and Services.*
- •Purchasing power *decreases* in case of inflation.
- •During inflation the *debtor/borrower benefits more than the lender.*
*E.g. If you borrow ₹ 10,000, from a lender at a fixed interest during inflation, the money you repay is worth less than when you borrowed it. As when inflation occurs, the general price goods and services increases, meaning the value of money decreases. When you repay the ₹ 10,000, the money you return has less purchasing power than when you borrowed it.*
[box] Real Interest Rate = Nominal Interest Rate - Inflation

**Fisher's Quantity Theory**
- •Irving Fisher gave the *"Money Illusion"* concept.
- •Fisher's Quantity Theory explains the relationship between money supply, velocity of money, price level, and transaction.
[box] MV = PT — M = Money Supply; P = Price Level; V = Velocity of Money; T = Transaction
- •The equation suggests that an *increase in the money supply (M) will lead to an increase in the price level (P) assuming that the velocity of money (V) and the level of transactions (T) are constant.*
- •The theory also implies that changes in the money supply have a *direct impact on inflation,* as an increase in the money supply leads to an *increase in the price level,* which in turn *reduces the purchasing power of money.*

**Types of Inflation**
1. Creeping: 3-4%
2. Walking: 4-10%
3. Running: 10-20%
4. Galloping: 20-100%
5. Hyper: >100%
[meme image: two-panel "Mehngai Daayan khaae jaat hai ✗" / "Inflation Daayan khaae jaat hai ✓"]
[box] *Inflationary Gap: When Demand exceeds Supply. In this case Actual GDP exceeds Potential GDP*

**Seigniorage**
The profit earned by the central bank by printing currency .
Seigniorage is the profit earned by central bank through issuing currency, calculated as the difference between the face value of money and its production cost.
Seigniorage = Face value - Production cost

**Right column**

**Causes of Inflation**
| Demand Pull Inflation | Cost Push Inflation |
|---|---|
| It is caused by an increase in demand (demand side inflation) | It is caused by an increase in production costs (supply side inflation) |
| It can be caused by strong economic growth, increased government spending or increased exports | It can be caused by increased wages, raw material prices, or indirect taxes |
| It can be short-term | It can be long-term |
| "Too much money chasing too few goods", an expression that describes demand-pull inflation | Increase in cost of any factors of Production and Input Cost |

**Measurement of Inflation**
- •Wholesale Price Index (WPI) and Consumer Price Index (CPI) is used to measure inflation.

**Inflation Rate Formula**
To find the percentage rate of inflation between two years:
Inflation Rate = [(Current Year Index − Previous Year Index) / Previous Year Index] × 100

**Wholesale Price Index (WPI)**
- •It measures the average change in prices of goods at the *wholesale level.*
- •It also reflects the change in prices of goods before they reach consumers.
- •*Published by: Office of Economic Adviser (OEA) under Ministry of Commerce and Industry*
- •Base Year: *2022-23* *(Previously by : 2011-12)*
- •It is published: *Monthly (14th day of each month)*

*Weightage Distribution in WPI*
- *Manufactured goods (64.2%)*
- *Primary articles (22.6%)*
- *Fuel and power (13.15%)*
[box] WPI = 100 × (Cost of a fixed basket of goods in the current year / Cost of fixed basket of goods in base year)

**Consumer Price Index (CPI)**
- •It measures the average change in prices of goods and services at the *consumer / retail level.*
- •It reflects the cost of living for consumers.
- •*Released by: National Statistical Office (NSO) under Ministry of Statistics and Programme Implementation (MOSPI)*
- •Base Year : *2023-24.*
- •Frequency: *Monthly (usually 12th day of each month)*

### [book p.267 / PDF 288]

**Left column**

**Weightage Distribution in CPI:**
- *Food and Beverages (highest 45.8%)*
- *Housing (10%)*
- *Fuel and power (6.8%)*
[box] CPI = 100 × (Cost of a fixed basket of goods and services in the current year / Cost of a fixed basket of goods and services in base year)

**Four types of CPI are as follows**
- CPI for Industrial Workers (IW) Base year: *2016*
- CPI for Agricultural Labourer (AL) Base year: *2019*
- CPI for Rural Labourer (RL) Base year: *2019*
- CPI (Rural/Urban/Combined)→ Base Year: *2024, published by National Statistical Office (NSO) under MoSPI*   [side box: CPI-AL, CPI-RL, CPI-IW released by the Labour Bureau]
- CPI combined is used by RBI for inflation targeting and formulating monetary policy.

**Producer Price Index :–** Measure the average change in selling prices received by domestic producers for their output.
- •*Perspective :–* Unlike CPI (buyer's perspective), PPI looks at inflation from the seller's perspective.
- •*Significance :–* It captures price changes at the "factory gate" before taxes and distribution costs are added.
- •It is considered a leading indicator because cost increases here are usually passed on to consumers later.

**Monetary Policy Framework Agreement (MPFA)**
It was signed between *GoI & RBI in Feb, 2015*
It mandates the RBI to maintain price stability while keeping growth in mindset with a goal to maintain *CPI Inflation at (4 ± 2)%*

**Core Inflation**
- •Core inflation is a measure of inflation that *excludes food and energy prices.* It's calculated using the consumer price index (CPI)
[box] Core Inflation = Headline Inflation-Volatile Inflation

**Index of Industrial Production (IIP)**
- •It is a statistical indicator that measures the *growth of industrial production in a country over time.*
- • Base Year: *2022-2023.*
- •*Published by: National Statistical Organisation (NSO) under MOSPI.*

**Right column**

- •*Eight* core industries have 40% contributions in IIP in decreasing order:
  Refinery Products → Electricity → Steel → Coal → Crude Oil → Natural Gas → Cement → Fertilizers

**Box heading: TYPES: INDICATOR AND DYNAMICS OF INFLATION**

**Disinflation**
- •*The decrease in the rate of inflation, while prices may still be rising.*
- •It is measured by the change in the inflation rate from one year to the next.
- •*Cause:* A slowdown in the economy's money supply through a tighter monetary policy.
[diagram: 10% → 8% → 7% → 5%, "Decrease in inflation rate over the years"]

**Deflation**
- •It is a *general decrease in price of goods and services. In deflation purchasing power increases.*
- •*Cause:* Reduced money supply, reduced investment, or lower production.

**Phillips Curve**
- •It is a graph that shows the relationship between *unemployment* and *inflation* in an economy.
[Graph 1: PHILLIPS CURVE — y-axis INFLATION RATE % (2%–8%), x-axis UNEMPLOYMENT RATE % (3%–8%); downward-sloping curve; box "INFLATION AND UNEMPLOYMENT HAVE INVERSE RELATIONSHIP"; point B "HIGH INFLATION LOW UNEMPLOYMENT", point A "LOW INFLATION HIGH UNEMPLOYMENT"]

**Stagflation**
- •It is a period of *high inflation and low economic growth.* It's a combination of the words "stagnation" and "inflation" *(Stagflation = Stagnation + Inflation)*
- •When stagflation increases, *high unemployment* is a direct consequence.
- •*Cause:* When the production of goods and services in an economy slows down or declines.

### [book p.268 / PDF 289]

**Left column**

**Shrinkflation**
A situation where the price of a product remains the same but the quantity or size of the product is reduced, effectively increasing the price per unit.

**Bottleneck Inflation**
A situation where production or supply is restricted at a particular stage of the production or distribution process, causing a slowdown and leading to shortages and price rise.

**Skewflation**
A situation where prices of essential goods (especially food items) rise sharply while prices of non-essential goods remain stable or rise slowly, causing an uneven or skewed pattern of inflation in the economy.

**Reflation**
A deliberate government policy to increase the level of economic activity and prices by increasing money supply, government expenditure, or reducing taxes, usually to recover from recession.

**Imported Inflation**
Inflation that occurs when the prices of imported goods and raw materials increase, which raises the overall price level in the domestic economy.

**Inflationary Gap**
The excess of aggregate demand over aggregate supply at full employment level, which leads to upward pressure on prices.

**Inflation Target**
The specific rate of inflation that a country's central bank aims to maintain through monetary policy to ensure price stability.

**Box heading: UNEMPLOYMENT**
- •Unemployment refers to a situation when a person actively searches for the job but is unable to find work.
*National Sample Survey Office (NSSO) uses three methods to measure Unemployment.*
1. *Usual status approach*
2. *Current weekly status approach.*
3. *Daily status approach.*
[side box, meme: photo captioned "Unemployment — Parmar Sir to students: 'Dil dukha hai lekin tuta toh nahi aur ummeed ka daaman abhi chhuta to nahi hai'." — not book content]

**Right column**

**A. Usual Status (US) Approach**
Reference period: Last 365 days (1 year) preceding the date of survey.
- •Used to identify long-term or chronic unemployment.
- •Usual Status has two components:
1. *Principal Status (PS)*
The activity in which a person spent most of the time during the last 365 days.
- •Determined using the majority time criterion.
2. *Subsidiary Status (SS)*
A person may perform additional economic activity for at least 30 days during the year apart from the principal activity.
- •This is known as Usual Status (PS + SS).

**B. Current weekly status (CWS):**
In labour force for last 1 week; If able to find work for at least 1 hour on any 1 day → Employed

**C. Current Daily Status (CDS):**
It measures the intensity of unemployment
In labour force for 1 day;
If work >4hrs=100% engaged
If work 1-4hrs=50% engaged
If work <1hr= unemployed

**UNEMPLOYMENT MEASUREMENT**
- •*Labour Force Participation Rate (LFPR):* The percentage of persons in the labour force (working or available for work) in the population.
- •*Worker Population Ratio (WPR):* The percentage of employed persons in the population.
- •*Unemployment Rate (UR):* The percentage of person unemployed among the persons in the labour force.

**Types of Unemployment**
*Structural Unemployment*
- •A type of unemployment that occurs when there is a *mismatch between the skills of the workforce and the skills required for available jobs.*
E.g. An advancement in technology like advanced machinery can replace farmer labour.
*Educated Unemployment*
- •Educated unemployment is when people with *academic degrees are unable to find employment.* It can also be called *graduate unemployment.*
- •It is seen in *urban* part of India.
(continues p.269)

### [book p.269 / PDF 290]

**Left column**

E.g. An engineering graduate, who is unable to find a job in their field because they lack practical skills.

*Frictional Unemployment*
- •A *short-term* type of unemployment that occurs *when people search for better job* that's why it is also called *Search/Voluntary unemployment.*
E.g. An employee leaving a current job to move to a better one as frequently seen in urban cities.

*Disguised Unemployment*
- •A type of unemployment where *more people are working than actually needed,* meaning their removal *would not affect productivity.*
Eg. A family of five works on a farm in rural area that could be run by three people.

*Cyclical Unemployment*
- •A type of unemployment that occurs when the *labor force is reduced* due to *business cycles or economic fluctuations.* When economy revives, there is an opportunity to employment (mostly seen in *urban areas*).
E.g. Great Recession.
[box] NOTE: *Great Recession* — Significant prolonged downturn of economic activity for 2 or more consecutive quarters E.g : US Subprime Lending crisis whereas prolonged recession is termed as depression. E.g. Great depression of USA (1929-1939)

*Seasonal Unemployment*
- •A type of unemployment that occurs during specific times of the year. The employment is based on *seasonal basis and is mostly seen in rural part of India.*
Ex: Lights or firework sellers during Diwali.

*Classical Unemployment*
- •It is also known as *real wage unemployment,* it occurs when the *real wage for a job is too high, which leads to more job seekers than job openings.*
E.g. When workers negotiate for a minimum salary that's more than what a company can afford.

*Natural Unemployment*
- •Natural unemployment refers to the *lowest unemployment level* a healthy economy can sustain *without causing inflation.*

**Right column**

**Marginal Worker vs Main Worker**
| Marginal Worker | Main Worker |
|---|---|
| Marginal worker is someone who is employed for less than 6 months or 183 days. | Main worker is someone who is employed for more than 6 months or 183 days. |
| E.g. Landless agricultural laborers and people who work in low-wage industries. | E.g. Construction workers, seasonal employees, independent contractors. |

■■■ (red end marker — end of Ch.5)

### Part A doubts (Ch.5) ⚠

## PART B — LUCENT'S GK (2026), Ch.06 "Indian Economy", §7 "Unemployment and Employment (Programmes and Schemes)" (Lucent book pp.262–[continues])

Page mapping: PDF = book + 12 (PDF 274 = book 262 … ). Section starts mid-left column of p.262 (after PURA, end of §6).

**Overlap note (Static GK "Govt Policies"):** Most of this section is a scheme/programme catalogue (Jan Dhan, MUDRA, Stand Up India, UPI, Aadhaar, DBT, DAY-NRLM, Ayushman Bharat, PMFBY-type items, etc.). The project holds no Static GK raw files at present (checked at start of this session), so it is transcribed once here and should be pointed to from the Static GK build later.

### [Lucent book p.262 / PDF 274 — left column (from "7.") and right column]

**Left column**

**7. Unemployment and Employment** *(Programmes and Schemes)*
- ➤ In common parlance anybody who is not gainfully employed in any productive activity is called unemployed. However, it can be of two kinds 1. voluntary unemployed and 2. involuntary unemployed. Here we are concerned with the second category of unemployed persons. Hence, unemployment can be defined as a situation when persons able and willing to work are seeking jobs at the prevailing wage level but they are unable to get the same. Unemployment in developing economies like India is not the result of deficiency of effective demand in the Keynesian sense, but a consequence of shortage of capital equipment or other complementary resources.
- ➤ In India unemployment is structural in nature due to lack of productive capacity and resources.

**Types of Unemployment**
1. *Cyclical unemployment :* It is the result of depression in an economy.
2. *Frictional unemployment :* This kind of unemployment is temporary. It is the result of a situation when new industries drive out old ones and workers change over to better jobs.
3. *Open unemployment :* It refers to those who have no work to do even though they are able and willing to do work.
4. *Seasonal unemployment :* It means the unemployment of the farmers and farm labourers during non-crop seasons. For example, in the period between past harvest and next sowing, agricultural labourers are unemployed.
5. *Educated unemployed :* This is mainly found in urban areas. Those educated persons who are unable to get work come under this category.
6. *Under-employment (Disguised unemployment) :* It results when a person contributes to production less than what he or she is capable of, for example, an engineer working as a clerk is underemployed.

**Right column**
7. *Compulsory unemployment :* It means the labour power which is ready to work on the current rate but does not get the work.
- ➤ During Ninth Plan, total 3.6 crore fresh unemployeds began to look for employment.
- ➤ Before its replacement by the 'NITI Aayog' the Planning Commission had to collect data of unemployment on the basis of 'Lakadawala Formula' effective from 11th March, 1997 and prior to this the process to collect data was on the basis of surveys of National Sample Survey Organisation (NSSO).
- ➤ In India, the data relating to unemployment are collected by National Sample Survey Organisation (NSSO). This Organisation has the following concepts with regard to unemployment :
1. *General status of unemployment :* In this category, generally, those unemployed for more than one year are included. As such it is a long-term unemployment.
2. *Weekly-unemployment :* The persons who have not got work for even one hour in a week are included in this category.
3. *Daily unemployment :* It is considered the best concept of unemployment.
- ➤ The main reasons for unemployment in India are slow economic development, population explosion, outdated technique, improper education system and limited effect of government planning.
- ➤ India's unemployment rate among people aged 15 years and above fell to 7.7% in May, 2023 from 8.5% in April 2023, due to a decline in the labour participation at 441.9 million, according to data from the Centre for Monitoring Indian Economy (CMIE). Compared to April, labour participation rate (LPR) fell by 1.1 percentage point to 39.6% in May, 2023.   *[Source : The ET, 10 June, 2023]*

**Employment trends**
- ➤ As per the 2023-24 annual Periodic Labour Force Survey (PLFS) report, the unemployment rate for individuals aged 15 years and above has steadily declined from 6 % in 2017-18 to 3.2 % in 2023-24.
- ➤ The labour force participation rate (LFPR) and the worker-to-population ratio (WPR) have also increased. In Q2 FY25, the urban unemployment rate for people aged 15 years and above improved slightly to 6.4 % compared to 6.6 % in Q2 FY24. Both LFPR and WPR also increased during this period.
- ➤ The formal sector in India has seen significant growth, with net Employees' Provident Fund Organisation (EPFO) subscriptions more than doubling from 61 lakh in FY19 to 131 lakh in FY24. In April -November 2024, net additions reached 95.6 lakh, driven largely by youth. Workers aged 18-25 years contributed to 47 % of the net payroll additions. This indicates a growing trend towards formal employment, which enhances workers' access to social security and stability.   *[Source : Economic Survey 2024-25]*
