**Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Scheme (MNREGS)**
- ➤ The National Rural Employment Guarantee Bill was passed by Parliament on September 7, 2005. It secured Presidential assent later in 2005 itself and became an Act (NREGA).
- ➤ The Act provides for at least 100 days of employment to one able bodied person in every rural household every year.
- ➤ The Act (NREGA) came into force from Feb. 2, 2006. Initially 200 districts have been selected for the enforcement of the scheme. At present the Act covers all the 740 rural districts of the country.
- ➤ The Govt. of India, on October 2, 2009, renamed the NREGA as the Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act (MNREGA).
- ➤ The Central government and the State governments bear 75% and 25% of the cost of material, wages of skilled and semi-skilled workers.
- ➤ The centre has hiked the wages under MGNREGS in the range of 2.33% to 7.48% for FY 2025-26. The MGNREGS wages have seen a hike in the range of ₹ 7 to ₹ 26. The MGNREGS wages have been hiked by ₹7 in 5 states. The highest increase of ₹ 26 has been recorded in Haryana, where it will touch the ₹ 400 mark.   *[Source : IE, 28.03.2025]*

**Right column**

**Employment Guarantee Act, 2005** [pink box]
The Government, on the advice of the National Advisory Council, has passed the National Rural Employment Guarantee Act. The main features of the Act are :
1. Every household in rural India will have a right to at least 100 days of guaranteed employment every year for at least one adult member. The employment will be in the form of casual manual labour at the statutory minimum wage, and the wages shall be paid within 7 days of the week during which work was done.
2. Work should be provided within 15 days of demanding it, and the work should be located within 5 kilometer distance.
3. If work is not provided to anybody within the given time, he/she will be paid a daily unemployment allowance, which will be at least one-third of the minimum wages.
4. Workers employed on public works will be entitled to medical treatment and hospitalization in case of injury at work, along with a daily allowance of not less than half of the statutory minimum wage. In case of death or disability of a worker, an ex-gratia payment shall be made to his legal heirs as per provisions of the Workmen Compensation Act.
5. 5% of wages may be deducted as contribution to welfare schemes like health insurance, accident insurance, survivor benefits, maternity benefits and social security schemes.
6. For non-compliance with rules, strict penalties have been laid down.
7. For transparency and accountability, all accounts and records of the programme will be made available for public scrutiny.
8. The District Collector/Chief Executive Officer will be responsible for the programme at the district level.
9. The Gram Sabha will monitor the work of the Gram Panchayat by way of social audit.

**Some Important Development and Employment Programmes**

**AIM (Atal Innovation Mission)**
- ➤ AIM is a flagship initiative of the Central Government, set up by NITI Aayog to promote innovation and entrepreneurship across the country.
- ➤ AIM is also envisaged as an umbrella innovation organisation that would play an instrumental role in alignment of innovation policies between Central, state and sectoral innovation schemes at various levels to establish an ecosystem of innovation and entrepreneurship.

**Pradhan Mantri Kaushal Vikas Yojana (PMKVY)**
- ➤ The Union Cabinet on March 21, 2015 cleared a scheme to provide skill training to 1.4 million youth, with an overall outlay of ₹ 1,120 crore.
- ➤ The Pradhan Mantri Kaushal Vikas Yojana, is being implemented by the Ministry of Skill Development and Entrepreneurship through the National Skill Development Corporation, focuses on fresh entrants to the labour market especially Class-X and Class–XII drop outs.
- ➤ According to BHARAT 2025, Substantial skill training delivery in short-term training indicating improved skill outcomes and employability with 1.56 crore candidates trained since the launch of the scheme in 2015 till October 8, 2024. Around 54% of these candidates are in the age group of 15-29 years.

**Aatmanirbhar Bharat Rojgar Yojana (ABRY)**
- ➤ ABRY was announced as a part of Aatmanirbhar Bharat 3.0 package to boost the economy, increase the employment generation in post Covid recovery phase [continues p.264]

### [Lucent book p.264 / PDF 276]

**Left column**
and to incentivize creation of new employment along with social security benefits and restoration of loss of employment during the pandemic. This scheme is being implemented through the Employees Provident Fund Organisation (EPFO).
- ➤ Till March 2024, 1,52,517 estabhishments have claimed benefits amounting to 10,188.50 crore in respect of 60.49 lakh beneficiaries.   *[Source : BHARAT 2025]*

**Schemes and Plans of Union and State Government**

**PAHAL Scheme**
- ➤ The PAHAL (DBTL) scheme was launched on 1st June 2013 and covered 291 districts.
- ➤ The DBTL modified Scheme for LPG consumers (PAHAL) was relaunched on November 15, 2014 in 54 districts and in the rest of the country on January 1, 2015. The Scheme aims to transfer the subsidy on LPG directly into the bank accounts of over 15 crore LPG consumers.
- ➤ PAHAL has entered into Guinness Book of World Records as being the largest Direct Benefit Transfer scheme.

**Beti Bachao-Beti Padhao**
- ➤ The 'Beti Bachao-Beti Padhao' scheme, which seeks to address gender imbalance and discrimination against the girl child was launched by the Prime Minister on 22 Jan, 2015 at Panipat in Haryana.
- ➤ Mahendergarh and Jhajjar districts of Haryana are badly effected with imbalanced Sex ratios where there are just about 775 girls for 1,000 boys, the lowest in India.
- ➤ Haryana Chief Minister launched a scheme for girl child- 'Aapki Beti Humari Beti'-with an aim to combat the problem of declining child sex ratio in the state.

**Sukanya Samriddhi Accounts**
- ➤ Rate of interest 9.1% Per Annum (2014-15), calculated on yearly basis, yearly compounded.
- ➤ Minimum INR. 1000 and Maximum INR. 1,50,000 in a financial year. Subsequent deposit in multiple of INR 100. Deposits can be made in lump-sum. No limit on number of deposits either in a month or in a Financial year.
- ➤ Account can be opened up to age of 10 years only from the date of birth.
- ➤ Account can be closed after completion of 21 years.

**Atal Pension Yojana**
- ➤ According to BHARAT 2025, the Atal Pension Yojana (APY) was launched in 2015, to address the longevity risks among the workers in unorganised sector who are not covered under any statutory social security scheme. The APY is focused on all citizens in the unorganised sector, who join the National Pension System (NPS) administered by the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority (PFRDA).

**Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana**
- ➤ With a view to increasing banking penetration and promoting financial inclusion and with the main objective of covering all households with at least one bank account per household across the country, a National Mission on financial inclusion named as (PMJDY) was announced in 2014.

**Right column**
- ➤ Objectives of PMJDY include : (i) universal access to banking facilities for all households across the country through a bank branch or a fixed point business correspondent (BC) within a reasonable distance; and (ii) to cover all households with at least one basic bank account with RuPay Debit card having in-built accident insurance cover of ₹ 1 lakh.

**Pradhan Mantri Mudra Yojana**
- ➤ The Pradhan Mantri Mudra Yoiana (PMMY) launched in 2015 is administered by the Micro Units Development Refinance Agency (MUDRA) Ltd, a corporate subsidiary of Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI). PMMY provides access to institutional finance for micro/small business. It provides collateral free credit up to ₹ 10 lakh to small entrepreneurs.
- ➤ There are three categories under MUDRA scheme : Shishu - for starters - covers loan up to ₹ 50,000, Kishore- for mid stage finance seekers - covers loan above ₹ 50,000 and up to ₹5,00,000 and Tarun - for growth seekers - covers loan above ₹ 5,00,000 and to ₹10 lakh.

**Stand Up India Scheme**
- ➤ The Stand-Up India Scheme, launched in 2016, aims to promote entrepreneurship among the Scheduled Caste/Scheduled Tribe and women by facilitating bank loans of value between ₹ 10 lakhs and ₹ 1 crore to at least one SC/ST borrower and one-woman borrower per bank branch of Scheduled Commercial Banks for setting up Greenfield enterprises in trading, manufacturing, services sector and also for activities allied to agriculture. In 2019-20, the StandUp India scheme was extended for the entire period coinciding with the 15th Finance Commission of 2020-25.
- ➤ The extent of margin money to be brought by the borrower is "up to 15 % of the project cost which can be arranged in convergence with the eligible central/state schemes. However, the borrower shall contribute at least 10 % of the project cost as own contribution.

**Digidhan Mission**
- ➤ DIGIDHAN Mission was put in place following the announcement in the Union Budget for 2017-18, of setting up of a Mission for promotion of digital payments with a target of 2,500 crore digital transactions during the financial year through UPI, USSD, Aadhaar Pay, IMPS and debit cards.
- ➤ In July 2023 Promotion of Digital Payments was transferred from MeitY to Department of Financial Services.

**Unified Payments Interface**
- ➤ Unified Payments Interface (UPI) is an indigenous digital payment system which provides the facility of quick and easy payments from multiple bank accounts in a single mobile application. UPI has revolutionized digital payments in the country, UPI transactions have grown from 92 crore in FY 2017-18 to 13,116 crore in FY 2023-24 at CAGR of 129 %.   *[Source : BHARAT 2025]*

**PM Jan Dhan Yojana**
- ➤ Aim-scheme to promote financial inclusion: Prime Minister Narendra Modi launched 'Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana' to help the poor open bank accounts. The slogan for the Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan mission is "Mera Khata – Bhagya Vidhaata". [continues p.265]

### [Lucent book p.265 / PDF 277]

**Left column**
- ➤ Every individual who opens a bank account will become eligible to receive an accident insurance cover of up-to ₹ 1 Lakh. HDFC Ergo General Insurance will provide the accident cover under the scheme.
- ➤ LIC (Life Insurance Corporation) will provide the life insurance cover of ₹ 30,000 under the scheme.

**Saansad Adarsh Gram Yojana**
- ➤ Saansad Adarsh Gram Yojana (SAGY), a new initiative in rural development, was launched by the Government of India on October 11, 2014 with the objective that these Adarsh Grams *(Model Villages)* serve as the "nucleus of health' cleanliness, greenery and cordiality" within the village community. The scheme's Guidelines call upon Members of Parliament (MPs) to make one village of their choice in their constituency a Model Village by 2016, and another two villages by 2019. The Adarsh Gram will have a population of 3,000 to 5,000 in plain areas and 1000 to 3000 in hilly, tribal and difficult areas. The time-frame for identification of new Adarsh Gram has ended in March 2024.

**Ayush**
- ➤ The Department of Indian System of Medicine and Homeopathy (ISM & H) was established in the Ministry of Health & Family Welfare in 1995.
- ➤ It was rechristened as Department of Ayurveda, Yoga and Naturopathy, Unani, Siddha and Homeopathy with acronym AYUSH in 2003.

**Ayushman Bharat**
- ➤ To provide health cover of ₹ 5 Lakh per family per year for secondary and tertiary care hospitalisation to poor and vulnerable sections of the population, Ayushman Bharat – Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (ABPMJAY) was launched in 2018 subsuming the erstwhile Rashtriya Swasthya Bima Yojana (RSBY).

**Shyama Prasad Mukherji Rurban Mission**
- ➤ The Ministry of Rural Development (MRD) launched the Shyama Prasad Mukherji Rurban Mission (SPMRM) in 2016, to implement the vision of "Development of a cluster of villages that preserve and nurture the essence of rural community life with focus on equity and inclusiveness without compromising with the facilities perceived to be essentially urban in nature, thus creating a cluster of "Rurban Villages". A 'Rurban cluster' is a cluster of geographically contiguous villages with a population of about 25000 to 50000 in plain and coastal areas and with a population of 5000 to 15000 in desert, hilly or tribal areas.

**Swachh Bharat Mission (Gramin)**
- ➤ The Government of India administers Swachh Bharat Mission *(Gramin)* [(SBM (G)] a comprehensive programme to ensure sanitation facilities in rural areas with the main goal of eradicating the practice of open defecation and ensuring clean environment. The rural sanitation programme was renamed as Nirmal Bharat Abhiyan (NBA) with effect from April 1, 2012. NBA has now been restructured into Swachh Bharat Mission *(Gramin)* [SBM (G)] with effect from October 2, 2014.
- ➤ According to BHARAT 2025 having achieved ODF, SBM-G Phase II launched in 2020-21 with the objective of achieving Sampoorna Swachhata by 2024-25 and transforming all villages from ODF to ODF Plus.

**Right column**

**Aadhaar**
- ➤ Aadhaar is the first on-scale Digital ID service of the world, which is formless and paperless. With more than 80 crore Aadhaar Cards issued, Aadhaar is the largest biometric programme in the world making India a global leader in biometric technology. A key objective of Aadhaar programme is to provide an identity infrastructure for delivery of various social welfare programmes and for effective targeting of welfare services.

**Direct Benefits Transfer (DBT)**
- ➤ To achieve targeted delivery of welfare services in a transparent and efficient manner, the Government launched Direct Benefits Transfer through Aadhaar Payment Bridge (APB) and other channels in 2013. As per the directions of Government of India, 35 centrally sponsored schemes come under the purview of DBT which is to be implemented across India. This Scheme is linked with DBT for LPG, PDS, MGNREGA etc.

**Deendayal Antyodaya Yojana—National Rural Livelihood Mission (DAY-NRLM)**
- ➤ According to BHARAT 2025, DAY-NRLM is a flagship poverty reduction programme of the Ministry of Rulral Development in 2011. DAY–NRLM is also one of the world's largest initiative to improve the livelihoods of the poor.
- ➤ Ajeevika Grameen Express Yojana (AGEY) has been introduced by the Govt. of India as a new sub-scheme under DAY-NRLM from the FY 2017-18.
- ➤ DAY-NRLM is primarily a capacity building programme which relies on adequate, professional, and trained manpower for successful implementation.
- ➤ Self-Help Groups (SHGs) are community-based organisations comprising of small groups of women who collaborate for mutual support, savings and credit activities.
- ➤ Women Enterprise Acceleration Fund (WEAF) is a key initiative under the Aajeevika NRLM for empowering women entrepreneurs in SHGs. Women entrepreneurs can receive loans of upto ₹ 5 lakhs for 5 years, with a 2% interest subvention for prompt repayment. Additionally, interest subvention is provided for loans of upto ₹ 1.5 Lakh per borrower for a maximum of 3 years.
- ➤ A special focus of the programme is on the Lakhpati Didis under this transformative initiative.   *[Source : BHARAT 2025]*

**Social Sector Programmes**
- ➤ The seven flagship programmes continue to receive high priority, viz. Sarva Siksha Abhiyan; Mid-Day Meal Scheme; National Health Mission; Integrated Child Development Services; Swachh Bharat Abhiyan; Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Scheme and 100 Smart Cities.
- ➤ After providing bank accounts to crores of unbanked people under *Jan Dhan Yojana* the Prime Minister has launched three social security schemes, including at ₹ 1 per day insurance cover. The schemes :
1. Pradhan Mantri Jeevan Jyoti Bima Yojana (PMJJBY)
2. Pradhan Mantri Suraksha Bima Yojana (PMSBY)
and [continues p.266]

### [Lucent book p.266 / PDF 278 — left column and right column (upper)]

**Left column**
3. Atal Pension Yojana (APY) were simultaneously launched at 115 locations throughout the country.
1. *Pradhan Mantri Jeevan Jyoti Bima Yojana (PMJJBY),* started in 2015, offers a renewable one year life cover of ₹ 2 lakh to all savings bank account holders in the age group of 18-50 years (life cover up to 55 years of age), covering death due to any reason, for a premium of ₹ 330 per annum per subscriber.
2. *Pradhan Mantri Suraksha Bima Yojana (PMSBY)* brought in 2015, offers a renewable one year accidental death-cum-disability cover of ₹ 2 lakh and 1 ₹ lakh for partial/permanent disability to all savings bank account holders in the age group of 18-70 years for a premium of ₹ 12 per annum per subscriber.
3. *Atal Pension Yojana (APS)* launched in May, 2015 is a scheme focussed on the workers of unorganised sector and provide subscribers a fixed minimum pension of ₹ 1,000, ₹ 2,000, ₹ 3,000, ₹ 4,000 or ₹ 5,000 per month, starting at the age of 60 years, depending on the contribution option exercised on entering at an age between 18 and 40 years.

**Pradhan Mantri Ujjwala Yojana (PMUY)**
- ➤ PMUY (Pradhan Mantri Ujjwala Yojana) is an ambitious social welfare scheme of Central Government launched on 1st May, 2016 from Ballia in Uttar Pradesh. Under the PM Ujjwala Yojana, the scheme aims to provide LPG connections to BPL households in the country. The scheme is aimed at replacing the unclean cooking fuels mostly used in the rural India with the clean and more efficient LPG *(Liquefied Petroleum Gas).*
- ➤ Ujjwala Yojana is aimed at providing 5 Crore LPG connections in the name of women in BPL *(Below Poverty Line)* households across the country. Some of the objectives of the scheme are Empowering women and protecting their health, reducing the serious health hazards associated with cooking based on fossil fuel, reducing the number of deaths in India due to unclean cooking fuel etc.

**Ujjwala 2.0**
- ➤ Ujjwala 2.0 was launched in 2021 to provide additional one crore LPG connections to adult women of poor households under PMUY.

**PM Surya Ghar Muft Bijli Yojana**
- ➤ A scheme of rooftop solar scheme launched by Prime Minister on 13th February, 2024. The scheme aims to solarize one crore households by providing free electricity to certain households every month.
- ➤ Under this scheme the central government will provide 300 units of free electricity per month to the beneficiaries by investing worth Rs. 75,000 crore. Free electricity scheme was already announced by the finance minister in an interim budget speech.

**PM Vishwakarma Scheme**
- ➤ PM Vishwakarma Scheme for traditional artisans and crafts people has been launched on the occasion of Vishwakarma Jayanti (17th September, 2023). Its logo, tagline and portal was also launched on the occasion.
- ➤ The scheme which has been conceptualised to benefit Indian artisans engaged in eighteen identified traditional trades. Under the scheme, the eligible beneficiaries (Vishwakarmas) would be registered free of charge through common services centres using biometric-based PM Vishwakarma portal.

**Right column (upper)**
- ➤ They will be provided recognition through PM Vishwakarma certificate and ID card, along with skill upskilling involving basic and advanced training and toolkit incentive of ₹ 15,000. Further, collateral-free credit support up to ₹1 lakh (first tranche) and up to ₹ 2 lakh (second tranche) at a concessional interest rate of 5 % would be made available, in addition to incentive for digital transactions and marketing support. Ministry of Micro, Small & Medium Enterprise is the nodal ministry of the scheme.   *[Source : BHARAT 2025]*

**PM-JANMAN**
- ➤ The Pradhan Mantri Janjatiya Unnat Gram Abhiyan is a programme that aims to improve the quality of life for Particularly Vulnerable Tribal Groups (PVTGs) to focus on 11 critical interventions through 9 line ministries. The programme is based on the success of the Pradhan Mantri Janjati Adivasi Nyaya Maha Abhiyan (PM-JAN-MAN), launched in 2023.
- ➤ India has ST population of 10.45 crore as per 2011 census, out of which 75 communities located in 18 states and the union territory of Andaman and Nicobar Islands have been categorized as PVTGS.   *[Source : BHARAT 2025]*

# ECONOMY RAW EXTRACTION — Banking, Monetary Policy, Financial System and Insurance

Sources: Parmar SSC Fatman (2nd Ed. 2026), Economics; Lucent's General Knowledge (2026), Ch.06 Indian Economy. Verbatim transcription with typos preserved; ⚠ marks doubts. No test cross-referencing and no ★-marking at this stage.

Order: Part A (Parmar) then Part B (Lucent) then Part C (cross-book comparison).

## PART A — PARMAR SSC FATMAN (2nd Ed. 2026): Economics Ch.6 "BANKING: PART 1" (book pp.270–273, PDF pp.291–294)

Running header "BANKING: PART 1". Chapter ends with ■■■ at bottom of right column, p.273. Contents (per the chapter map): RBI, Types of Money, History of Banking System in India, Microfinance Institutions, Other Financial Institutions.

### [book p.270 / PDF 291]

**Left column — box titled "BANKING: PART 1"**

**Reserve Bank of India (RBI)**
- •Reserve Bank of India (RBI) was established as an independent body based on the recommendations of *Royal Commission* on Indian Currency & finance. It was headed by *Hilton Young in 1926.*
- •The RBI was officially established under the *Reserve Bank of India Act* in *1934* and started operations on *1st April 1935 in Calcutta.*
- •RBI was nationalised on *1st January 1949.*
- •The headquarter was moved to *Mumbai in 1937.*
- •First Governor of RBI: *Sir Osborne Smith*
- •First Indian Governor of RBI: *Sir CD Deshmukh*
[side box: "Logo of RBI features a tiger and a palm tree." (RBI emblem image)]

**Central Board of Directors**
- • It is the apex governing body of the Reserve Bank of India (RBI), established under the RBI Act of 1934 to provide direction to the bank's affairs. Appointed by GOI for 4 years.
*The Board comprises*
- •The Governor (Chairperson)
- •4 Deputy Governors (full-time)
- •10 non-official directors from various fields
- •2 government officials (Ministry of Finance)
- •4 directors representing the Local Boards.

*RBI introduced Banking Ombudsman scheme in: 1995*
*Fixed Rate Repo were announced by Govt in 1997*

**LEAD BANK SCHEME**
- •Launched by the RBI in December 1969.
- •It assigns a specific public or private sector bank to lead the development of banking and credit services in a particular district.
- •It promotes an *"Area Approach"* to enhance rural credit, financial inclusion, and priority sector lending (PSL).

**Functions of RBI**
1. *Regulator of Banks:* RBI under the *Banking Regulation Act of 1949,* holds extensive authority to regulate the country's banking system and hence called as *"Bank of Banks".* *E.g. It grants licenses to banking companies before commencing operations, regulation of Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR) and Statutory Liquidity Ratio (SLR), the RBI also influences interest rates through monetary policy instruments like the repo rate (the rate at which banks borrow from the RBI).*

**Right column**

**Narasimham Committee - I (1991)**
*Focus:* Banking sector reforms & liberalisation.
*Key Recommendations:*
- •Reduction in SLR and CRR to improve liquidity in the banking system.
- •Introduction of prudential norms for income recognition, asset classification, and provisioning.
- •Adoption of capital adequacy norms (as per Basel standards)
- •Greater autonomy to banks and reduction in government interference.
- •Promotion of a competitive and efficient banking system.
- •Entry of private sector banks.

**Narasimham Committee - II (1998)**
*Focus:* Strengthening reforms & global competitiveness.
*Key Recommendations:*
- •Reduction of Non-Performing Assets (NPAs)
- •Strengthening of banking supervision and regulation.
- •Consolidation and merger of banks to create strong global banks.
- •Creation of Asset Reconstruction Funds/Companies (ARCs) to deal with bad loans.
- •Strengthening of capital adequacy and risk management systems.
- •Recommendation of 3-tier banking structure.

2. *Monetary Authority:* The RBI formulates and implements monetary policy to *control inflation, manage money supply, and ensure adequate credit flow to various sectors of the economy.*
3. *Currency Printing:* The RBI prints and manages currency in India, including banknotes *(except for ₹1 note and coins issued under Ministry of Finance, GOI)*
4. The RBI is often referred to as the *"lender of last resort"* (it acts as the final source of funding for commercial banks when they are unable to secure loans from other sources during financial crises).

[box] In India, currency is printed in Nasik, Dewas, Mysore, Salboni and the coins are minted in Mumbai, Hyderabad, Calcutta, Noida.

The *Bimal Jalan Committee (2018-19),* appointed by the RBI, recommended a new Economic Capital Framework (ECF) that enabled transferring surplus reserves to the government while maintaining a Contingency Risk Buffer (CRB) of *5.5%–6.5%* of the RBI's balance sheet.

### [book p.271 / PDF 292]

**Left column**

**Types of Money**
*1. Fiat Money*
- •It is a type of currency that a government declares as *legal tender, meaning it is authorised by government to be used as a medium of exchange.* It is issued by a central bank and is *not backed* by a physical commodity like *gold or silver.*
*2. Fiduciary Money*
Fiduciary money is money that is accepted as a medium of exchange due to the *trust that exists between the payer and the payee.* Ex Cheques, tokens/bitcoins.
*3. Plastic Money*
- •Plastic money, also known as electronic money, is a payment method that uses electronic fund transfers instead of cash.
- •It includes debit cards, credit cards, and prepaid cards.
*4. Hot Money*
- •Hot money refers to funds that investors *quickly move between countries or financial markets* to take advantage of short-term opportunities such as *stocks, deposits, bonds,* etc.

**Clean Money Policy**
- •RBI introduced *Clean Note Policy in 1999,* asking the banks to not pass on the *soiled, torn, and scribbled notes to the customer and deposit the same to the RBI.*
- •This was introduced under *Section 35A* of the *Banking regulation Act, 1949.*

*According to RBI Act 1934, banking system can be classified as*
[Flow chart: (top) → **Scheduled Banks** → Commercial → Profit → {**Public Sector Banks** → "12 Nationalised Banks"; **Private Sector Banks** → "Indian Banks Ex: HDFC, Kotak" and "Foreign Banks Ex: City Group, HSBC"}; and **Non-scheduled Banks** → Cooperative Banks → Non-Profit ("Depositors are owners")]
- •*State Bank Of India* was the only nationalised bank then, The *14 banks* were nationalised through *The Banking Companies(Acquisition & Transfer Of Undertaking) Act,* (continues right column)

**Right column**

*1969* with total deposit of *more than 50 crore,* later on *6 banks (Andhra Bank,Corporation Bank, New Bank of India, Oriental Bank of Commerce, Punjab and Sind Bank, Vijaya Bank)* were nationalised through, *The Banking Companies(Acquisition & Transfer Of Undertaking) Act, 1980* with a deposit of *more than 200 crore.*

**Box heading: HISTORY OF BANKING SYSTEM IN INDIA**
- •*Bank of Hindustan* was the first bank to be established in *1770* in *Calcutta.*
- •During the British Rule in India, three Presidential Banks: *Bank of Calcutta (1806), Bank of Bombay (1840),* and *Bank of Madras (1843)* were eventually *merged into a single bank → Imperial Bank of India in 1921.*
- •The Imperial Bank of India was later *nationalised* and renamed as *State Bank of India in 1955.*
- •First Indian owned bank is *Allahabad Bank,* established in *1865.*
- •*Regional Rural Banks (RRB)* was established as a *statutory body* under *RRB Act 1976* to enhance financial activities in rural areas. E.g. Grameen Banks.
- •*Pratham Grameen Bank* was set up on *2nd October 1975* in *Moradabad, Uttar Pradesh* (First one).

*In Regional Rural Banks (RRB)*
- •The *Central Government* holds *50%* ownership in RRBs, making it the largest stakeholder.
- •*Commercial banks* that sponsor RRBs hold *35%* ownership and provide financial and operational support.
- •The respective *State Government* where the RRB operates holds *15%* ownership.
- •They are supervised by *NABARD* and regulated by *RBI.*
- •RRBs were set up on the recommendations of the Narasimham Committee on Rural Credit.
- •The consolidation of RRBs began in 2005, following the recommendations of the Dr. Vyas Committee (2001)
*The Kelkar Committee (1986)* recommended strengthening RRBs by increasing their authorized capital to Rs 5 crore and paid-up capital to Rs 1 crore, directing surplus funds into profitable investments. It also suggested merging weak, uneconomic RRBs.

**Function Of Commercial Banks**
*Primary Functions of Commercial Banks:*
Accepting deposits from the public (saving, current, and fixed deposits) and providing loans and advances such as cash credit, overdraft, and bill discounting.

### [book p.272 / PDF 293]

**Left column**

*Secondary Functions of Commercial Banks:*
Providing agency services (collection of cheques, payment of bills, acting as agent) and general utility services (locker facility, demand drafts, foreign exchange, cards, and online banking).

**Box heading: OTHER FINANCIAL INSTITUTIONS**

**National Bank of Agriculture and Rural Development (NABARD)**
- •NABARD was established on *12 July 1982,* on the recommendation of *B. Sivaraman Committee (1979)* through *NABARD Act 1981.*
- •*Headquarter*: *Mumbai*
*Functions of NABARD*
- •It offers loans and financial assistance to promote *agriculture growth and rural development.*
- •It oversees and regulates the working of *cooperative banks* and *RRBs* to ensure their financial stability.
- •Unlike commercial banks, *NABARD does not provide loans or banking services to individuals* (meaning: It does not deal directly with public).
- •It provides various rural financing schemes through *PMAY (Pradhan Mantri Grameen Awas Yojana), KCC (Kisan Credit Cards) RuPay Kisan Cards.*
[box] In 2015, NABARD had launched initiatives E-Shakti' and 'LEDP (Livelihood and Enterprise Development Program)

**National Housing Bank**
- •It was established in *1988* through → *Act of 1987.*
- • *Headquarter:* *New Delhi*

**Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI)**
- •It was established on *2nd April 1990.*
- •*Headquarter:* *Lucknow*

**Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI)**
- •It was established as statutory body on *12th April 1988* through *SEBI Act of 1992.*
- •*Headquarter:* *Mumbai*
- •*Chairman*: *Tuhin Kanta Pandey*
*Functions of SEBI*
- •It safeguard the interests of investors in the securities market.

**Right column**

**Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority (IRDAI)**
- •It was established as a statutory body on *April 2000* through *IRDAI Act of 1999.*
- • *Headquarter:* *Hyderabad*

**National Agricultural Co-Operative Marketing Federation Of India Limited (NAFED)**
- •It was established on *2 October 1958.*
- •It is an apex organisation of agricultural produce marketing cooperatives.
- • *Headquarter:* *New Delhi*

**Industrial Development Bank of India Limited (IDBI)**
- •It was established by GOI in 1964 as a subsidiary of the RBI. LIC (Life Insurance Corporation of India) later acquired 51% stake in the bank.
- • *Headquarter:* *Mumbai*

**Box heading: MICROFINANCE INSTITUTIONS (MFI)**
- •MFIs provide *micro-credit* and other financial services like loan, savings, insurance, and remittances to *low income population.*
- •MFIs provide a collateral-free microfinance loan to households having income upto *3 lakh/annum.*
- •*Father of Microfinance system: Muhammad Yunus (in 2006, he won Nobel Peace Prize for creating Grameen Bank)*
- •*First Micro Finance institution in India: Self Employed Women Association (SEWA) Bank,* which was established in *Ahmedabad* in *1974.*

**Business Model of Micro Finance Banks**
- •The business model of Microfinance Banks revolves around providing small loans and financial services to low-income individuals, primarily in rural area through various schemes and groups.

**Self Help Groups (SHG)**
- •It is a group of *10-20 people Below Poverty Line (BPL)* who come together to find ways to improve their living conditions in rural areas.
- •SHGs provide *collateral-free loans* to members at *lower interest rates* than the informal sector.
- •SHGs also help *women* and *other marginalized groups* become more self-reliant.

### [book p.273 / PDF 294]

**Left column**

**Joint Liability Groups (JLG)**
- •It comprises a group of *4-10 people* who come together to apply for a bank loan.
- •By forming a JLG, individuals can avail of *collateral-free loans* and expand their businesses without worrying about security or margin.
- •This loan is offered to *individuals, tenant farmers, sharecroppers, and small and marginal farmers.*

**Pradhan Mantri Mudra Yojana (PMMY) Scheme**
- •This is a government scheme that promotes financial inclusion by providing *micro-credit or loans to small businesses.*
*The loans are categorised into*
- •*Shishu:* Covering loans upto ₹ 50,000
- •*Kishor:* Covering loans above ₹ 50,000-₹ 5 lakh.
- •*Tarun:* Covering loans above ₹ 5 lakh-₹ 10 lakh.
- •*Tarun Plus:* Covering loans above ₹ 10 lakh-₹ 20 lakh.

**Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs)**
- •NBFCs are also known as *Non-banking Financial Institutions (NBFIs),* that offer various banking services but do not have a banking license.
- •They are registered under *Companies Act of 1956* and are regulated by RBI under *RBI Act 1934.*
- •They *cannot accept Demand Deposits* (like savings/ current accounts) and *cannot issue cheques.*
- •NBFCs provide *loans against Gold* and these are called *Gold Loans.*
- •*Deposits are not guaranteed in NBFCs.* If an NBFC fails, depositors may lose their money, as there is no government protection.
- •They are *not required* to maintain *CRR* and *SLR* like banks.
(* Explained in the next chapter Pg. 232) (as printed — cross-reference page number "232" does not match this book's Economics pagination, where CRR/SLR are explained on p.274; ⚠ likely a stale reference from another edition)

**Non-Banking Financial Company-Micro Finance Institution (NBFC-MFI)**
- •They are registered under RBI and regulated by RBI.
- •They provide micro loans/micro financial services to *low-income individuals, especially in rural areas,* who lack access to formal banking services.
- •NBFC-MFIs were established based on the recommendations of the *Malegam Committee (2010)* → also looked after the issues of MFIs.

**Right column**

- •As per RBI guidelines, for entities other than NBFC-MFIs, the maximum limit on microfinance loans is *25% of their total assets.*
- •To qualify for NBFC-MFI licence they are required to have *75% of their total assets* in the form of microfinance loans *(earlier this was 85%, however the guidelines were revised in 2022)*

**Capital Requirements for NBFCs**
For *NBFC-MFI (Microfinance Institutions),* the minimum *Net Owned Fund (NOF)* requirement is tiered based on geography:
*General (All over India):* Must have a minimum *NOF of 5 crore.*
*North-East Region:* Requires a lower minimum *NOF of 2 crore* to encourage financial inclusion in that specific area.

**Payment & Settlement Act 2007**
All payment systems in India (like *UPI, NEFT, RTGS,* and *Credit Cards*) are regulated by the RBI under the Payment and Settlement Systems *Act, 2007.*

**Priority Sector Lending (PSL)**
- •The RBI mandates that banks allocate a specific portion of their lending to sectors that impact large segments of the population.
- •*Commercial Banks (Domestic & Foreign ): Must allocate 40% of ANBC (Adjusted Net Bank Credit) to PSL.*
- •Regional Rural Banks (RRBs) & Small Finance Banks (SFBs): Have a higher target of 75% of ANBC.
- •*NBFCs:* Generally, NBFCs do not have specific PSL targets like commercial banks.
*Specific Sub-targets:*
*Agriculture: 18%.*
*Weaker Sections (SC, ST & Women's): 12%.*
- •Non-Achievement: If banks fail to meet these targets, the shortfall is deposited into the *Rural Infrastructure Development Fund (RIDF),* managed by *NABARD.*

**Urban Cooperative Banks (UCBs)**
- •They operate under two regulatory bodies: *RBI & Registrar of Cooperative Societies (RCS).*

[box] • *MUDRA: Micro Units Development & Refinance Agency Ltd.*
• *Launch year: 8 April 2015*
• *It receives 100% funding from SIDBI.*

■■■ (red end marker — end of Ch.6)

## PART A — PARMAR SSC FATMAN (2nd Ed. 2026): Economics Ch.7 "MONETARY POLICY" (book pp.274–275, PDF pp.295–296)

Running header "MONETARY POLICY". Chapter ends with ■■■ at bottom of right column, p.275. Contents (per chapter map): Reserves Maintained by Banks, Monetary Policy Tools, Fiscal Policy.

### [book p.274 / PDF 295]

**Left column — box titled "MONETARY POLICY"**
- •Monetary policy refers to the regulation of the money supply and interest rates by the RBI to maintain price stability, control inflation, and ensure economic growth.
- •RBI uses various monetary policy tools to achieve these objectives, which are categorized into *quantitative* and *qualitative instruments.*
- •For the better understanding of Monetary Policy, we first need to learn about Assets and Liabilities of a Bank.

**Balance Sheet**
- •A bank's balance sheet is a *financial statement that shows its Assets* (what the bank owns) and *Liabilities* (what the bank owes to others, including depositors).
[Diagram "Structure of Balance Sheet": Liabilities → "Deposits (liability for the bank)" → Demand Deposits (• Savings accounts • Current accounts) and Time/Term Deposits: (• Fixed deposits (FDs) • Recurring deposits (RDs)); Assets → "Loans granted by Bank"]

**Net Demand and Time Liability (NDTL)**
- •NDTL refers to the total deposits held by a bank, which determines the money available for lending.
- •It is an important factor in determining reserve requirements set by RBI, are of two types:
*NDTL consist of*
1. *Demand Liabilities:* Deposits that can be withdrawn at any time. (E.g. Savings and current accounts).
2. *Time Liabilities:* Deposits with a fixed tenure (E.g. Fixed deposits and recurring deposits).

**Reserves Maintained by Banks**
- •RBI keeps a part of *NDTL as a reserve.* This is done through two mechanisms: *CRR (Cash Reserve Ratio) and SLR (Statutory Liquidity Ratio).* This is done to ensure that Banks have sufficient vault cash to cater any emergency situation.

| Cash Reserve Ratio | Statutory Liquid Ratio |
|---|---|
| Banks must keep this reserve in cash with the RBI. | Banks must keep this reserve in liquid assets like cash, gold, or approved securities. |
| Banks don't earn interest on money kept as CRR. | Banks earn interest on money kept as SLR. |

**Right column**

**Monetary Policy Committee (MPC)**
- •The Monetary Policy Committee (MPC) was established based on the *Urjit Patel Committee's recommendations.*
*A total of 6 members were suggested:*
- •3 members from RBI, that includes *RBI Governor, Deputy Governor,* and an *Executive.* The RBI Governor is the chairman.
- •3 members from the Government of India.
*The MPC meets bimonthly to decide monetary policy actions, such as changes in repo rate and CRR.*

**Box heading: MONETARY POLICY TOOLS**

**Monetary Policy Tools used by RBI**
**Quantitative Tools**
- •These tools influence the overall money supply and credit in the economy.
1. *Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR)*
2. *Statutory Liquidity Ratio (SLR)*
3. *Bank Rate (BR)*
- •It is an interest rate at which *RBI provides loan to commercial banks for a long-term without keeping any securities/collateral.*
- •Higher bank rates make borrowing costlier, reducing money supply and lower bank rates make loans cheaper, increasing liquidity.
4. *Open Market Operations (OMO)*
- •It is also called as *sterilization.*
1. *Outright Purchase:* RBI directly buys *government securities* without any kind of repurchase agreement.
2. *Repurchase Obligation (Rere):* When *banks borrow money from RBI,* using government securities as collateral and with a promise of repurchasing them later, it is called as *Repo.* Similarly, when RBI invites bank to deposit their extra funds in RBI in exchange of government securities, it is called as *Reverse Repo.*
*"Repo and SDF Special Depository Facility are collectively known as Liquidity Adjustment Facility (LAF)*
*Special Depository Facility (SDF)* is a facility by which banks deposit excess money with the Reserve Bank of India without giving any collateral.
[box] **Collateral (गिरवी)** — Collateral is something valuable that a borrower gives as a guarantee for a loan. If the borrower fails to repay, the lender can take the collateral to recover the money.

### [book p.275 / PDF 296]

**Left column**

5. *Marginal Standing Facility (MSF)*
- •MSF is a way for banks to borrow money from the RBI in emergencies, when they don't have enough funds. It allows banks to get *"overnight loans" but at a higher interest rate than the repo rate.*
- •If the bank fails to maintain the required *CRR* and *SLR,* it must pay a penalty of *3% above the Bank Rate for each day of default.*
- •Under MSF, banks can use *G-Secs within their SLR quota to borrow funds from RBI. Only scheduled commercial banks can access MSF.*
[box] Minimum rate below which banks are not allowed to lend money is called as Base Rate.
- •If banks are unable to maintain the required CRR and SLR, they may borrow funds from the other banks in the *Interbank Lending Market,* This borrowing occurs at *Call Money Rate* (it is decided on basis of *RRR* and *MSF*), which is the interest rate charged for *short-term, overnight loans* between banks and other financial institutions.
- •Limit: 2% of NDTL.

**MCLR (Marginal Cost of Funds Based Lending Rate):**
- •MCLR is the minimum interest rate below which banks cannot lend, It was introduced by the *Reserve Bank of India* in April 2016 to make loan interest rates more transparent and responsive to policy rate changes.

**Money Multiplier (MM)**
- •The Money Multiplier (MM) is the highest possible extent to which the money supply can increase due to changes in bank deposits.
[box] Money Multiplier = 1 / CRR
[box] NBFC and NBFC-MFI need not to maintain CRR and SLR.
- •When *CRR increases,* banks keep more money as reserves. They have less money to lend. Fewer loans mean less money creation. This *reduces the money multiplier and vice versa.*

**Monetary Policy and Inflation/Deflation Control**
| Situation | RBI Action | Effects | Policy |
|---|---|---|---|
| During inflation | Increase in CRR, SLR, BR, RRR, RR | Decrease in money supply | Contractionary/ Tight/Dear/Hawkish Monetary Policy |
| During deflation | Decrease in CRR, SLR, BR, RRR, RR | Increase in money supply | Expansionary/Easy/ Dovish Monetary Policy |

**Right column**

**Qualitative Monetary Policy Tools**
- •These are selective tools used by RBI to control the use and direction of credit in specific sectors of the economy.
- •*The central bank limits the amount of loans and advances that commercial banks can give.*

1. *Changing Margin Requirements*
- •The RBI *increases the margin (the amount borrowers must pay upfront) for non-essential sectors to limit excessive borrowing* and reducing it for important sectors like *agriculture* to encourage lending.
2. *Consumer Credit Regulation*
- •RBI *sets rules on loan installments, down payments, and repayment periods in advance.* This helps manage inflation by controlling how much people can borrow.
3. *Moral Suasion*
- •It is a technique used by RBI to influence economic activity through *persuasion* and *implicit threats.* RBI advises or persuades banks to follow its monetary policy.
4. *Credit rationing*
- •Credit rationing is when RBI *limits the total amount of loans* and *credit banks can issue to certain sectors.* It fixes the credit supply for each bank, preventing excessive lending. It also *sets credit limits for different sectors to ensure balanced lending.*

**Box heading: FISCAL POLICY**
- •Fiscal policy is the government's strategy to manage the economy by adjusting taxes, government spending, and borrowing. *It is used to control inflation or deflation, unemployment, and economic growth.*
*When the government wants to control inflation, it can take the following steps*
- •Increased Taxes: *Higher taxes reduce people's disposable income,* leading to lower spending and demand.
- •Lower Subsidies: *Reducing subsidies makes goods and services more expensive,* discouraging excessive consumption.
- •Lower MSP (Minimum Support Price): A lower MSP *discourages excessive crop production* and *controls the rise in food prices* by keeping supply in check.
[box] • *MSP stands for Minimum Support Price, a price set by the government for certain agricultural products.*
• *Monetary Policy is used by RBI and Fiscal Policy is used by Government.*

■■■ (red end marker — end of Ch.7)

### Part A doubts (Ch.7) ⚠

- ⚠ **Bank Rate definition** as printed: "an interest rate at which RBI provides loan to commercial banks for a long-term without keeping any securities/collateral" — as printed.
- ⚠ **Collateral / SDF:** "Repo and SDF Special Depository Facility are collectively known as Liquidity Adjustment Facility (LAF)" — as printed ("Special Depository Facility" is Parmar's expansion of SDF; the RBI's own expansion is "Standing Deposit Facility" — flagged, not corrected).
- ⚠ **OMO/"Rere":** Parmar labels the repo/reverse-repo item "Repurchase Obligation (Rere)" under Open Market Operations — as printed.
- ⚠ **MSF penalty:** "penalty of 3% above the Bank Rate for each day of default" (for failing to maintain CRR/SLR) — as printed; separate from the MSF facility itself.
- ⚠ **Call Money Rate** "decided on basis of RRR and MSF" and "Limit: 2% of NDTL" — as printed.
- ⚠ **MCLR "introduced by RBI in April 2016"** — as printed.
- ⚠ **"Base Rate"** box on p.275 (Minimum rate below which banks are not allowed to lend) sits beside the MCLR text; both are described as "minimum lending rate" — as printed.
- ⚠ **Money multiplier = 1/CRR** — as printed (a simplified formula; the reserve-ratio form usually includes other leakages).

## PART A — PARMAR SSC FATMAN (2nd Ed. 2026): Economics Ch.8 "BANKING: PART 2" (book pp.276–279, PDF pp.297–300)

Running header "BANKING: PART 2". Chapter ends with ■■■ in the left column, upper part of p.279 (rest of page blank). Contents (per chapter map): Liquidity, Money Aggregate, Banking Regulations.

### [book p.276 / PDF 297]

**Left column — box titled "LIQUIDITY"**
- •Liquidity means how easily an asset can be converted into cash without losing its value. Money has the highest liquidity because it can be used immediately for transactions.
*Liquidity Order*
Cash > Gold > Cheque > Bond
[box] Demand Deposit is more liquid than Term Deposit

Money was invented to solve the issue of barter system .
*Limitations of Barter system: Double Coincidence of Wants, Lack of Divisibility, Lack of Store of Value.*

**Function of money are classified into 2 categories**
- •*Primary : Medium of Exchange,* It can be used for buying and selling goods and services.
- •*Secondary:Medium of value (Unit Of Account),* It helps to measure the value of goods and services.
  *a) Standard of Deferred Payment*
  *b) Store of Value: Valuable in Nature,* It retains value over time, unlike perishable goods (Money has no intrinsic value, unlike commodities such as gold). Modern paper has value because of government backing.
  *c) Highly Portable and Durable:* Money is easy to carry around and does not degrade over time.

*Liquidity Preference theory (Keynesian theory of demand for money)*
It states that interest rates are determined by the demand and supply of money, rather than just savings and investment.

*The Cambridge Cash Balance Approach*
It was developed by *Marshall, Pigou, and Keynes*
It states that the value of money is determined by the demand to hold cash balances (as a store of value) rather than just transaction velocity.

**There are Three Reasons Why People Hold Money**
*Transaction Motive*
- •This is the most basic reason for holding money. Money is held to facilitate smooth spending on goods and services. *E.g. people keep cash on hand to pay for regular purchases like groceries, transportation, or bills etc.*
*Precautionary Motive:*
- •Precautionary motive refers to holding money to meet unexpected or emergency expenses. *such as illness, accidents, or sudden financial needs.*

**Right column**

*Speculative Motive (by J M Keynes)*
- •People hold money to take advantage of investment opportunities or avoid potential losses. *E.g. buying stocks when the market dips, by being ready to invest when prices are considered favorable.*

**The paradox of thrift**
It is a *Keynesian economic theory* stating that an individual increase in savings (thrift) during an economic downturn can, collectively reduce aggregate demand and total savings in the economy. As households save more, reduced consumption lowers business revenues, leading to decreased income and ultimately less total savings.

**Speculative Demand for Money**
- •Speculative demand for money is the *desire to hold cash or other liquid assets* for investment purposes, rather than for daily needs.
[box] Speculative Demand for Money ∝ 1 / Rate of Interest
- •Speculative demand for money and interest rates share an inverse relationship. As interest rates rise, speculative demand for money falls, and vice versa.
- •*When interest rates rise → People prefer to invest in bonds and financial assets instead of holding cash → Speculative demand for money decreases.*
- •*When interest rates fall → Returns on investments are low, so people hold more cash instead of investing → Speculative demand for money increases.*
[Graph 1: Speculative Demand Curve — y-axis r (with r_max and r_min marked), x-axis Speculative Demand; downward-sloping curve; formula-like label "Ms = r_max ÷ r_min" partly legible; legend: "r_max = maximum interest rate", "r_min = minimum interest rate", "Ms = Speculative Demand for money"]
[box] *"Interest rates are an opportunity cost or 'price' of holding money balance in an economy."*

**Liquidity Trap**
- •A liquidity trap is an economic situation *where interest rates are low and people prefer to hold cash instead of spending or investing,* which can lead to any Monetary Policy ineffective.

### [book p.277 / PDF 298]

**Left column**

[Graph 2: Liquidity Trap — y-axis Rate of Interest (3, 6, 9, 12, 15), x-axis Speculative Demand for Money; curve L falls then flattens horizontally at low rate labelled "Liquidity trap"]
• The *horizontal portion* of the liquidity preference curve is referred to as the liquidity trap.
• In this portion of the curve, the demand for money is *infinitely elastic* and the liquidity preference curve becomes *perfectly elastic.*

**Types of Money in our Economy**
*1. Currency in Circulation*
- •*Currency with Banks:* Money held by banks for daily operations.
- •*Currency with Public:* Money available for spending by people.
[box] CDR = Money held by public in currency / Money held by public in deposits
[meme-style question box, with cartoon student]: *During a festival, does the Currency Deposit Ratio (CDR) Increases or decreases?*

*2. Deposits with Banks*
Banks hold two types of deposits from customers:
- •*Demand Deposits:* Deposits that are withdrawable anytime *(Ex: Savings and Current Accounts)*
- •*Term Deposits:* They are fixed deposits for a set period *(Ex: Fixed Deposits, Recurring Deposits).*

*3. Deposits with RBI*
- •*Bank Deposits with RBI:* It includes the CRR, which is the mandatory percentage of a bank's *Net Demand and Time Liabilities (NDTL) that must be kept with the RBI.* This helps in controlling inflation and liquidity in the economy.
- •*Other Deposits with RBI:* It includes deposits from government institutions, foreign central banks, international financial organisations and some private entities.

*4. Postal Deposits*
They are similar to bank deposits (Term Deposits and Demand Deposits), but managed by the *Post Office Savings Bank (POSB).*

**Right column**

**Box heading: MONEY AGGREGATES**
- •Monetary aggregates *(or Money Supply)* refers to the amount of money in circulation in the economy at a given time. The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) measures it using different monetary aggregates *(M0, M1, M2, M3, M4).*

**Types of Money Supply**
| Money Aggregates | Components |
|---|---|
| M0 | Currency in circulation + Banker's deposit with RBI |
| M1 | Currency with public + Other deposits with RBI + Demand Deposits |
| M2 | M1+ Saving deposits with Post Office savings bank |
| M3 | M1+Net time deposits of commercial banks |
| M4 | M3+Total deposits with Post Office |

**Points To Note**
1. *M0* is called as *High Powered Money/Base Money/ Reserve Money. It is the total Amount of Money printed by RBI.*
2. *M1* is called as *Narrow Money.*
3. *M3* is called as *Broad Money* and *Aggregate Monetary Resource.*

**Liquidity order** (sub-heading as printed)
- •The rate at which banks lend to each other to meet *short-term needs (such as fulfilling CRR requirement by RBI)* is called the Interbank Offer Rate, also known as the *Offer Rate.*

**London Interbank Offer Rate (LIBOR)**
- •*LIBOR* served as a base rate for loans and financial products worldwide, including loans, mortgages, etc.
- •It was no longer published after *31st December 2021* and was officially replaced with *SOFR (Secured Overnight Financing Rate)* for the *US dollar.*
- •The *MIBOR (Mumbai Interbank Offer Rate)* was the Indian version of LIBOR. MIBOR was replaced by the *FBIL-Overnight MIBOR* in *2015.*
[box] *"Asia's oldest stock exchange is "Bombay Stock Exchange" established in 1875."*

### [book p.278 / PDF 299]

**Left column**

**Money Market** *(Short-term Loans for Less than 1 year)*
- •*Call Money:* Loans are to be repaid within *1 day.*
- •*Notice Money:* Loans are to be repaid in *2-14 days.*

**Capital Market** *(Long-Term Loans for More than 1 year)*
- •They are used for long-term investments, including stocks and bonds.

**Treasury Bills** *(Issued by RBI)*
- • It is also known as *Zero Coupon Securities.*
- •They are short-term debt instruments issued by the *GOI through the Reserve Bank of India (RBI).*
- •*T-Bills are issued in three tenure: 91 days, 182 days, and 364 days*
- •T-Bills are categorised as money market instruments because they have a *maturity of less than one year.*
- •They are always listed at a discount, *no interest is paid.*

**Assets**
- •Assets are resources owned by an individual or entity that hold economic value (reserve loan).
*Types of Assets.*
1. *Regular Assets:* Regular Assets are financial assets that generate consistent returns over the long term. They typically include loans where both interest and principal are repaid on time.
2. *Stressed Assets:* Stressed assets are loans that are not performing, or are in danger of not performing (when principal + interest rate is *overdue for less than 90 days).*
3. *Non-performing Assets (NPAs):* It is a loan or advance where the interest or principal payment is *overdue for more than 90 days.*
   *Classified as:*
   - •*Substandard Assets:* Loans that are overdue for more than *90 days but less than 12 months.*
   - •*Doubtful Assets:* Loans that are overdue for *more than 12 months.*
   - •*Loss Assets:* Loans that are considered uncollectible and have *no chance of recovery.*

**Wilful Defaulter**
A borrower who has the capacity to repay loans but deliberately chooses not to pay.

**Bad Banks**
- •A bad bank is a financial entity which specialises in *handling bad loans or NPAs.* It buys the NPAs of commercial banks and tries to recover at least a part of the value of such NPAs.

**Right column**

*2021 Budget announced two bad banks to help resolve NPAs:*
- *National Asset Reconstruction Company Limited (NARCL)*
- *India Debt Resolution Company Limited (IDRCL)*

**Banking Regulations and Security Laws**
*SARFAESI Act, 2002*
- •*Full Form: Securitisation and Reconstruction of Financial Assets and Enforcement of Securities Interest Act.*
- •*Purpose:* It allows banks to seize properties of borrowers who fail to repay loans.

**Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (IBC), 2016**
- •*Objective:* To resolve the *Twin Balance Sheet Problem* (where banks are stressed with bad loans and corporates are stressed with debt).
- •It helps in the quick resolution of *financial insolvency,* ensuring that defaulting companies repay lenders in a time-bound manner.
- •It initiates the *Corporate Insolvency Resolution Process (CIRP)* for resolving insolvency of companies.
- •Under *IBC, 2016,* a moratorium period *(calm period)* of *180 days* is granted when a company undergoes insolvency proceedings *(can be extended to 270 days in certain cases).*

*There are two governing bodies*
*National Company Law Tribunal (NCLT):* Handles the initial insolvency cases.
*National Company Law Appellate Tribunal (NCLAT):* Handles appeals against NCLT decisions.

**Box heading: INTERNATIONAL BANKING REGULATIONS**

**Basel Norms**
- •The Basel Norms, also known as the Basel Accords, are a set of international banking regulations that aim to strengthen the banking system and manage risk.
- •*Established by:* Central Bank Governors (G-10 countries) in *1974.*
- •*Headquarter:* BIS (Bureau of International Settlement) office in Basel, Switzerland.
- •The *Basel Committee on Bank Supervision (BCBS)* has issued three sets of regulations which are known as *Basel-I, II, and III.*

**Basel-1**
- •*Introduced in: 1988*
- •It focused almost entirely on credit risk.
(continues p.279)

### [book p.279 / PDF 300]

**Left column**
- •The minimum capital requirement was fixed at *8% of risk weighted assets (RWA).*
- •India adopted Basel-I guidelines In *1999.*

**Basel-II**
- •*Introduced in:* 2004
Capital Adequacy Ratio *(CAR)* Also known as *CRAR* (Capital to Risk (Weighted) Assets Ratio). *It is the ratio of a bank's capital to its risk.*
[formula] CAR = (Tier1 Capital + Tier2 Capital) / Risk Weighted Assets × 100

**Basel-III**
- •*Introduced in:* 2010
- •A bank needs to maintain the *capital adequacy ratio (CAR)* is to be maintained at *10.5%.* The minimum *Tier 1 (Core)* capital ratio and the minimum *Tier 2 (Supplementary)* capital ratio have to be maintained at 6% and 2% of risk-weighted assets respectively and The Capital Conservation Buffer (2.5%).
- •India's RBI-mandated CAR is *11.5%*

Section starts in the left column of p.267 (below §8's terminology list), runs through pp.268–269, and ends at the end of the "Household Consumption Expenditure Survey 2023-24" block at the top of the left column of p.270; §10 "Indian Fiscal System" follows.

**Cross-book note:** Parmar Ch.6 (Banking part 1: RBI, NABARD/SEBI/IRDAI, UCBs, NBFCs), Ch.7 (Monetary policy) and Ch.8 (Banking part 2: liquidity, NPAs, Basel) are in `partA_ch06…`, `partA_ch07…`, `partA_ch08…`. Lucent's regulators table (with chairpersons as of 29.05.2025) is time-sensitive.

### [Lucent book p.267 / PDF 279 — left column (from "9.") and right column]

**Left column**

**9. Indian Financial System**
- ➤ Indian Financial System is a system in which People, Financial Institutions, Banks, Industrial Companies and the Government demand for fund and the same is supplied to them.
- ➤ There are two parts of Indian Financial System–first demand side and second supply side. The representative of demand side can be Individual investor, Industrial and Business Companies, Government etc and the representative of supply side will be Banks, Insurance Companies, Mutual Fund and other Financial Institutions.
- ➤ The Indian financial system, which refers to the borrowing and lending of funds or to the demand for and supply of funds of all individuals, institutions, companies and of the Government consists of two parts, viz., the Indian money market and the Indian capital market.
- ➤ The Indian *money market* is the market in which short-term funds are borrowed and lent. The capital market in India, on the other hand, is the market for medium term and long-term funds.
- ➤ The Indian financial system performs a crucial role in economic development of India through saving-investment process, also known as capital formation.

**Right column**
- ➤ The financial system is commonly, classified into: 1. Industrial finance, 2. Agricultural finance, 3. Development finance and 4. Government finance.
- ➤ *Devaluation* means lowering the official value of the local money in terms of foreign currency or gold.
- ➤ *Balance of Payments* (BoP) is a systematic record of all the economic transactions between one country and the rest of the world in a given period.
- ➤ BoP is divided in current account and capital account.
- ➤ *Balance of Trade* (BoT) is the difference between the value of goods exported and the value of goods imported per annum. Services not included in BoT.
- ➤ EXIM Policy 2000-01 introduced Special Economic Zones (SEZ) Scheme.
- ➤ 1994-95, Indian Rupee was made fully convertible on current account.
- ➤ Fiscal Policy is the policy relating to public revenue and public expenditure and allied matters.
- ➤ Usually, the Indian money market is classified into organised sector and the unorganised sector.
- ➤ The unorganised sector consists of indigenous bankers including the Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs). Besides, these two, there are many sub-markets in the Indian money market.
- ➤ The highest financial institution in organized sector is Reserve Bank of India and in addition to this Banks of Public Sector, Banks of Private Sector, Foreign Banks and other financial institutions are also part of organized sector.

**Reserve Bank of India**
- ➤ The *Reserve Bank of India* regulates and controls the money of the country.
- ➤ The RBI was established under the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934 on 1st April, 1935 with a capital of Rs. 5 crore. It was nationalised on 1st January, 1949; on the recommendation of Parliamentary Committee in 1948. It is the Central Bank of India.
- ➤ The Reserve Bank of India is the supreme monetary and banking authority in the country and has the responsibility to control the banking system in the country. It keeps the reserves of all commercial banks and hence is known as the 'Reserve Bank'. Its financial year is 1st July to 30th June.

**The Indian Capital Market**
- ➤ The Indian capital market is the market for long-term capital (or loans); it refers to all the facilities and institutional arrangements for borrowing and lending 'term funds' — medium term and long term funds.
- ➤ The Capital Market in India includes : 1. Government Securities *(Gilt-edged market)* 2. Industrial Securities Market 3. Development financial institutions like IFCI, IDBI, ICICI, SFCs, IIBI, UTI etc 4. Financial Intermediaries like Merchant banks.
- ➤ Individuals who invest directly on their own in securities are also supplier of fund to capital market. The trend in the capital market is basically affected by two important factors : 1. operations of the institutional investors in the market and 2. the excellent results flowing in from the corporate sector.

**Left column**

*[Singapore International Monetary Exchange (old name)]*

- ➤ The capital market in India can be classified into :
  - ★ Gilt-edged market or market for Government and semi-government securities;
  - ★ Industrial securities market;
  - ★ Development financial institutions and
  - ★ Non-banking financial companies.
- ➤ *The gilt-edged securities market* is the market for Government and Semi government securities which carry fixed interest rates.
- ➤ *The industrial securities market* is the market for equities and debentures of companies of the corporate sector. This market is further classified into—
  (a) new issue markets for raising fresh capital in the form of shares and debentures *(commonly referred to as primary market),* and
  (b) old issues market *(or secondary market)* for buying or selling shares and debentures of existing companies— this market is commonly referred to as the stock market or stock exchange.
- ➤ If shares or debentures of private corporations, primary sureties of government companies or new sureties and issue of bonds of public sector are sold or purchased in the capital market, then the market is called *Primary Capital Market.*
- ➤ *Secondary Market* includes transactions in the stock exchange and gilt-edged market.
- ➤ Merchant Bank, Mutual Fund, Leasing Companies, Risk Capital Companies etc. collect and invest public money into the capital market.

**Right column**
- ➤ Unit Trust of India (UTI) is the biggest Mutual Fund Institution of India.

**Stock Exchange**
- ➤ The stock exchange is the market for buying and selling of stocks, shares, securities, bonds and debentures etc. It increases the market ability of existing securities by providing simple method for public and others to buy and sell securities.
- ➤ The first organised stock exchange in India was started in Bombay *(now Mumbai),* when the 'Native Share Brokers' Association' known as the Bombay Stock Exchange (BSE) was formed by the brokers in 1875. BSE is Asia's oldest stock exchange.

**Credit Rating Agency in India & World** [pink box] *(Registered intermediaries as on 13 July, 2023)*
Indian credit rating industry mainly comprises of CRISIL, ICRA, CARE, ONICRA, FITCH *(India Ratings & Research)* & SMERA.
- CRISIL : Credit Rating Information Services of India Limited, Headquarter— Mumbai
- ICRA : Investment information and Credit Rating Agency, Headquarter—New Delhi, India
- CARE : Credit Analysis and Research Ltd, Headquarter—Mumbai
- ACUITE Ratings & Reseach Limited—HQ : Mumbai
- BRICKWORK Ratings India Private Limted—HQ : Bangalore
- INFOMERICS Valuation and Rating Pvt. Ltd—HQ : New Delhi
- India Ratings & Research Pvt. Ltd. formerly (FITCH) : Headquarter—Mumbai
- SMERA Ratings Limited, Headquarter—Mumbai
- Standard & Poor's (S&P) : Headquarter—New York, US
- MOODY'S : Headquarter—New York, US
- Fitch : Headquarter—New York, US
*Note : CRISIL is the largest credit rating agency in India, with a market share of greater than 60%*   *[Source : SEBI]*

- ➤ In 1894, the Ahmedabad Stock Exchange was started to facilitate dealings in the shares of textile mills there.
- ➤ The Calcutta Stock Exchange was started in 1908 to provide a market for shares of plantations and jute mills.
- ➤ The number of stock exchanges rose from 7 in 1939 to 21 in 1945.
- ➤ Under the securities contract *(Regulation)* Act of 1956, the Government of India has so far recognised 23 stock exchanges. Bombay is the premier exchange in the country.
- ➤ With the setting up of National Stock Exchange, all regional stock exchanges have lost relevance.
- ➤ The BSE transformed itself into a corporate entity from being a brokers association, from the middle of August, 2005.

**Regulators in India (As on 29.05.2025)** [pink table, spans bottom of p.268 and top of p.269]
| S. | Regulator | Sector | Headquarter | Chairperson |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1. | RBI – Reserve Bank of India | Banking & Finance, Monetary Policy | Mumbai | Sanjay Malhotra (Governor) |

**Left column**
- ➤ As a public limited company, BSE *(Bombay Stock Exchange)* is obliged to dilute stock brokers stake to 49%.
- ➤ To prevent excessive speculation and volatility in the stock market SEBI has introduced rolling settlements from July 2, 2001, under which settlement has to be made everyday.

**Some Important Share Price Index of India**
- ➤ *BSE SENSEX :* This is the most sensitive share index of the Mumbai Stock Exchange. This is the representative index of 30 main shares. Its base year is 1978-79. BSE is the oldest stock exchange of India, founded in 1875.
- ➤ *BSE 200 :* This represents 200 shares of Mumbai Stock Exchange. Its base year is 1989-90.
- ➤ *DOLLEX :* Index of 200 BSE Dollar Value Index is called DOLLEX. Its base year is 1989-90.
- ➤ *NSE-50 :* From 28th July, 1998, its name is S and P CNX Nifty. National Stock Exchange has launched a new share Price Index, NSE-50 in place of NSE-100 in April 1996. NSE-50 includes 50 companies shares. This stock exchange was founded on Ferwani Committee's recommendation in 1994.
- ➤ *CRISIL :* set up in 1988, is a credit rating agency. It undertakes the rating fixed deposit programmes, convertible and non-convertible debentures and also credit assessment of companies.
- ➤ *CRISIL 500* is the new share Price Index introduced by Credit Rating Agency the 'Credit Rating Information Services of India Limited' (CRISIL) on January 18, 1996.
- ➤ The National Stock Exchange (NSE) has launched a new version of its online trading software called 'National Exchange for Automatic Trading' (NEAT).

**Ranking of India in Different Indexes (As on 25.05. 2025)**
1. In Edelman Trust Barometer-2025, China ranked 1st, Indonesia 2nd and India third.
2. Transparency International India (TII)–Global Corruption Perception Index–2024 India ranked 96th among 180 countries *(Denmark Topped, South Sudan last).*
3. World Economic Forum's Gender Gap Index 2024–India 129th among 146 countries *(Iceland top, Sudan last).*

**Right column**
4. Global Hunger Index Report 2024–India ranked 105th among 127 countries. *Somalia ranked 127th, followed by Yemen 126th).*
5. Human Development Index 2023-2024–India ranked 134th in the list of 193 countries *(Switzerland top, Somalia last).*
6. World Press Freedom Index 2025–India ranked 151st in the list of 180 countries *(Norway top, Eritrea last).*
7. Global Peace Index 2024–India ranked 116th out of 163 countries *(Iceland top, Yemen is the least peaceful place).*
8. Global Innovation Index 2024–India ranked 39th, out of 133 countries *(Switzerland top and Angola last).*
9. World Happiness Report 2025–India ranked 118th out of 147 countries *(Finland top and Afghanistan last).*

**Household Consumption Expenditure Survey 2023-24** (Based on 75th National Sample Survey, Aug 2023–July, 2024)

**Rising Consumption**
- ➤ Rural India: Average Monthly Per Capita Expenditure (MPCE) rose to ₹ 4,122 (up 9.3% from ₹ 3,773 in 2022-23).
- ➤ Urban India: Average MPCE increased to ₹6,996 (up 8.3% from ₹6,459 in 2022-23). With imputed values for free benefits, MPCE is ₹4,247 (rural) and ₹7,078 (urban).

**Urban-Rural Gap**
- ➤ The consumption gap narrowed to 69.7% in 2023-24, down from 71.2% in 2022-23 and 83.9% in 2011-12.

**Spending Patterns**
- ➤ *Rural :* 47.04% of MPCE spent on food; highest on beverages and processed food (11.09%), milk products (8.44%), and vegetables (6.03%).
- ➤ *Urban :* 39.68% of MPCE spent on food; higher share of non-food items, especially conveyance and clothing.
- ➤ Non-food items dominate: 53% of rural and 60% of urban expenditure.

**Regional Trends**
- ➤ Highest MPCE: Sikkim (rural ₹ 9,377; urban ₹13,927).
- ➤ Lowest MPCE: Chhattisgarh (rural ₹ 2,739; urban ₹ 4,927).

### [Lucent book p.270 / PDF 282 — top of left column]
- ➤ States with above-average spending: Maharashtra, Punjab, Tamil Nadu, Telangana, Kerala.
- ➤ States with below-average spending: West Bengal, Bihar, Uttar Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh.

**State-wise Growth**
- ➤ Odisha saw the highest increase in rural MPCE (14%), Punjab in urban MPCE (13%).

In summary:
- ➤ The HCES 2023-24 shows robust growth in household consumption, a narrowing urban-rural gap, and declining inequality, reflecting positive economic trends across India.   *[Source : MoSPI]*

## PART B — LUCENT'S GK (2026), Ch.06 "Indian Economy", §11 "Money, Banking and Insurance in India" (Lucent book pp.274–280)

Page mapping: unnumbered ad page at PDF 285 shifts the offset: book p.274 = PDF 287, p.275 = PDF 288, p.276 = PDF 289, p.277 = PDF 290, p.278 = PDF 291, p.279 = PDF 292, p.280 = PDF 293 (PDF = book + 13). Section starts in the right column of p.274 and ends before "12. Tax System" in the right column of p.280.

**Cross-book note:** Overlaps with Parmar Ch.6 (banking part 1: RBI, RRBs, NABARD/SEBI/IRDAI, MFIs, NBFCs, PSL, UCBs), Ch.7 (repo/reverse repo/CRR/SLR/MSF/LAF/bank rate/MCLR) and Ch.8 (NPAs, SARFAESI, IBC, Basel, T-bills, M0–M4) — see Part C. Rate values below (Dec 2023) are time-sensitive.

### [Lucent book p.274 / PDF 287 — right column, from "11."]

**11. Money, Banking and Insurance in India**
- ➤ The Reserve Bank of India was established on 1st April, 1935 and it was nationalised on 1st January, 1949.
- ➤ The Finance Ministry issues Currency Notes and Coins of rupee one, all other Currency Notes are issued by the Reserve Bank of India.
- ➤ The first bank of limited liability managed by Indians was Oudh Commercial Bank founded in 1881. Subsequently, Punjab National Bank was established in 1894.
- ➤ Swadeshi movement, which began in 1906, encouraged the formation of a number of commercial banks.
- ➤ The Banking Companies Act was passed in February, 1949, which was subsequently amended to read as Banking Regulation Act, 1949.

**Monetary Policy Transmission**
- ➤ RBI has reduced repo rate by 250 bps since February 2019 (the current easing cycle). The transmission of policy repo rate changes has been weak on quantity of credit. However, there has been improved transmission on rate structure and term structure.

**Fifth Monetary Policy of FY 2023-24** [pink table] *Key Rates and Ratios (As on December 8, 2023)*
| Rate/Ratio | In % |
|---|---|
| Repo Rate | 6.50 |
| Standing Deposit Facility | 6.25 |
| Marginal Standing Facility Rate | 6.75 |
| Bank Rate | 6.75 |
| Reverse Repo Rate | 3.35 |
| Cash Reserve Ratio | 4.50 |
| Statutory Liquidity Ratio | 18.0 |

**How Monetary Policy Works**
- ➤ Monetary Policy, the process by which the central bank manages money supply in the economy, serves many objectives such as price stability and stable economic growth.
- ➤ Monetary policy is the process by which a central bank (Reserve Bank of India or RBI) manages money supply in the economy. [continues p.275]

### [Lucent book p.275 / PDF 288]

**Left column**
- ➤ The objectives of monetary policy include ensuring inflation targeting and price stability, full employment and stable economic growth.
- ➤ The money supply can be directly affected through reserve ratios or open market operations and can be indirectly affected by using key interest rates to influence the cost of credit.
- ➤ An easy or expansionary monetary policy is implemented by reducing statutory bank reserves of lowering key interest rates and improving market liquidity to encourage economic activity.
- ➤ A contractionary or tight monetary policy reduces liquidity and increases interest rates which has a negative impact on both production and consumption and therefore, economic growth.   *[Source : ET]*

**Banking in India**

**Banking Ecosystem**
- ➤ The banking sector in India, evolved over several decades is well established and has been serving the credit and banking needs of the economy. This ecosystem is providing impetus to economic growth and development of the country, and catering to the specific and varied financial requirements of different customers and borrowers.
- ➤ Presently, commercial banks including 137 scheduled commercial banks, viz., Public Sector Banks (12), Private Sector Banks (21), Small Finance Banks (11), Payment Banks (6), Regional Rural Banks (43) and Foreign Banks (44), are providing banking services in India. Co-operative banks and local area banks are also providing banking services in various segments in different locations of the country.   *[Source : BHARAT 2025]*

**Banks in India are Classified into four Categories—**
1. Commercial Banks (Comprises Commercial Banks, Public Sector Bank, Private Sector Bank, Regional Rural Bank and Foreign Bank)
2. Small Finance Banks
3. Payments Banks
4. Co-operative Banks (Comprises Urban Co-operative Bank and Rural Co-operative Bank)

**Right column**

**Public Sector Banks**
- ➤ Public Sector Banks (PSBs) are a major type of bank in India, where a majority stake (i.e. more than 50%) is held by the government.
- ➤ In April 2019, Vijaya Bank and Dena Bank were merged with Bank of Baroda.
- ➤ On 30 August, 2019, Union Finance Minister Nirmala Sitaraman announced merger of six public sector banks (PSBs) with four better performing anchor banks in order to streamline their operation and size, two banks were amalgamated to strengthen national presence and four were amalgamated to strengthen regional focuses. Subsequently, the number of public sector banks has been reduced to 12 from 27 [⚠ partly faded in scan].

**MUDRA Bank** [logo]
MUDRA stands for Micro Units Development Refinance Agency.
MUDRA Bank has been set up through a statutory enactment. It is responsible for development and refinancing through Pradhan Mantri MUDRA Yojana (PMMY), launched on 8th April, 2015.
This bank will nurture small businesses through different stages of growth and development of businesses termed as Shishu *(when the business is just starting. Loan cover in this stage will be upto ₹ 50,000),* Kishor *(Loan cover ranging from ₹ 50,000 to ₹ 5 lakh)* and Tarun *(Loan cover upto 10 lakh).*
The MUDRA Bank was proposed in Budget 2015–16 with an initial corpus of ₹ 20,000 crore and a credit guarantee fund of ₹ 3,000 crore.
By August, 2023 more than 43.09 crore loans amounting to ₹24.99 lakh crore have been sanctioned since launch of the Scheme. About 68% loans of the total loans have been sanctioned to Women Entrepreneurs and 51% loans have been sanctioned to SC/ST/OBC categories of borrowers.
